# Daily Summary: 2025-06-27

### OctoThinker: Mid-training Incentivizes Reinforcement Learning Scaling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20512v1)
- **Authors**: Zengzhi Wang, Fan Zhou, Xuefeng Li, Pengfei Liu
- **Abstract**: Different base language model families, such as Llama and Qwen, exhibit divergent behaviors during post-training with reinforcement learning (RL), especially on reasoning-intensive tasks. What makes a base language model suitable for reinforcement learning? Gaining deeper insight into this question is essential for developing RL-scalable foundation models of the next generation. In this work, we investigate how mid-training strategies shape RL dynamics, focusing on two representative model families: Qwen and Llama. Our study reveals that (1) high-quality mathematical corpora, such as MegaMath-Web-Pro, significantly improve both base model and RL performance, while existing alternatives (e.g., FineMath-4plus) fail to do so; (2) further adding QA-style data, particularly long chain-of-thought (CoT) reasoning examples, enhances RL outcomes, and instruction data further unlocks this effect; (3) while long-CoT improves reasoning depth, it can also induce verbosity of model responses and unstability of RL training, underscoring the importance of data formatting; (4) scaling mid-training consistently leads to stronger downstream RL performance. Building on these insights, we introduce a two-stage mid-training strategy, Stable-then-Decay, in which base models are first trained on 200B tokens with a constant learning rate, followed by 20B tokens across three CoT-focused branches with learning rate decay. This yields OctoThinker, a family of models demonstrating strong RL compatibility and closing the performance gap with more RL-friendly model families, i.e., Qwen. We hope our work will help shape pre-training strategies for foundation models in the RL era. To support further research, we release our open-source models along with a curated math reasoning-intensive corpus of over 70 billion tokens (i.e., MegaMath-Web-Pro-Max).
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "OctoThinker: Mid-training Incentivizes Reinforcement Learning Scaling" investigates how mid-training strategies influence the reinforcement learning (RL) performance of language models, focusing on Llama and Qwen families. The authors find that high-quality mathematical corpora (e.g., MegaMath-Web-Pro) are crucial for both base model and RL performance.  They also explore the impact of QA-style data, long chain-of-thought (CoT) examples, and instruction data on RL outcomes. A two-stage mid-training strategy (Stable-then-Decay) is introduced, resulting in the OctoThinker family of models which demonstrate improved RL compatibility and close the performance gap with RL-friendly Qwen models. The paper releases open-source models and a curated math reasoning corpus.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The idea of strategically using mid-training to improve the RL scaling of language models is not entirely new.  Previous works have explored the benefits of intermediate training stages, as cited. However, the paper’s systematic analysis of various factors (data quality, data format, instruction data) *and* their impact specifically on RL dynamics, is a significant contribution. The "Stable-then-Decay" training strategy is a practical method for adapting Llama models for improved RL scalability, but the novelty lies in the combination of strategies, analysis, and dataset curation.

*   **Significance:**  The paper tackles a crucial problem in the field: the inconsistent RL scalability across different language model families. Identifying key factors like data quality and the nuances of CoT data helps provide actionable insights for developing RL-scalable foundation models. The release of the OctoThinker models and the MegaMath-Web-Pro-Max corpus are valuable resources for the research community, potentially accelerating further research in RL-based language model training.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Systematic Analysis:** The paper presents a well-designed set of experiments that methodically explore the impact of different mid-training strategies on RL performance.
    *   **Actionable Insights:** The findings provide concrete guidance on data curation and training strategies for improving RL scalability.
    *   **Reproducibility:**  The release of models, data, and training scripts promotes reproducibility and encourages further research.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of inconsistent RL scaling and offers a practical solution.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Scope:** The experiments are primarily focused on mathematical reasoning tasks. While this provides a focused evaluation, it raises questions about the generalizability of the findings to other reasoning-intensive tasks or other domains.
    *   **Evolution of Llama:** The paper uses Llama 3.2, but LLama 3 and 3.5 models now offer better base reasoning. Re-examining the OctoThinker strategy in this context might be helpful.
    *   **Incremental Contribution:** While systematic and well-executed, the work builds upon existing ideas in mid-training and RLHF.  The impact is more evolutionary than revolutionary.  The results highlight existing disparities between Llama and Qwen base models but do not fully explain these disparities. More detailed insights as to why Llama responds poorly to specific data mixes might be beneficial.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper will likely influence researchers working on RL for language models, particularly those interested in improving the reasoning abilities of foundation models. The OctoThinker models and datasets will serve as valuable benchmarks for future research. The paper's focus on mid-training strategies is highly relevant given the growing interest in efficient and scalable methods for adapting language models for specific tasks.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper addresses a significant problem, provides valuable insights through systematic experimentation, and offers practical solutions and resources to the research community.  While the core ideas are not entirely groundbreaking, the rigor of the analysis, the actionable nature of the findings, and the release of resources justify a relatively high score. However, the limited scope and the lack of transformative results prevent it from receiving a score in the highest tier.

### Asymmetric REINFORCE for off-Policy Reinforcement Learning: Balancing positive and negative rewards
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20520v1)
- **Authors**: Charles Arnal, Gaëtan Narozniak, Vivien Cabannes, Yunhao Tang, Julia Kempe, Remi Munos
- **Abstract**: Reinforcement learning (RL) is increasingly used to align large language models (LLMs). Off-policy methods offer greater implementation simplicity and data efficiency than on-policy techniques, but often result in suboptimal performance. In this work, we study the intermediate range of algorithms between off-policy RL and supervised fine-tuning by analyzing a simple off-policy REINFORCE algorithm, where the advantage is defined as $A=r-V$, with $r$ a reward and $V$ some tunable baseline. Intuitively, lowering $V$ emphasizes high-reward samples, while raising it penalizes low-reward ones more heavily. We first provide a theoretical analysis of this off-policy REINFORCE algorithm, showing that when the baseline $V$ lower-bounds the expected reward, the algorithm enjoys a policy improvement guarantee. Our analysis reveals that while on-policy updates can safely leverage both positive and negative signals, off-policy updates benefit from focusing more on positive rewards than on negative ones. We validate our findings experimentally in a controlled stochastic bandit setting and through fine-tuning state-of-the-art LLMs on reasoning tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Asymmetric REINFORCE" (AsymRE), a modified off-policy REINFORCE algorithm for reinforcement learning (RL), particularly targeting the alignment of large language models (LLMs). The core idea is to tune the baseline `V` in the advantage function (A = r - V). By lowering `V`, the algorithm emphasizes high-reward samples, while raising it penalizes low-reward samples more heavily. The authors provide theoretical analysis demonstrating that when the baseline `V` lower-bounds the expected reward, the algorithm guarantees policy improvement. The analysis reveals that off-policy updates benefit from focusing more on positive rewards compared to negative ones.  The paper validates its findings through controlled experiments with stochastic bandits and by fine-tuning state-of-the-art LLMs on reasoning tasks. The results confirm the intuition that conservatively picking a smaller baseline is beneficial in off-policy settings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The idea of tuning the baseline in REINFORCE for off-policy learning is somewhat novel, although not entirely groundbreaking. While the paper makes a case for the *asymmetric* treatment of positive and negative rewards, the concept of baseline adjustment is not new.  The specific theoretical analysis of the algorithm's convergence and the impact of the baseline on policy support *does* contribute new insights. It extends existing work in off-policy RL.  Roux et al. (2025), a concurrent work also introduces an asymmetric variant of importance sampling, though the theoretical motivation for AsymRE appears quite distinct and justified.

* **Significance:**  The significance lies in the practical implications for LLM alignment. The paper addresses a crucial problem: the instability and challenges of applying off-policy RL to LLMs. The theoretical guarantees, while limited to a simple algorithm and tabular setting, provide guidance for practitioners. Demonstrating that a small modification to REINFORCE (baseline tuning) can improve stability and performance is valuable. The experimental results with LLMs on reasoning tasks further enhance the paper's relevance.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Theoretical Analysis:** The paper provides a rigorous theoretical analysis of AsymRE's convergence and behavior, especially regarding policy support.
    * **Practical Relevance:** The findings directly address the problem of off-policy RL for LLMs, offering practical guidelines for tuning the baseline.
    * **Experimental Validation:** The paper supports its theoretical claims with experiments in both a controlled environment (bandits) and a real-world LLM setting.
    * **Clear Intuition:** The core intuition about asymmetric treatment of rewards is well-explained.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Simplicity of AsymRE:** The algorithm itself is a relatively simple modification of REINFORCE. The impact comes from careful baseline tuning rather than a fundamentally new algorithm.
    * **Limited Theoretical Scope:** The theoretical analysis is primarily confined to tabular settings and a specific (softmax) policy representation. Extending the analysis to more complex policy spaces would be beneficial. The algorithm converges to something that improves upon your initial policy in tabular settings. A similar guarantee is not provided for the LLM setting.
    * **Empirical Scope:** While the LLM experiments are relevant, the number of tasks and models considered could be expanded for more comprehensive validation.
    * **Dependence on Behavioral Policy:** Performance seems to depend on behavioral policy and initial starting point, which may result in unpredictable performance.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the way off-policy RL is applied to LLMs, particularly in scenarios where stability and sample efficiency are critical. It encourages practitioners to carefully consider and tune the baseline in REINFORCE-like algorithms.  The analysis of policy support and its relation to the baseline could also inspire further research in this area.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution, although it's not a complete paradigm shift. The theoretical insights, practical recommendations for LLM alignment, and experimental validation strengthen the contribution, but the relative simplicity of the method and the limitations on theoretical scope restrict its significance. While not revolutionary, the presented work offers practical value for off-policy RL applied to LLMs and provides important theoretical groundwork for further research in this area.

### Case-based Reasoning Augmented Large Language Model Framework for Decision Making in Realistic Safety-Critical Driving Scenarios
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20531v1)
- **Authors**: Wenbin Gan, Minh-Son Dao, Koji Zettsu
- **Abstract**: Driving in safety-critical scenarios requires quick, context-aware decision-making grounded in both situational understanding and experiential reasoning. Large Language Models (LLMs), with their powerful general-purpose reasoning capabilities, offer a promising foundation for such decision-making. However, their direct application to autonomous driving remains limited due to challenges in domain adaptation, contextual grounding, and the lack of experiential knowledge needed to make reliable and interpretable decisions in dynamic, high-risk environments. To address this gap, this paper presents a Case-Based Reasoning Augmented Large Language Model (CBR-LLM) framework for evasive maneuver decision-making in complex risk scenarios. Our approach integrates semantic scene understanding from dashcam video inputs with the retrieval of relevant past driving cases, enabling LLMs to generate maneuver recommendations that are both context-sensitive and human-aligned. Experiments across multiple open-source LLMs show that our framework improves decision accuracy, justification quality, and alignment with human expert behavior. Risk-aware prompting strategies further enhance performance across diverse risk types, while similarity-based case retrieval consistently outperforms random sampling in guiding in-context learning. Case studies further demonstrate the framework's robustness in challenging real-world conditions, underscoring its potential as an adaptive and trustworthy decision-support tool for intelligent driving systems.
- **Summary**: This paper introduces CBR-LLM, a framework that combines Case-Based Reasoning (CBR) with Large Language Models (LLMs) to improve decision-making in safety-critical driving scenarios (SCDSs). The core idea is to leverage LLMs' reasoning abilities while grounding them in experiential knowledge retrieved from a curated database of past driving cases. The framework processes dashcam video data, identifies risk scenarios, retrieves similar historical cases, and then uses an LLM to generate context-aware and risk-sensitive maneuver recommendations. The authors evaluate CBR-LLM with several open-source LLMs on a real-world driving dataset, demonstrating improvements in decision accuracy, justification quality, and alignment with human expert behavior compared to vanilla LLM approaches. Risk-aware prompting and similarity-based case retrieval are shown to further enhance performance. Case studies illustrate the framework's robustness in challenging real-world conditions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Addressing a Key Limitation:** The paper directly addresses a critical limitation of LLMs in autonomous driving – their lack of domain-specific experiential knowledge and contextual grounding necessary for safe and reliable decision-making in dynamic, high-risk environments.
*   **Sound Methodology:** The CBR-LLM framework is well-designed, integrating semantic scene understanding, case retrieval, and LLM reasoning in a logical and coherent manner. The use of a real-world driving dataset adds to the practical relevance of the work.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation is thorough, comparing multiple LLMs, prompt configurations, and example selection methods. The use of both accuracy metrics and text generation evaluation metrics (BLEU, METEOR, ROUGE-L, CIDEr) provides a comprehensive assessment of performance. The comparison against Vanilla LLM's are crucial to evaluating the utility of the method.
*   **Cognitively Inspired:** The approach is inspired by cognitive science principles on human decision-making, providing a strong theoretical foundation for the framework.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the methodology, experiments, and results. The inclusion of case studies helps to illustrate the framework's capabilities.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** While the integration of CBR and LLMs is a valuable contribution, the individual components (CBR, LLMs, scene understanding) are not entirely novel. The novelty lies primarily in the combination and application to the specific problem of safety-critical driving. It is crucial to acknowledge prior work in applying CBR and LLMs in autonomous driving separately.
*   **Limited Dataset Size:** The dataset used for evaluation, while being a valuable and naturalistic data source, is somewhat limited in size (1000 videos after selection of only 7 risk categories). Larger-scale evaluations would further strengthen the findings.
*   **Specific Domain:** The framework is designed for a specific domain (SCDS) and specific tasks (evasive driving), and the results may not directly generalize to other driving tasks or other domains.
*   **Manual Labeling Requirements:** The semantic annotation of SCDSs, whilst producing good results, does increase the manual overhead that is required. It would have been beneficial if this section was improved with automated mechanisms to further the reach of its application.
*   **Scalability Concerns:** The paper describes CBR-LLM as a viable approach for real-world applications, but scaling CBR for real-time applications often encounters issues as the case-base increases in size. This aspect could benefit from further exploration.

**Significance:**

The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of autonomous driving by providing a more robust, adaptive, and trustworthy decision-making framework for safety-critical scenarios. The integration of experiential knowledge through CBR addresses a crucial gap in LLM-based approaches, potentially leading to safer and more human-like driving behavior. The work can also contribute to the broader field of AI by demonstrating how symbolic reasoning and neural networks can be effectively combined to solve complex real-world problems.

**Score: 7.5**

**Justification:**

The paper makes a significant contribution by addressing a critical challenge in applying LLMs to autonomous driving. The well-designed methodology, comprehensive evaluation, and promising results demonstrate the potential of the CBR-LLM framework for improving safety and trustworthiness in SCDSs. However, the incremental novelty of the individual components, limited dataset size, specific domain focus, manual labeling requirements and scaling concerns prevent it from achieving a higher score. Nevertheless, the paper is a valuable contribution to the field and has the potential to influence future research in autonomous driving and AI.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### WattsOnAI: Measuring, Analyzing, and Visualizing Energy and Carbon Footprint of AI Workloads
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20535v1)
- **Authors**: Hongzhen Huang, Kunming Zhang, Hanlong Liao, Kui Wu, Guoming Tang
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of AI, particularly large language models (LLMs), has raised significant concerns about the energy use and carbon emissions associated with model training and inference. However, existing tools for measuring and reporting such impacts are often fragmented, lacking systematic metric integration and offering limited support for correlation analysis among them. This paper presents WattsOnAI, a comprehensive software toolkit for the measurement, analysis, and visualization of energy use, power draw, hardware performance, and carbon emissions across AI workloads. By seamlessly integrating with existing AI frameworks, WattsOnAI offers standardized reports and exports fine-grained time-series data to support benchmarking and reproducibility in a lightweight manner. It further enables in-depth correlation analysis between hardware metrics and model performance and thus facilitates bottleneck identification and performance enhancement. By addressing critical limitations in existing tools, WattsOnAI encourages the research community to weigh environmental impact alongside raw performance of AI workloads and advances the shift toward more sustainable "Green AI" practices. The code is available at https://github.com/SusCom-Lab/WattsOnAI.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces WattsOnAI, a software toolkit designed to measure, analyze, and visualize the energy consumption, power draw, hardware performance, and carbon emissions of AI workloads.  WattsOnAI integrates with existing AI frameworks, provides standardized reports, supports fine-grained time-series data exports for benchmarking and reproducibility, and enables correlation analysis between hardware metrics and model performance. The authors emphasize the tool's ease of use, low overhead, and multi-dimensional visualization capabilities to promote "Green AI" practices.  A case study involving Llama2-7b inference is presented to demonstrate WattsOnAI's features and highlight the importance of geographical location in carbon emission estimation.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper addresses a relevant and increasingly important issue: the environmental cost of AI.  While the motivation is strong and the problem well-defined, the *novelty* of the specific solution is somewhat limited. The core idea of monitoring energy and carbon impact isn't entirely new, as the paper itself acknowledges existing tools like PyJoules and CodeCarbon. The contributions lie primarily in the *integration*, *standardization*, and *visualization aspects*.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Metric Integration:** WattsOnAI's ability to combine real-time energy, power, hardware, and carbon emission data is a clear advantage over fragmented existing tools. The unified reports and time-series data exports are useful for benchmarking.
    *   **Emphasis on Ease of Use:** The claim of seamless integration with AI workflows and low performance overhead is a significant practical benefit.  This is especially valuable for researchers who might be deterred by complex setups.
    *   **Multi-Dimensional Visualization:** The implementation of multi-dimensional visualization capabilities and correlation analysis bridges a gap in many existing tools, allowing for a more in-depth understanding of AI workload efficiency.
    *   **Practical Demonstration:** The Llama2-7b case study offers a concrete example of how WattsOnAI can be used to analyze phase dynamics, estimate carbon emissions based on location, and measure overhead.
    *   **Open Source Availability**: Makes the work accesible to other researchers.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Technical Depth on Core Measurement Techniques:** The paper doesn't delve into the specific algorithms or methods used for energy/power measurement or carbon emission estimation. It relies heavily on existing hardware interfaces and external services (Electricity Maps, WattTime). A deeper discussion of the potential inaccuracies or limitations of these underlying methods would be beneficial.
    *   **Lack of Comparison to Existing Solutions:** Although the paper mentions other tools, there isn't a detailed quantitative comparison demonstrating WattsOnAI's superiority in terms of overhead, accuracy, or comprehensiveness. This makes it difficult to objectively assess its advantages.
    *   **Limited Validation:** The overhead analysis is relatively basic and could be expanded. A more rigorous validation of the accuracy of the energy/carbon measurements would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Incremental Innovation:** The tool's innovation is mostly incremental, bringing together functionality from other tools in a convenient manner. It doesn't introduce a fundamentally new method for energy or carbon measurement in AI.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Promoting Green AI:** By making energy and carbon impact easier to measure and analyze, WattsOnAI can encourage researchers and practitioners to prioritize energy-efficient AI development. This is a significant step towards making AI more sustainable.
    *   **Reproducibility:** Providing time-series data and standardized reports will improve reproducibility and facilitate benchmarking of AI workloads' energy efficiency.

**Justification for the score:**

WattsOnAI addresses a real and growing concern about the sustainability of AI. The tool offers practical value by integrating fragmented features into a single, easy-to-use system and emphasizing useful visualizations. However, the innovation is incremental rather than groundbreaking. The reliance on existing measurement interfaces without significant contributions to measurement accuracy or efficiency limits the overall novelty. The lack of a detailed quantitative comparison against existing solutions is also a drawback.  The open-source availability enhances the project's impact and allows others to build upon the foundation, making it a valuable, although not revolutionary, advancement.

### When Life Gives You Samples: The Benefits of Scaling up Inference Compute for Multilingual LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20544v1)
- **Authors**: Ammar Khairi, Daniel D'souza, Ye Shen, Julia Kreutzer, Sara Hooker
- **Abstract**: Recent advancements in large language models (LLMs) have shifted focus toward scaling inference-time compute, improving performance without retraining the model. A common approach is to sample multiple outputs in parallel, and select one of these as the final output. However, work to date has focused on English and a handful of domains such as math and code. In contrast, we are most interested in techniques that generalize across open-ended tasks, formally verifiable tasks, and across languages. In this work, we study how to robustly scale inference-time compute for open-ended generative tasks in a multilingual, multi-task setting. Our findings show that both sampling strategy based on temperature variation and selection strategy must be adapted to account for diverse domains and varied language settings. We evaluate existing selection methods, revealing that strategies effective in English often fail to generalize across languages. We propose novel sampling and selection strategies specifically adapted for multilingual and multi-task inference scenarios, and show they yield notable gains across languages and tasks. In particular, our combined sampling and selection methods lead to an average +6.8 jump in win-rates for our 8B models on m-ArenaHard-v2.0 prompts, against proprietary models such as Gemini. At larger scale, Command-A (111B model) equipped with our methods, shows +9.0 improvement in win-rates on the same benchmark with just five samples against single-sample decoding, a substantial increase at minimal cost. Our results underscore the need for language- and task-aware approaches to inference-time compute, aiming to democratize performance improvements in underrepresented languages.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates how to effectively scale inference-time compute for multilingual large language models (LLMs) in open-ended generative tasks. Instead of retraining or increasing model size, the authors explore parallel sampling, where multiple outputs are generated and the best one is selected. They find that strategies optimized for English often fail to generalize across languages. To address this, they propose novel sampling (Hedged Sampling) and selection (CHOPS, X-MBR) strategies tailored for multilingual and multi-task scenarios. Their methods demonstrate significant improvements across languages and tasks, particularly for underrepresented languages, even when compared against larger, proprietary models like Gemini. They distill their approach into a "Multilingual LLMonade Recipe" for maximizing performance with minimal cost.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its focus on multilingual and multi-task inference, a relatively underexplored area compared to English-centric research. The introduction of "hedged sampling" (combining stochastic and deterministic outputs) and the task-aware selection strategies "CHOPS" and "X-MBR" represents a tangible advancement. The emphasis on generalizing *across* languages and tasks, rather than optimizing for a specific domain, is a welcome and novel approach.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance is threefold:

    1.  **Practical Impact:** The proposed methods are computationally efficient, achieving substantial performance gains with minimal added cost, making them practical for real-world deployment. This is especially important for democratizing access to high-performing LLMs in underrepresented languages.
    2.  **Generalizability:** The methods are shown to be effective across a diverse range of languages, tasks (open-ended generation, math reasoning, translation), and models.
    3.  **Insights and Future Directions:** The paper provides valuable insights into the challenges of multilingual inference and highlights the need for language- and task-aware approaches. It suggests future research directions focused on model-based judgements within the LLM rather than relying on other models.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong empirical evaluation across diverse benchmarks and models, including comparisons against proprietary models.
    *   Clear explanation and justification of the proposed methods, with detailed analysis of the results.
    *   Practical and efficient approaches suitable for real-world deployment.
    *   Emphasis on addressing the needs of underrepresented languages, contributing to a more equitable landscape.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on GPT-4 for win rate calculation is noted.
    *   The selection of languages and tasks, while broad, is still a subset of the vast linguistic and problem space. Performance on truly low-resource languages remains an open question, though the X-MBR potentially helps with that.
    *   While the "LLMonade Recipe" is a good summary, the "optional" localization of temperature remains a hurdle for wider deployment, requiring extensive tuning.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in several ways:

    *   It could encourage researchers to focus more on multilingual and multi-task inference.
    *   It provides a strong baseline and valuable insights for developing more generalizable inference strategies.
    *   It could inspire the development of novel sampling and selection methods that are better adapted to the challenges of diverse languages and tasks.
    *   The efficient techniques will aid in democratizing performance in underrepresented languages.

*   **Justification:**
    The paper tackles an important problem (scaling inference for multilingual LLMs) with a novel and well-validated approach. The practical benefits, generalizability, and potential influence on the field justify a reasonably high score. While not a *fundamental* breakthrough, it addresses a significant limitation of current LLMs and provides a clear path forward for future research.

### Pay Less Attention to Deceptive Artifacts: Robust Detection of Compressed Deepfakes on Online Social Networks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20548v1)
- **Authors**: Manyi Li, Renshuai Tao, Yufan Liu, Chuangchuang Tan, Haotong Qin, Bing Li, Yunchao Wei, Yao Zhao
- **Abstract**: With the rapid advancement of deep learning, particularly through generative adversarial networks (GANs) and diffusion models (DMs), AI-generated images, or ``deepfakes", have become nearly indistinguishable from real ones. These images are widely shared across Online Social Networks (OSNs), raising concerns about their misuse. Existing deepfake detection methods overlook the ``block effects" introduced by compression in OSNs, which obscure deepfake artifacts, and primarily focus on raw images, rarely encountered in real-world scenarios. To address these challenges, we propose PLADA (Pay Less Attention to Deceptive Artifacts), a novel framework designed to tackle the lack of paired data and the ineffective use of compressed images. PLADA consists of two core modules: Block Effect Eraser (B2E), which uses a dual-stage attention mechanism to handle block effects, and Open Data Aggregation (ODA), which processes both paired and unpaired data to improve detection. Extensive experiments across 26 datasets demonstrate that PLADA achieves a remarkable balance in deepfake detection, outperforming SoTA methods in detecting deepfakes on OSNs, even with limited paired data and compression. More importantly, this work introduces the ``block effect" as a critical factor in deepfake detection, providing a robust solution for open-world scenarios. Our code is available at https://github.com/ManyiLee/PLADA.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Pay Less Attention to Deceptive Artifacts: Robust Detection of Compressed Deepfakes on Online Social Networks":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of detecting deepfakes in real-world online social network (OSN) scenarios, where images are often compressed, leading to "block effects" that can mislead existing detectors.  The authors propose a novel framework called PLADA (Pay Less Attention to Deceptive Artifacts) that comprises two modules: Block Effect Eraser (B2E) and Open Data Aggregation (ODA).  B2E uses a dual-stage attention shift mechanism to mitigate block effects, while ODA leverages both paired (original and compressed) and unpaired data to improve detection robustness.  The authors conduct extensive experiments on a large number of datasets, demonstrating that PLADA outperforms state-of-the-art methods, especially in detecting compressed deepfakes on OSNs with limited paired data. The authors introduce the "block effect" as a critical factor in deepfake detection.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in identifying and explicitly addressing the "block effect" caused by compression as a significant challenge in real-world deepfake detection. While compression robustness has been tackled before, this work specifically targets the artifacts introduced by compression, which mimic forgery artifacts and thus makes detector fail. The proposed B2E module, with its dual-stage attention mechanism and a dedicated approach to attention shifting, provides a novel solution to this problem. ODA's use of both paired and unpaired data is also a valuable contribution, given the limited availability of paired data in real-world scenarios. The architecture is also novel in the way it shifts attention to the artifacts with new knowledge injection layers that guide the backbone.

* **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its focus on a practical problem: detecting deepfakes in OSNs.  The extensive experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of PLADA in this context, outperforming existing methods. The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contributions of the individual modules. The experiments include many different generative models and comprehensive analysis. This is very useful as it gives a real picture about the performance in wide context.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Problem Focus:** The paper addresses a critical and relevant problem with significant real-world implications.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The extensive experiments across many datasets, including both GAN-generated and diffusion-model-generated deepfakes, and the detailed ablation studies, provide strong evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed method.
    * **Clear and Well-Written:** The paper is generally well-written and clearly explains the proposed method and the experimental results.
    * **Reproducibility:** Providing the code link enhances reproducibility.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Complexity:** The proposed framework, with its dual-stage attention mechanism and ODA module, is relatively complex. While the ablation studies provide some insights, a more intuitive explanation of *why* the specific design choices were made would be beneficial.
    * **Limited Exploration of Alternatives:** While the paper compares PLADA to several existing methods, there is limited discussion of alternative approaches for mitigating block effects. The paper could benefit from a more critical analysis of the strengths and weaknesses of different approaches, not only methods for deepfake detection but also tools for image processing and compression.
    * **Dependence on CLIP:** The method relies on CLIP, which has known biases and limitations. It would be valuable to explore the performance of PLADA with other backbone models to assess its generalizability.
    * **Generalizability to video:** Paper only discusses images but in real world scenarios, most deepfakes are usually in videos. So, future direction should focus on video based deepfake detection.
    * **Lack of failure cases**: It's important to discuss the cases where this fails so future work can target that.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in deepfake detection by highlighting the importance of addressing compression artifacts. The proposed PLADA framework and the B2E module provide a strong foundation for developing more robust deepfake detectors. The paper also is significant because the authors provide the code, which is important for future developments.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a significant contribution to the field of deepfake detection, the issues noted, particularly regarding complexity and limited exploration of alternatives, prevents it from achieving a higher score. I am giving a score of 7 due to novel identification of a real world problem:

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Exploring Graph-Transformer Out-of-Distribution Generalization Abilities
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20575v1)
- **Authors**: Itay Niv, Neta Rabin
- **Abstract**: Deep learning on graphs has shown remarkable success across numerous applications, including social networks, bio-physics, traffic networks, and recommendation systems. Regardless of their successes, current methods frequently depend on the assumption that training and testing data share the same distribution, a condition rarely met in real-world scenarios. While graph-transformer (GT) backbones have recently outperformed traditional message-passing neural networks (MPNNs) in multiple in-distribution (ID) benchmarks, their effectiveness under distribution shifts remains largely unexplored. In this work, we address the challenge of out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization for graph neural networks, with a special focus on the impact of backbone architecture. We systematically evaluate GT and hybrid backbones in OOD settings and compare them to MPNNs. To do so, we adapt several leading domain generalization (DG) algorithms to work with GTs and assess their performance on a benchmark designed to test a variety of distribution shifts. Our results reveal that GT and hybrid GT-MPNN backbones consistently demonstrate stronger generalization ability compared to MPNNs, even without specialized DG algorithms. Additionally, we propose a novel post-training analysis approach that compares the clustering structure of the entire ID and OOD test datasets, specifically examining domain alignment and class separation. Demonstrating its model-agnostic design, this approach not only provided meaningful insights into GT and MPNN backbones. It also shows promise for broader applicability to DG problems beyond graph learning, offering a deeper perspective on generalization abilities that goes beyond standard accuracy metrics. Together, our findings highlight the promise of graph-transformers for robust, real-world graph learning and set a new direction for future research in OOD generalization.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the out-of-distribution (OOD) generalization abilities of graph transformers (GTs) compared to message-passing neural networks (MPNNs) for graph classification. The authors systematically evaluate GT and hybrid GT-MPNN backbones in OOD settings, adapting several domain generalization (DG) algorithms and comparing performance to MPNNs using the GOOD benchmark. They find that GT and hybrid architectures consistently demonstrate stronger generalization, even without specialized DG algorithms. A novel post-training analysis framework is proposed, examining the clustering structure of ID and OOD data via Maximum Mean Discrepancy (MMD) and Silhouette scores to analyze domain alignment and class separation. This analysis provides insights beyond standard accuracy metrics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper makes several contributions, including:

    *   **Systematic OOD evaluation of GTs:** This is a significant contribution, as prior work primarily focused on MPNNs or in-distribution performance. The comparison of GTs, hybrid architectures, and MPNNs under various distribution shifts is valuable.
    *   **Adaptation of DG algorithms:** The effort to adapt several DG algorithms to GT backbones is beneficial for future research.
    *   **Post-training analysis framework:** The proposed MMD and Silhouette score-based analysis is a novel way to evaluate OOD generalization, offering a deeper understanding of model behavior in latent space. The ability to inspect a model's OOD generalization capabilities without retraining is a great aspect for real-world applications.
    *   **Empirical findings:** The empirical results, demonstrating the superior generalization of GTs (especially hybrid ones) and showing the importance of backbone choice over specific DG algorithms, are significant.
*   **Significance:**

    *   **Addressing a critical challenge:** OOD generalization is a crucial problem in graph learning, and this paper directly addresses it. The findings have practical implications for deploying graph models in real-world scenarios.
    *   **Benchmarking and insights:** The systematic evaluation and the new analysis framework provide valuable benchmarks and insights for the community.
    *   **Future directions:** The paper highlights the promise of GTs for robust graph learning and sets a new direction for future research in OOD generalization.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper provides a comprehensive set of experiments that involve various distribution shifts, DG algorithms, and backbone architectures.
    *   **Novel Metrics:** The introduction of the post-hoc analysis framework based on MMD and Silhouette scores presents a novel and insightful method for evaluating model generalization.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents the methods, results, and analysis.
    *   **Reproducibility:** The authors mentioned that the source code will be publicly available. This fosters reproducibility and encourages future research building upon their work.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational cost:** While the hybrid GT-MPNN approaches yield robust performance, the computational cost of GT's attention mechanism may be a significant bottleneck for large graphs, which isn't explicitly addressed.
    *   **Limited datasets:** The reliance on only the GOOD benchmark might limit the generalizability of the findings to other types of graphs and distribution shifts.
    *   **Tuning of hyperparameters:** While they tune hyperparameters, it's unclear how exhaustive this process was, and whether there is room for further improvement in specific backbone/DG combinations.

*   **Overall:** The paper offers a valuable contribution to the field by systematically evaluating GTs for OOD graph learning, proposing a novel analysis framework, and providing valuable insights and future directions. The novelty is significant, and the results have the potential to influence future research in this area.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:**

The score of 8 reflects the paper's strong contributions in terms of novelty, significance, and comprehensive evaluation. The insights from the new MMD and Silhouette score metrics, combined with the demonstration of superior performance by hybrid GT-MPNN architectures, advance the field of OOD generalization in graph learning. The limitations (computational cost, limited datasets, and hyperparameter tuning) are relatively minor compared to the overall impact and don't detract significantly from the contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### TRIM: A Self-Supervised Video Summarization Framework Maximizing Temporal Relative Information and Representativeness
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20588v1)
- **Authors**: Pritam Mishra, Coloma Ballester, Dimosthenis Karatzas
- **Abstract**: The increasing ubiquity of video content and the corresponding demand for efficient access to meaningful information have elevated video summarization and video highlights as a vital research area. However, many state-of-the-art methods depend heavily either on supervised annotations or on attention-based models, which are computationally expensive and brittle in the face of distribution shifts that hinder cross-domain applicability across datasets. We introduce a pioneering self-supervised video summarization model that captures both spatial and temporal dependencies without the overhead of attention, RNNs, or transformers. Our framework integrates a novel set of Markov process-driven loss metrics and a two-stage self supervised learning paradigm that ensures both performance and efficiency. Our approach achieves state-of-the-art performance on the SUMME and TVSUM datasets, outperforming all existing unsupervised methods. It also rivals the best supervised models, demonstrating the potential for efficient, annotation-free architectures. This paves the way for more generalizable video summarization techniques and challenges the prevailing reliance on complex architectures.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces TRIM, a novel self-supervised video summarization framework.  It aims to address the limitations of existing methods that rely on supervised annotations or computationally expensive attention-based mechanisms. TRIM leverages a 1D CNN to capture spatio-temporal dependencies between frames in a video.  It employs a two-stage self-supervised learning paradigm, using a novel set of Markov process-driven loss metrics and a representativeness loss.  The first stage pre-trains the network with a masking-based approach. The second stage fine-tunes the network using the proposed loss functions. The authors demonstrate state-of-the-art performance on the SUMME and TVSUM datasets compared to other unsupervised methods, and competitive performance relative to some supervised methods while using significantly less computational power than many modern attention-based models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper has several novel aspects:
    * **Markov Process-Driven Loss Metrics:**  The use of PTRI and PCTRI to quantify information change across frames is a fresh approach. This is one of the most significant contributions, moving away from direct feature comparisons.
    * **1D CNN for Spatio-Temporal Dependencies:** While CNNs are common in video processing, the specific use of a *simple* 1D CNN (avoiding attention or transformers) for spatio-temporal dependency modeling in a *self-supervised* video summarization setting is novel.  The emphasis on efficiency is a key differentiating factor.
    * **Two-Stage Self-Supervised Approach:** The combination of masking-based pre-training and fine-tuning with the proposed losses seems well-designed.

* **Significance:**
    * **Computational Efficiency:** A major strength is the demonstration of high performance with significantly lower computational costs compared to many existing methods, particularly those employing attention. This opens up possibilities for deployment in resource-constrained environments.
    * **Reduced Reliance on Annotations:** The self-supervised nature reduces the need for expensive labeled data, making the approach more scalable and adaptable to new datasets and domains.
    * **Generalizability:**  The authors claim improved generalizability, but this could be further validated with evaluations on a wider range of datasets.  The dataset specific hyperparameter tuning (mentioned in Sections 3 and 4, and in the supplemental material) raises some questions about generalizability that require addressing.

* **Strengths:**
    * Strong empirical results on standard benchmarks.
    * Well-defined loss functions with clear motivation.
    * Detailed ablation studies that provide insights into the contribution of each component.
    * Thorough comparison with related work.
    * The design emphasis on simplicity and computational efficiency.
    * Very clearly presented results and ablations with appropriate justifications for the chosen method configurations (e.g., hyperparameter selections in sections 3 and 4). The justification in A.3.2 of the appendix is especially good for defending the design choices.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Architecture Exploration:** While the simplicity of the 1D CNN is a strength, the paper doesn't extensively explore alternative architectures. It's unclear if the proposed losses would generalize to more complex (but still efficient) architectures.
    * **Dataset-Specific Tuning:**  The need to tune hyperparameters (specifically ν, α, β, and γ) for different datasets could indicate that the method is not completely free from dataset-specific dependencies. This reduces the practical usability of the algorithm since end-users will need to perform expensive hyperparameter tuning whenever a new dataset is encountered. A more rigorous exploration of the hyperparameter spaces, together with a more detailed justification for the ranges searched, would strengthen the experimental setup.
    * **Modest improvement in F1 score.**: While the novelty is in the loss function and the performance is comparable to supervised methods, the actual gains in F1 scores for self-supervised learning (from 48.7 on SSPVS to 59.61 for TRIM) is a relatively small improvement.
    * **Limited qualitative assessment:** While metrics are good, more qualitative summarizations to support the results will be beneficial.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by:
    * Encouraging more research into efficient and annotation-free video summarization techniques.
    * Providing a new set of tools (Markov process-driven losses) for tackling temporal dependency modeling.
    * Challenging the prevailing reliance on complex, computationally expensive architectures.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

TRIM presents a novel and well-executed approach to self-supervised video summarization with a strong emphasis on efficiency.  The Markov process-driven losses and the effective use of a simple 1D CNN are significant contributions.  The empirical results are convincing, and the ablation studies are insightful.  The key weakness is the need for dataset-specific tuning. A more exhaustive evaluation of generalizability would significantly strengthen the paper, justifying a higher score. While the gains in F1 score are modest, the significantly reduced compute requirements represent a trade-off that may be valued in certain applications. The limitations in architectural exploration also justify a lower score.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Video Perception Models for 3D Scene Synthesis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20601v1)
- **Authors**: Rui Huang, Guangyao Zhai, Zuria Bauer, Marc Pollefeys, Federico Tombari, Leonidas Guibas, Gao Huang, Francis Engelmann
- **Abstract**: Traditionally, 3D scene synthesis requires expert knowledge and significant manual effort. Automating this process could greatly benefit fields such as architectural design, robotics simulation, virtual reality, and gaming. Recent approaches to 3D scene synthesis often rely on the commonsense reasoning of large language models (LLMs) or strong visual priors of modern image generation models. However, current LLMs demonstrate limited 3D spatial reasoning ability, which restricts their ability to generate realistic and coherent 3D scenes. Meanwhile, image generation-based methods often suffer from constraints in viewpoint selection and multi-view inconsistencies. In this work, we present Video Perception models for 3D Scene synthesis (VIPScene), a novel framework that exploits the encoded commonsense knowledge of the 3D physical world in video generation models to ensure coherent scene layouts and consistent object placements across views. VIPScene accepts both text and image prompts and seamlessly integrates video generation, feedforward 3D reconstruction, and open-vocabulary perception models to semantically and geometrically analyze each object in a scene. This enables flexible scene synthesis with high realism and structural consistency. For more precise analysis, we further introduce First-Person View Score (FPVScore) for coherence and plausibility evaluation, utilizing continuous first-person perspective to capitalize on the reasoning ability of multimodal large language models. Extensive experiments show that VIPScene significantly outperforms existing methods and generalizes well across diverse scenarios. The code will be released.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Video Perception Models for 3D Scene Synthesis":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces VIPSCENE, a novel framework for 3D scene synthesis.  It leverages video generation models to encode 3D physical world common sense, ensuring coherent scene layouts and consistent object placements across views. VIPSCENE takes text and/or image prompts and integrates video generation, 3D reconstruction, and open-vocabulary perception models to analyze objects semantically and geometrically. It also introduces FPVSCORE (First-Person View Score), a new metric that uses continuous first-person perspective and multimodal large language models to evaluate coherence and plausibility, improving assessment quality. Experiments show VIPSCENE significantly outperforms existing methods and generalizes well.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its clever integration of video generation priors into the 3D scene synthesis pipeline. Existing methods often rely on language models (LLMs), visual priors from image generation, or a combination of both, but these have limitations in 3D spatial reasoning or viewpoint consistency.  The use of video, with its inherent temporal coherence, to inform 3D scene layout is a significant innovation.  The development of FPVSCORE is another point of novelty, addressing the limitations of existing metrics in evaluating spatial coherence by adopting a first-person, comprehensive view assessment. Also, the modular decomposition is a nice touch.

* **Significance:**  The potential impact of this work is considerable. Automating 3D scene synthesis has many applications in architectural design, robotics simulation, VR/AR, and gaming.  By improving the realism and consistency of generated scenes, VIPSCENE can contribute significantly to these fields.  The more effective evaluation metric FPVSCORE could drive further advancements in the field by providing a more accurate and reliable way to assess and compare scene synthesis methods.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Effective Integration:** VIPSCENE successfully integrates diverse components (video generation, 3D reconstruction, object detection) into a coherent framework.
    * **Improved Spatial Reasoning:** Leveraging video priors leads to more realistic and consistent object placement compared to LLM-only or image-only approaches.
    * **FPVSCORE Innovation:** Addresses shortcomings of traditional metrics and provides a more human-aligned evaluation, validated by user studies.
    * **Modularity:** The decomposition and re-composition pipeline facilitates further improvements and extensions.
    * **Strong Experimental Results:** Demonstrates superior performance compared to baselines, both qualitatively and quantitatively.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on Video Generation Quality:** The performance of VIPSCENE is dependent on the quality of the underlying video generation model.  Artifacts or limitations in the video will propagate to the synthesized scene.
    * **Asset Database Dependence:**  The re-composition step relies on a database of 3D assets, which might limit the diversity and realism of generated objects if the database is incomplete. While the paper uses a subset of Objaverse, it's crucial to consider how the method scales with a more comprehensive (but potentially lower quality) database.
    * **Limited Object-Level Realism:** While the paper addresses layout coherence, it acknowledges limitations in object-level realism and the need for advanced 3D object generation and rendering techniques.
    * **Computational Cost:**  The pipeline involves several computationally intensive steps (video generation, 3D reconstruction, asset retrieval, optimization), which could limit its practicality for real-time applications. This is not thoroughly discussed in the paper.

* **Justification for Score:** The paper presents a substantial contribution with a novel and effective approach to 3D scene synthesis. The integration of video priors and the development of FPVSCORE address key limitations in the field. While it has some weaknesses in terms of reliance on video and asset quality and computational cost, these are potential areas for future research. The experimental results and user studies support the claims made in the paper.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### Model Editing as a Double-Edged Sword: Steering Agent Ethical Behavior Toward Beneficence or Harm
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20606v1)
- **Authors**: Baixiang Huang, Zhen Tan, Haoran Wang, Zijie Liu, Dawei Li, Ali Payani, Huan Liu, Tianlong Chen, Kai Shu
- **Abstract**: Agents based on Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated strong capabilities across a wide range of tasks. However, deploying LLM-based agents in high-stakes domains comes with significant safety and ethical risks. Unethical behavior by these agents can directly result in serious real-world consequences, including physical harm and financial loss. To efficiently steer the ethical behavior of agents, we frame agent behavior steering as a model editing task, which we term Behavior Editing. Model editing is an emerging area of research that enables precise and efficient modifications to LLMs while preserving their overall capabilities. To systematically study and evaluate this approach, we introduce BehaviorBench, a multi-tier benchmark grounded in psychological moral theories. This benchmark supports both the evaluation and editing of agent behaviors across a variety of scenarios, with each tier introducing more complex and ambiguous scenarios. We first demonstrate that Behavior Editing can dynamically steer agents toward the target behavior within specific scenarios. Moreover, Behavior Editing enables not only scenario-specific local adjustments but also more extensive shifts in an agent's global moral alignment. We demonstrate that Behavior Editing can be used to promote ethical and benevolent behavior or, conversely, to induce harmful or malicious behavior. Through comprehensive evaluations on agents based on frontier LLMs, BehaviorBench shows the effectiveness of Behavior Editing across different models and scenarios. Our findings offer key insights into a new paradigm for steering agent behavior, highlighting both the promise and perils of Behavior Editing.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Behavior Editing," a novel framework for steering the ethical behavior of Large Language Model (LLM)-based agents.  It frames ethical alignment as a model editing task, allowing for precise and efficient modifications to agent behavior without extensive retraining.  The authors develop BEHAVIORBENCH, a multi-tiered benchmark grounded in psychological moral theories, to evaluate and compare behavior editing techniques.  The study demonstrates that behavior editing can effectively steer agents toward both benevolent and malicious behaviors, highlighting both the potential benefits and the significant safety risks associated with this approach.  Experiments on both open-source and proprietary LLMs demonstrate the effectiveness of the framework. The paper concludes by emphasizing the need for responsible deployment and ongoing research into robust and reliable methods for behavior steering.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper presents a novel perspective by framing ethical behavior steering as a model editing problem. While model editing itself is not entirely new, applying it to the domain of ethical behavior and exploring its bi-directional (benevolent vs. malicious) nature is a significant contribution.  The introduction of BEHAVIORBENCH, specifically designed for evaluating ethical behavior edits, is also a valuable contribution. However, the individual techniques they utilize are pre-existing methods, they are not novel. The real novelty lies in the conceptual framework and the specific application to steering ethical agent behavior.
* **Significance:**  The significance of the paper stems from the growing concern regarding the ethical alignment of LLM-based agents. By demonstrating the feasibility of steering agent behavior, even towards harmful actions, the paper underscores the urgency of developing robust safeguards. BEHAVIORBENCH provides a valuable tool for researchers to evaluate and compare different alignment techniques. The paper also raises crucial ethical considerations regarding the potential misuse of behavior editing for malicious purposes. The detailed analysis based on normative ethical factors offers a more nuanced understanding of how editing techniques can influence agent behavior in different ethical dimensions. The discovery that more recent, reasoning-capable models are better at resisting malicious editing is a key finding that can guide future research.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of ethical behavior steering and presents a well-motivated framework for addressing it.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** BEHAVIORBENCH offers a comprehensive evaluation framework, covering a range of ethical scenarios and different levels of moral reasoning.
    * **Empirical Validation:** The paper provides extensive experimental results, demonstrating the effectiveness of behavior editing across various LLMs.
    * **Ethical Considerations:** The authors acknowledge and discuss the ethical implications of their work, highlighting the potential for misuse and the need for responsible deployment.
    * **Well-Structured and Clear Writing:** The paper is well-written and clearly organized, making it easy to understand the proposed framework and the experimental results.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope of Mitigations:** While the paper discusses the risks of malicious behavior editing, it does not provide concrete mitigation strategies. This could be considered a limitation.
    * **Reliance on Existing Editing Methods:** The paper primarily relies on existing model editing techniques, rather than proposing new ones. This limits the novelty of the approach to the conceptual framework and the application to ethical behavior.
    * **Textual Environments:** The paper focuses on steering ethical behavior in textual environments. The transferability of these techniques to more complex, real-world scenarios is an open question.
* **Impact:** The paper has a good potential impact because it is timely, contributes a novel evaluation benchmark, and explores a problem of increasing interest and relevance to researchers and practitioners concerned with the ethical implications of AI.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper is an interesting and well-done exploration of ethical behavior steering. The novelty of the *framing* of behavior editing to guide agents is interesting but is a incremental contribution as the underlying techniques are mostly pre-existing, but the systematic approach to ethical issues earns this a score that is moderately high. The benchmark is useful to future researchers.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### AI Assistants to Enhance and Exploit the PETSc Knowledge Base
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20608v1)
- **Authors**: Barry Smith, Junchao Zhang, Hong Zhang, Lois Curfman McInnes, Murat Keceli, Archit Vasan, Satish Balay, Toby Isaac, Le Chen, Venkatram Vishwanath
- **Abstract**: Generative AI, especially through large language models (LLMs), is transforming how technical knowledge can be accessed, reused, and extended. PETSc, a widely used numerical library for high-performance scientific computing, has accumulated a rich but fragmented knowledge base over its three decades of development, spanning source code, documentation, mailing lists, GitLab issues, Discord conversations, technical papers, and more. Much of this knowledge remains informal and inaccessible to users and new developers. To activate and utilize this knowledge base more effectively, the PETSc team has begun building an LLM-powered system that combines PETSc content with custom LLM tools -- including retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), reranking algorithms, and chatbots -- to assist users, support developers, and propose updates to formal documentation. This paper presents initial experiences designing and evaluating these tools, focusing on system architecture, using RAG and reranking for PETSc-specific information, evaluation methodologies for various LLMs and embedding models, and user interface design. Leveraging the Argonne Leadership Computing Facility resources, we analyze how LLM responses can enhance the development and use of numerical software, with an initial focus on scalable Krylov solvers. Our goal is to establish an extensible framework for knowledge-centered AI in scientific software, enabling scalable support, enriched documentation, and enhanced workflows for research and development. We conclude by outlining directions for expanding this system into a robust, evolving platform that advances software ecosystems to accelerate scientific discovery.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) to enhance and exploit the knowledge base of PETSc, a widely used numerical library for high-performance scientific computing. The authors propose an LLM-powered system that combines PETSc content with custom LLM tools like Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), reranking, and chatbots to assist users, support developers, and suggest documentation updates. They detail the system architecture, RAG implementation, evaluation methodologies, and user interface design. They show how LLM responses can improve numerical software development and use, focusing on scalable Krylov solvers.  The goal is to create an extensible AI framework for scientific software, improving support, documentation, and workflows. A key component is the integration with the PETSc Discord server to allow for real-time AI assistance while maintaining developer oversight.  The paper showcases preliminary results demonstrating the benefits of RAG and reranking in improving the accuracy of LLM responses to PETSc-related queries.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its specific application of LLMs to the domain of high-performance scientific computing and, more particularly, to the PETSc library. While RAG and similar techniques are established in the broader AI field, their application to a niche, technically deep software library like PETSc presents unique challenges and opportunities. The integration with Discord to maintain a human-in-the-loop approach also adds a practical dimension to the research. The concept of "wet knowledge base" and its relation to the LLM system is also an interesting framing.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work stems from the potential to improve the accessibility and usability of complex scientific software.  PETSc, like many such libraries, suffers from fragmented documentation and implicit knowledge held by a limited number of experts. By leveraging LLMs, the authors aim to democratize access to this knowledge and improve developer productivity. The potential to improve scientific discovery by aiding in literature reviews and suggesting algorithms, while ambitious, is a worthwhile goal.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Well-defined Problem:** The paper clearly identifies the challenges in leveraging existing knowledge within a scientific software project.
    *   **Practical Approach:** The authors focus on a concrete use case (PETSc) and implement a working system integrating RAG, reranking, and a Discord chatbot.
    *   **Evaluation:** The paper includes a preliminary evaluation demonstrating the benefits of the proposed techniques. The comparative analysis of RAG with and without reranking provides valuable insights.
    *   **Human-in-the-Loop:** The emphasis on maintaining developer oversight and avoiding full automation is crucial for ensuring the quality and trustworthiness of the AI-assisted responses.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of Evaluation:** The evaluation is preliminary and based on a relatively small set of questions. A more comprehensive evaluation with a larger user base and a wider range of tasks would be needed to fully assess the effectiveness of the system.
    *   **Dependency on Proprietary Systems:** The heavy reliance on proprietary LLMs (OpenAI's GPT-4o) raises concerns about long-term sustainability and potential vendor lock-in. The paper acknowledges this but does not present alternative strategies.
    *   **Lack of Comparative Baselines:** While the paper compares RAG and reranking, it lacks a comparison against traditional methods of information retrieval and knowledge management within PETSc (e.g., improved search functionality or curated FAQs). This makes it difficult to assess the relative improvement offered by the LLM-based approach.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Costs:**  The paper does not adequately address the computational costs associated with running LLMs, RAG, and reranking pipelines, which could be a significant barrier to adoption.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the way scientific software libraries are developed, maintained, and used. It could inspire other projects to adopt similar LLM-based approaches for knowledge management and user support. However, the limited scope of the evaluation and the reliance on proprietary systems limit its immediate impact.

*   **Justification for Score:** While the application of LLMs to PETSc is novel and significant, the paper is weakened by its preliminary nature, limited evaluation, and reliance on proprietary technologies. The lack of a comparative baseline also hinders a comprehensive assessment of its value.  Therefore, while the paper offers promising insights and a concrete implementation, its impact is currently limited.
