### Multiple Streams of Relation Extraction: Enriching and Recalling in Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20746v1)
- **Authors**: Todd Nief, David Reber, Sean Richardson, Ari Holtzman
- **Abstract**: When an LLM learns a relation during finetuning (e.g., new movie releases, corporate mergers, etc.), where does this information go? Is it extracted when the model processes an entity, recalled just-in-time before a prediction, or are there multiple separate heuristics? Existing localization approaches (e.g. activation patching) are ill-suited for this analysis because they tend to replace parts of the residual stream, potentially deleting information. To fill this gap, we propose dynamic weight-grafting between fine-tuned and pre-trained language models to show that fine-tuned language models both (1) extract relation information learned during finetuning while processing entities and (2) ``recall" this information in later layers while generating predictions. In some cases, models need both of these pathways to correctly generate finetuned information while, in other cases, a single ``enrichment" or ``recall" pathway alone is sufficient. We examine the necessity and sufficiency of these information pathways, examining what layers they occur at, how much redundancy they exhibit, and which model components are involved -- finding that the ``recall" pathway occurs via both task-specific attention mechanisms and a relation extraction step in the output of the attention and the feedforward networks at the final layers before next token prediction.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary**

The paper investigates how Large Language Models (LLMs) encode and recall new relational information after fine-tuning. The authors introduce a novel technique called "dynamic weight grafting," which allows selective replacement of weights between a fine-tuned model and its pre-trained counterpart during text generation. This method is designed to overcome limitations of activation patching and ablation studies, which can inadvertently disrupt upstream computations. The key finding is that LLMs use two distinct pathways for relation extraction: 1) an "enrichment" pathway where entity representations are modified early in the sequence processing, and 2) a "recall" pathway, active closer to the prediction phase, that can extract relation information even from unenriched entities. The authors further localize the "recall" pathway to task-specific attention mechanisms and specific components of the feedforward networks in the final layers before token prediction.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty and Significance:** The paper introduces a valuable new methodology – dynamic weight grafting – which represents a clear advance over standard activation patching and ablation techniques. The two-pathway finding regarding enrichment and recall is also a significant contribution, shedding light on the internal mechanisms of LLMs when learning new relational information. The localization of the recall mechanism to specific attention and feedforward components further enhances the understanding. This could potentially lead to more efficient fine-tuning strategies or targeted editing of LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The dynamic weight grafting approach is a sound methodological contribution. It allows for more nuanced causal analysis than previous methods.
    *   The identification of the two pathways provides a compelling narrative about the mechanisms LLMs use.
    *   The paper is well-written and logically structured.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The data used (synthetic relations) limit the generalizability of the findings. Relations in real-world text are much more complex and nuanced, and the mechanisms used might differ.
    *   The models used are on the smaller side, raising concerns if the observed behaviour is relevant to contemporary, more powerful models.
    *   The analysis is primarily focused on a few specific model architectures. While this provides in-depth insights, it reduces the generalizability to the vast landscape of different LLM architectures. Further, they only examine the top layers, and it may be useful to check other layers if possible.
    *   While the paper identifies where these pathways operate, it does not fully explain *how* they work. Mechanistically understanding the learned parameters would help.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has strong potential for influencing future research in LLM interpretability. The dynamic weight grafting technique is applicable to other tasks, and the two-pathway discovery opens up new avenues for exploring how LLMs encode and use knowledge. The findings regarding the role of specific attention and feedforward components could inform the design of more efficient and controllable LLMs.

*   **Conclusion:** While the reliance on synthetic data and analysis of mostly smaller architectures limits the scope somewhat, the novel methodology and the interesting finding of dual-pathway relation extraction make this a valuable contribution to the field. The paper paves the way for more detailed investigations of LLM mechanisms.

Score: 7.5
- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Towards Probabilistic Question Answering Over Tabular Data
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20747v1)
- **Authors**: Chen Shen, Sajjadur Rahman, Estevam Hruschka
- **Abstract**: Current approaches for question answering (QA) over tabular data, such as NL2SQL systems, perform well for factual questions where answers are directly retrieved from tables. However, they fall short on probabilistic questions requiring reasoning under uncertainty. In this paper, we introduce a new benchmark LUCARIO and a framework for probabilistic QA over large tabular data. Our method induces Bayesian Networks from tables, translates natural language queries into probabilistic queries, and uses large language models (LLMs) to generate final answers. Empirical results demonstrate significant improvements over baselines, highlighting the benefits of hybrid symbolic-neural reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces LUCARIO, a new benchmark and framework for probabilistic question answering (QA) over tabular data.  It addresses the limitations of existing NL2SQL systems which struggle with questions requiring reasoning under uncertainty, latent correlations, incomplete evidence, or conditional patterns. LUCARIO includes both real-world and synthetic datasets and covers a wide spectrum of probabilistic reasoning patterns. The authors propose a novel framework that automatically constructs a Bayesian Network from tabular data (regardless of its size), translates natural language queries into probabilistic queries, and leverages large language models (LLMs) for final answer generation.  The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of their hybrid symbolic-neural approach in extensive experiments, showing improvements over LLM-only and retrieval-based baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The core novelty lies in the combination of several elements: (1) the creation of a probabilistic QA benchmark, LUCARIO, that scales to large, real-world datasets where existing datasets are usually smaller and/or synthetic, (2) the automatic construction of Bayesian Networks from large tabular datasets, a process that is traditionally manual, (3)  a neuro-symbolic framework that tightly integrates probabilistic inference in BNs with LLMs for both query translation and answer generation. While individual components like BNs and LLMs are not novel, the combination within this specific context of probabilistic tabular QA is innovative.

*   **Significance:**  The paper addresses a significant gap in the field of QA over tabular data. While NL2SQL systems excel at factual retrieval, they fall short when dealing with uncertainty. LUCARIO provides a crucial benchmark for evaluating systems that can reason probabilistically. The proposed framework, Auto-BN, demonstrates a promising approach, combining the strengths of symbolic reasoning (BNs) and neural language models (LLMs). If widely adopted, LUCARIO could become a standard evaluation resource, and the Auto-BN framework could inspire new research directions in hybrid QA systems. The emphasis on scalability through automated BN construction is important for real-world applicability.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Benchmark Contribution:** LUCARIO fills a critical need for a comprehensive, scalable benchmark for probabilistic tabular QA. The inclusion of diverse query types and the detailed statistics provided are commendable.
    *   **Framework Design:** The Auto-BN framework is well-motivated and presents a logical approach to combining symbolic and neural reasoning.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The extensive experiments and detailed analysis of the results are thorough and convincing.  The comparison to strong baselines highlights the advantages of the proposed method. The ablation studies (effect of premises, insights) provide valuable insights into system behavior.
    *  **Handling Lexical Leakage:** The techniques described to mitigate lexical leakage within the model are an important part of the experimental rigour

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on LLM Quality:** The performance of Auto-BN is still reliant on the LLM's ability to accurately translate natural language queries into structured probabilistic queries. While the results are strong, potential errors in this translation step introduce a point of failure, making the end-to-end system sensitive to the LLM's capabilities.
    *   **Limited Causal Reasoning Depth:** While Bayesian Networks can represent causal relationships, the depth of reasoning they support is inherently limited by the network structure. More complex causal inference scenarios might require more sophisticated techniques.
    *   **Evaluation Biases:** The strong performance of AUTO-BN compared to NL2SQL is somewhat unsurprising. NL2SQL is a retrieval method that does not handle inherent probabilities very well. This also speaks more to the use case rather than a clear advantage of AUTO-BN over NL2SQL in other standard metrics of the latter.

*   **Potential Influence:** LUCARIO has a strong potential to influence the field. It sets a clear target for future research, providing a standard benchmark and a promising initial framework for probabilistic tabular QA.  The paper can motivate researchers to explore new hybrid approaches that combine symbolic reasoning and neural language models.

**Overall:**

This is a solid paper that addresses a critical gap in tabular QA and provides a benchmark and an approach for tackling the challenge of probabilistic reasoning. Although the method depends to some degree on an LLM, the combination of Bayesian Networks and LLMs in this setting is sufficiently novel and impactful. Given the limitations of prior work and this paper's substantial contributions, a score of **7** is warranted. The novelty is good, the significance is strong, and the paper addresses an important problem. Further improvements in overcoming LLM reliance and scaling the causal models might warrant an even higher score in the future.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Characterization and Mitigation of Training Instabilities in Microscaling Formats
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20752v1)
- **Authors**: Huangyuan Su, Mujin Kwun, Stephanie Gil, Sham Kakade, Nikhil Anand
- **Abstract**: Training large language models is an expensive, compute-bound process that must be repeated as models scale, algorithms improve, and new data is collected. To address this, next-generation hardware accelerators increasingly support lower-precision arithmetic formats, such as the Microscaling (MX) formats introduced in NVIDIA's Blackwell architecture. These formats use a shared scale within blocks of parameters to extend representable range and perform forward/backward GEMM operations in reduced precision for efficiency gains. In this work, we investigate the challenges and viability of block-scaled precision formats during model training. Across nearly one thousand language models trained from scratch -- spanning compute budgets from $2 \times 10^{17}$ to $4.8 \times 10^{19}$ FLOPs and sweeping over a broad range of weight-activation precision combinations -- we consistently observe that training in MX formats exhibits sharp, stochastic instabilities in the loss, particularly at larger compute scales. To explain this phenomenon, we conduct controlled experiments and ablations on a smaller proxy model that exhibits similar behavior as the language model, sweeping across architectural settings, hyperparameters, and precision formats. These experiments motivate a simple model in which multiplicative gradient bias introduced by the quantization of layer-norm affine parameters and a small fraction of activations can trigger runaway divergence. Through \emph{in situ} intervention experiments on our proxy model, we demonstrate that instabilities can be averted or delayed by modifying precision schemes mid-training. Guided by these findings, we evaluate stabilization strategies in the LLM setting and show that certain hybrid configurations recover performance competitive with full-precision training. We release our code at https://github.com/Hither1/systems-scaling.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the challenges and viability of using block-scaled precision formats (MX formats), specifically MXFP8 and MXFP6, for training large language models (LLMs).  These formats are supported by newer hardware like NVIDIA's Blackwell architecture and offer potential efficiency gains due to lower-precision arithmetic. The authors empirically observed that training LLMs in MX formats exhibits instabilities, manifesting as loss spikes, especially at larger compute scales.  To understand these instabilities, they constructed a smaller, controlled student-teacher MLP proxy model, demonstrating two main failure modes: those stemming from stochastic optimization dynamics, and instabilities *induced* by quantization noise. They pinpoint the quantization of layer normalization affine parameters as a key driver of the quantization-induced instabilities.  The paper proposes and evaluates mitigation strategies, such as using higher precision activations or performing quantization only in the forward pass. These strategies enable the recovery of valid empirical scaling laws.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper makes a significant contribution by deeply characterizing instabilities during low-precision LLM training, especially in the context of newer, block-scaled formats like MX. While previous work has noted instabilities in low-precision training (e.g., with FP8), the paper goes further by: (1) identifying the specific role of layer normalization affine parameter quantization in MX format instability, (2) developing a controlled proxy model to systematically investigate the issue, (3) and proposing concrete mitigation strategies that allow training to proceed stably and achieve competitive performance. The combination of large-scale LLM experiments, a controlled proxy model, and actionable mitigation strategies is a strong point.  The isolation of the layer norm quantization effect and linking it to skewed distributions is a novel mechanistic explanation.

*   **Significance:**  The work has direct relevance to the development of next-generation AI hardware and software. MX formats are designed to improve the efficiency of LLM training, and this paper provides valuable insights into the challenges of using them effectively. The proposed mitigation strategies have the potential to accelerate LLM training on new hardware, enabling faster experimentation and deployment. Furthermore, the identification of a bias due to clustering of layer norm parameters has the potential to extend to other quantization problems.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly identifies a practical problem: instabilities in low-precision training using MX formats.
    *   **Comprehensive empirical analysis:**  The paper includes both large-scale LLM experiments and smaller, controlled experiments with a proxy model. This allows for a more nuanced understanding of the underlying issues.
    *   **Mechanistic explanation:**  The identification of layer norm quantization as a key source of instability provides a clear explanation of the observed phenomenon.
    *   **Actionable mitigation strategies:** The paper proposes and evaluates practical mitigation strategies that can be implemented in real-world LLM training pipelines.
    *   **Code Release:** The authors release their code, which allows for reproducibility and further research in this area.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Proxy Model Simplifications:** The proxy model, while useful for isolation, inevitably simplifies aspects of real LLM training. It is still a proxy after all. While the observed behaviors of LLMs were also observable in the proxy model, one potential weakness could be regarding any potential *interactions* between components in LLMs vs in the proxy, as the proxy is significantly simplified.
    *   **Limited Scale of LLM Experiments:**  The LLM experiments, while substantial, are conducted on models up to ~1.7B parameters. While showing stabilization effects, it would be valuable to see if the mitigation techniques extrapolate well to larger models (e.g., 100B+ parameters).  The authors acknowledge this limitation.
    *   **Scope of Mitigations:** The explored mitigation strategies are somewhat limited. It is possible that more sophisticated quantization schemes or adaptive precision techniques could further improve stability and performance.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the design of future LLM training pipelines, especially those targeting hardware accelerators with support for MX formats. It provides valuable guidance on how to avoid instabilities and achieve competitive performance using these lower-precision formats. The results are useful to other researchers in the field, and the paper will likely be cited by those working on quantization and efficient LLM training.

*   **Justification for Score:** The paper's novelty, significance, empirical rigor, and actionable insights justify a score of 8. The identification of a key instability mechanism and successful mitigation strategies represent a valuable contribution to the field. The weaknesses regarding limited scale are acknowledged and don't detract significantly from the overall impact, but prevent this from being rated a top-tier paper.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### StereoDiff: Stereo-Diffusion Synergy for Video Depth Estimation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20756v1)
- **Authors**: Haodong Li, Chen Wang, Jiahui Lei, Kostas Daniilidis, Lingjie Liu
- **Abstract**: Recent video depth estimation methods achieve great performance by following the paradigm of image depth estimation, i.e., typically fine-tuning pre-trained video diffusion models with massive data. However, we argue that video depth estimation is not a naive extension of image depth estimation. The temporal consistency requirements for dynamic and static regions in videos are fundamentally different. Consistent video depth in static regions, typically backgrounds, can be more effectively achieved via stereo matching across all frames, which provides much stronger global 3D cues. While the consistency for dynamic regions still should be learned from large-scale video depth data to ensure smooth transitions, due to the violation of triangulation constraints. Based on these insights, we introduce StereoDiff, a two-stage video depth estimator that synergizes stereo matching for mainly the static areas with video depth diffusion for maintaining consistent depth transitions in dynamic areas. We mathematically demonstrate how stereo matching and video depth diffusion offer complementary strengths through frequency domain analysis, highlighting the effectiveness of their synergy in capturing the advantages of both. Experimental results on zero-shot, real-world, dynamic video depth benchmarks, both indoor and outdoor, demonstrate StereoDiff's SoTA performance, showcasing its superior consistency and accuracy in video depth estimation.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and rigorous evaluation of the "StereoDiff: Stereo-Diffusion Synergy for Video Depth Estimation" paper.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces StereoDiff, a novel two-stage video depth estimation approach. It combines stereo matching for accurate global consistency, especially in static regions like backgrounds, with video depth diffusion models for smooth local consistency in dynamic areas. The authors argue that static and dynamic areas in videos need different treatment for consistency, using stereo matching for global 3D cues and learned diffusion models for smooth temporal transitions. The paper demonstrates through frequency domain analysis and experiments on zero-shot real-world benchmarks that StereoDiff effectively synergizes the strengths of both techniques, achieving state-of-the-art performance in video depth estimation. StereoDiff processes video frames in pairs to establish strong global consistency. In a second stage, a video depth diffusion model is applied to enhance local consistency.

**Rigorous and Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining stereo matching with a diffusion model for video depth estimation has merit. The key novelty lies in the explicit recognition and separate handling of global vs. local consistency requirements. This insight is not entirely groundbreaking, but its concrete implementation as a two-stage pipeline and the supporting frequency analysis provide a valuable contribution. While prior work has used SfM or Diffusion models independently, the explicit fusion of these ideas in this manner has not been previously explored in the literature. Prior work leverages sfm for motions without rigid assumptions and then uses optimization approaches that are resource intensive, so this approach avoids the need for this expensive computational step. This allows the authors to achieve accurate and robust results given monocular videos in an inference based manner, even with motions.
*   **Significance:** Achieving consistent and accurate video depth estimation is crucial for various applications, including AR/VR, robotics, and autonomous driving. StereoDiff's ability to deliver state-of-the-art performance on zero-shot benchmarks is significant. The paper also claims to be training-free and does not require test-time optimization, improving efficiency. However, it needs to be evaluated on more diverse and challenging real-world datasets to demonstrate generalizability truly.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of video depth estimation and motivates the need for separate handling of static and dynamic regions.
    *   **Methodological Soundness:** The two-stage approach is logically structured, and the frequency domain analysis provides theoretical justification for the synergy between stereo matching and diffusion.
    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experiments on zero-shot benchmarks show that StereoDiff outperforms existing methods across several metrics.
    *   **Efficiency:** The claim of faster inference than existing approaches.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dependency on Pre-trained Models:** StereoDiff relies on a pre-trained SVD model (DepthCrafter) and a stereo matching network (MonST3R). While this enables zero-shot performance, it limits the method's flexibility and potential for further improvement. In other words, its performance is tightly bounded to MonST3R's ability to assign zero confidence when motion is detected.
    *   **Limited Discussion of Failure Cases:** While the paper presents promising results, it lacks a thorough discussion of failure cases, such as scenes with significant occlusions, textureless regions, or complex lighting conditions. A critical analysis of these limitations is necessary to understand the method's robustness.
    *   **Lack of ablation:** Lack of individual loss functions used.

*   **Potential Impact:** The work could inspire further research on combining analytical (stereo) and learned (diffusion) approaches for video understanding tasks. The explicit handling of global and local consistency could be a valuable principle for other video processing applications. While the paper relies on pre-trained models, it provides a solid foundation for future research that could integrate end-to-end training or explore more sophisticated fusion strategies.

Overall, the paper presents a well-motivated and technically sound approach to video depth estimation, achieving state-of-the-art results on several benchmarks.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper earns a 7 for its valuable contribution to the field, but is penalized for the fact that performance is largely dependent upon pre-trained models and an SfM architecture. The insight of separately managing global/local consistency is valuable but not exceptionally novel. The reliance on existing architectures limits its potential. Further, limited ablation and failure case analysis prevents the paper from being scored higher. The significance of achieving better results on zero-shot benchmarks strengthens the positive attributes.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Stochastic and Non-local Closure Modeling for Nonlinear Dynamical Systems via Latent Score-based Generative Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20771v1)
- **Authors**: Xinghao Dong, Huchen Yang, Jin-Long Wu
- **Abstract**: We propose a latent score-based generative AI framework for learning stochastic, non-local closure models and constitutive laws in nonlinear dynamical systems of computational mechanics. This work addresses a key challenge of modeling complex multiscale dynamical systems without a clear scale separation, for which numerically resolving all scales is prohibitively expensive, e.g., for engineering turbulent flows. While classical closure modeling methods leverage domain knowledge to approximate subgrid-scale phenomena, their deterministic and local assumptions can be too restrictive in regimes lacking a clear scale separation. Recent developments of diffusion-based stochastic models have shown promise in the context of closure modeling, but their prohibitive computational inference cost limits practical applications for many real-world applications. This work addresses this limitation by jointly training convolutional autoencoders with conditional diffusion models in the latent spaces, significantly reducing the dimensionality of the sampling process while preserving essential physical characteristics. Numerical results demonstrate that the joint training approach helps discover a proper latent space that not only guarantees small reconstruction errors but also ensures good performance of the diffusion model in the latent space. When integrated into numerical simulations, the proposed stochastic modeling framework via latent conditional diffusion models achieves significant computational acceleration while maintaining comparable predictive accuracy to standard diffusion models in physical spaces.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a latent score-based generative AI framework for developing stochastic, non-local closure models for nonlinear dynamical systems in computational mechanics. This framework aims to address the challenge of modeling complex multiscale systems without clear scale separation.  The key idea is to use convolutional autoencoders to compress high-dimensional fields (state variables and closure terms) into lower-dimensional latent spaces. A conditional diffusion model is then trained within this latent space to learn the conditional probability distribution of the closure term given the corresponding state. This approach aims to significantly reduce computational cost compared to training diffusion models directly in physical space while maintaining predictive accuracy.  The paper proposes a joint training scheme for the autoencoder and diffusion model to better optimize the latent space for the generative process, and demonstrates its efficacy through numerical simulations of a 2D Kolmogorov flow. The method shows promise for enabling faster ensemble simulations and uncertainty quantification.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper builds upon existing work on diffusion-based closure modeling and latent diffusion models, but introduces a specific *joint training* approach to adapt the latent space for closure modeling. The key novelty lies in the recognition that autoencoders trained solely for reconstruction might produce latent spaces unsuitable for effective diffusion modeling in the context of closure modeling.  The end-to-end training scheme specifically addresses this issue. While latent diffusion models are well-established, their specific application and the tailored training process for closure modeling represents a significant contribution.

*   **Significance:** Addressing the computational cost of diffusion-based closure modeling is crucial for making these methods practical for real-world applications. The demonstrated speedup of the proposed method in ensemble simulations (around 10x compared to a physical-space diffusion model) is significant. Moreover, the approach is able to maintain prediction accuracy compared to diffusion models trained directly in physical spaces. This could have a meaningful impact on fields like turbulent flow modeling and weather forecasting, where computational limitations often restrict the complexity and scope of simulations. The ability to perform practical uncertainty quantification, which is otherwise computationally prohibitive, increases the significance. The systematic analysis of the energy spectrum further strengthens the credibility and utility of the method. The presentation clarity and systematic study further contribute to the value of this paper.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clearly articulates the problem and its significance.
    *   Provides a well-motivated and technically sound solution.
    *   Demonstrates the effectiveness of the approach through numerical experiments on a relevant benchmark problem (2D Kolmogorov flow).
    *   Includes a thorough comparison against existing methods.
    *   Comprehensive experimental and implementation details for reproducibility.
    *   Analysis of energy spectrum for comparison.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The evaluation is limited to a single, relatively simple test case (2D Kolmogorov Flow).  While this is a common benchmark, the generalizability of the approach to more complex, higher-dimensional, and different types of systems (e.g., 3D turbulence, reactive flows, multiphysics systems) is not fully established.
    *   The joint training strategy, while effective, might require careful tuning of hyperparameters (as acknowledged in the paper).  The sensitivity of the method to these hyperparameters and the robustness of the training process could be further explored.
    *   The complexity of autoencoders could restrict their effectiveness in extremely complex systems.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more computationally efficient and accurate closure models for complex dynamical systems.  The joint training strategy for latent diffusion models could be adopted and extended by other researchers in this area. The framework provides a potential avenue for incorporating physics-informed constraints within the latent representation. Its significance lies in potentially democratizing access to uncertainty quantification for computationally demanding applications.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a novel and well-executed approach to addressing a significant challenge in closure modeling. While the evaluation is somewhat limited in scope, the results are compelling and demonstrate the potential of the proposed method. The clear articulation of the problem, the sound technical solution, and the thorough experimental evaluation justify a high score. The joint training aspect is a clear contribution beyond a simple application of LDM, warranting the assignment of a respectable score of 8.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### Multi-lingual Functional Evaluation for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20793v1)
- **Authors**: Victor Ojewale, Inioluwa Deborah Raji, Suresh Venkatasubramanian
- **Abstract**: Multi-lingual competence in large language models is often evaluated via static data benchmarks such as Belebele, M-MMLU and M-GSM. However, these evaluations often fail to provide an adequate understanding of the practical performance and robustness of models across multi-lingual settings. In response, we create multi-lingual functional benchmarks -- Cross-Lingual Grade School Math Symbolic (CL-GSM Symbolic) and Cross-Lingual Instruction-Following Eval (CL-IFEval)-- by translating existing functional benchmark templates from English to five additional languages that span the range of resources available for NLP: French, Spanish, Hindi, Arabic and Yoruba. Our results reveal that some static multi-lingual benchmarks capture functional performance much more closely than others (i.e. across models, there is a 24%, 17% and 18% decrease in performance between M-GSM and CL-GSM Symbolic in English, French and Spanish respectively; similarly there's a 15 - 24% performance drop across languages between Belebele and CL-IFEval, and only a 0.5% to 3% performance drop between M-MMLU and CL-IFEval). Similarly, we find that model robustness across languages varies significantly, with certain languages (eg. Arabic, English) being the most consistently well performing across evaluation iterations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the limitations of using static, multi-lingual benchmarks for evaluating the functional performance of large language models (LLMs).  The authors argue that static benchmarks (like M-MMLU and M-GSM) don't adequately capture the robustness of models across different languages in real-world scenarios. To address this, they create multi-lingual functional benchmarks: Cross-Lingual Grade School Math Symbolic (CL-GSM Symbolic) and Cross-Lingual Instruction-Following Eval (CL-IFEval).  They translated existing English functional benchmark templates into five additional languages (French, Spanish, Hindi, Arabic, and Yoruba). Their experiments compare performance on these newly created benchmarks with the existing static benchmarks, revealing inconsistencies in model performance across languages and highlighting the importance of functional evaluation. They find that models often perform better on static benchmarks than functional ones, with significant variations across languages and models. The authors also analyze robustness at the instruction and template level within their new benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in the *creation and release* of the CL-GSM Symbolic and CL-IFEval datasets. While translating existing benchmarks isn't a groundbreaking methodology *in itself*, the systematic application to these specific "functional" benchmark templates and the subsequent analysis is valuable.  It's an important step towards more realistic multi-lingual LLM evaluation.  The instruction and template-level analysis adds further depth, going beyond simple aggregate performance scores. However, similar efforts have been made by others (mentioned as concurrent work in the paper).

*   **Significance:** The paper has significant implications for how we assess multi-lingual LLMs.  It effectively demonstrates that static benchmarks can be misleading and that functional evaluation reveals critical performance gaps and inconsistencies. By releasing their translated benchmarks, the authors enable further research and more robust evaluation of LLMs. The finding that robustness varies across languages is particularly important for practitioners deploying LLMs in diverse linguistic contexts. That said, the scope is relatively narrow, focusing on only two functional tasks. The reliance on automated translation (Google Translate) also presents a weakness, potentially introducing inaccuracies that could skew the results, especially for lower-resource languages, even though the authors acknowledge this limitation. The translation inaccuracies may limit the reliability of the findings.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing evaluation methods.
    *   **Dataset Creation:**  The creation and public release of translated functional benchmarks are a valuable contribution to the community.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:**  The paper analyzes a range of open-source LLMs and includes analysis at different levels of granularity.
    *   **Discussion of Limitations:** The authors honestly acknowledge the limitations of their approach, particularly regarding automated translation.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Translation Quality:** The use of Google Translate is a significant weakness, especially for lower-resource languages. Manual review and potentially back-translation would significantly improve the reliability of the findings.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The evaluation is limited to two English functional benchmarks, which restricts the generalizability of the conclusions.
    *   **Lack of Theoretical Framework:** The paper is primarily empirical. It lacks a deeper theoretical exploration of why these performance gaps occur (e.g., relating it to specific linguistic features or model architectures).

*   **Impact:**  The paper has the potential to influence future research on multi-lingual LLM evaluation by highlighting the need for functional benchmarks. The released datasets will likely be used by other researchers, driving progress in the field. The insights into robustness inconsistencies across languages have practical implications for deploying LLMs in diverse settings. However, because of the limitations listed above (especially the quality of the translations), the impact might be somewhat lessened.

**Score:** 6

**Rationale:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by creating and releasing valuable multi-lingual functional benchmarks and demonstrating the inadequacy of reliance solely on static benchmarks. The instruction and template-level analysis also add depth. However, the use of Google Translate as the primary translation method is a significant weakness that limits the robustness of the findings and diminishes the novelty. Additionally, the relatively narrow scope (two base benchmarks) restricts the generalizability of the results. A more rigorous translation process and a broader set of tasks would have significantly increased the score. While not groundbreaking, the paper represents a worthwhile contribution to the field of multi-lingual LLM evaluation.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### The Ideation-Execution Gap: Execution Outcomes of LLM-Generated versus Human Research Ideas
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20803v1)
- **Authors**: Chenglei Si, Tatsunori Hashimoto, Diyi Yang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown promise in accelerating the scientific research pipeline. A key capability for this process is the ability to generate novel research ideas, and prior studies have found settings in which LLM-generated research ideas were judged as more novel than human-expert ideas. However, a good idea should not simply appear to be novel, it should also result in better research after being executed. To test whether AI-generated ideas lead to better research outcomes, we conduct an execution study by recruiting 43 expert researchers to execute randomly-assigned ideas, either written by experts or generated by an LLM. Each expert spent over 100 hours implementing the idea and wrote a 4-page short paper to document the experiments. All the executed projects are then reviewed blindly by expert NLP researchers. Comparing the review scores of the same ideas before and after execution, the scores of the LLM-generated ideas decrease significantly more than expert-written ideas on all evaluation metrics (novelty, excitement, effectiveness, and overall; p < 0.05), closing the gap between LLM and human ideas observed at the ideation stage. When comparing the aggregated review scores from the execution study, we even observe that for many metrics there is a flip in rankings where human ideas score higher than LLM ideas. This ideation-execution gap highlights the limitations of current LLMs in generating truly effective research ideas and the challenge of evaluating research ideas in the absence of execution outcomes.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a concise summary of the paper, followed by a critical evaluation of its novelty and significance, including a justified score.

**Summary:**

The paper, titled "The Ideation-Execution Gap: Execution Outcomes of LLM-Generated versus Human Research Ideas," investigates whether research ideas generated by Large Language Models (LLMs) translate into comparable research outcomes when actually executed, compared to ideas originating from human experts. The authors conducted an execution study where 43 expert researchers spent over 100 hours each executing randomly assigned research ideas, some generated by LLMs and others written by human experts. The resulting executed projects were then reviewed by other expert NLP researchers. The study compared review scores of the ideas before and after execution, focusing on metrics like novelty, excitement, effectiveness, and overall quality. The key finding is that while LLM-generated ideas may initially be judged as more novel, their scores decrease significantly more after execution compared to human-generated ideas.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty of the Question:** The paper tackles a very important and relevant question. While LLMs have demonstrated promise in many areas, their ability to truly drive scientific discovery hinges on the quality and utility of the *ideas* they generate. It is crucial to go beyond simply judging the apparent novelty of an idea and assess its real-world potential.  The approach of rigorously testing AI-generated ideas through execution is a strong contribution.
*   **Significance and Impact:** The ideation-execution gap uncovered by the paper is significant. Previous work has primarily focused on the ideation stage, often using LLM judges or limited human evaluations. This paper reveals the limitations of current LLMs in generating truly effective research ideas and highlights the challenge of evaluating research ideas *in the absence of execution outcomes*. This has implications for the design of AI-driven research pipelines and the interpretation of prior work. This discovery could affect future studies of LLM creativity and innovation.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** The execution study is well-designed with a reasonable sample size (N=43) and controls (blinding, randomization) to enable a fair comparison. The expert review process adds credibility.
    *   **Real-world Execution:**  Having experts spend a substantial amount of time (100+ hours) executing the ideas makes the findings much more meaningful than purely theoretical analyses.
    *   **Clear and Compelling Results:** The finding that LLM-generated ideas suffer a more significant drop in scores after execution is statistically significant and provides valuable insight.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Scope of Ideas:**  The authors acknowledge the limitation of focusing on a specific type of research idea (novel prompting techniques in NLP).  While this constraint was necessary for feasibility, it might limit the generalizability of the results to other domains or types of research questions.  This is a valid limitation that is addressed in the conclusion.
    *   **Potential for Executor Bias:** Even with blinding, some executors might implicitly recognize AI-generated ideas based on characteristic styles or feasibility issues, introducing subtle bias. The detailed analysis of the changes made to ideas during execution does alleviate this concern though.
    *   **Reliance on Expert Reviewers:** As the study focuses on NLP, the reviewers are all NLP experts, and their scores might differ from experts in other fields. The reliance on subjective ratings introduces inherent limitations.
    *   **Modest Sample Size:**  While N=43 is respectable for an execution study, increasing this would strengthen the statistical power and address executor variability.
*   **Overall:** This is a well-executed study that raises an important point about current LLMs.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper provides significant insights into the limitations of current LLMs and the challenges of evaluating AI-generated research ideas. The study's rigorous methodology and real-world execution strengthen its findings. However, the narrow scope of research ideas and other potential biases slightly limit the generalizability of the results. Overall, though, the study contributes significant value.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Poster: Enhancing GNN Robustness for Network Intrusion Detection via Agent-based Analysis
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20806v1)
- **Authors**: Zhonghao Zhan, Huichi Zhou, Hamed Haddadi
- **Abstract**: Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) show great promise for Network Intrusion Detection Systems (NIDS), particularly in IoT environments, but suffer performance degradation due to distribution drift and lack robustness against realistic adversarial attacks. Current robustness evaluations often rely on unrealistic synthetic perturbations and lack demonstrations on systematic analysis of different kinds of adversarial attack, which encompass both black-box and white-box scenarios. This work proposes a novel approach to enhance GNN robustness and generalization by employing Large Language Models (LLMs) in an agentic pipeline as simulated cybersecurity expert agents. These agents scrutinize graph structures derived from network flow data, identifying and potentially mitigating suspicious or adversarially perturbed elements before GNN processing. Our experiments, using a framework designed for realistic evaluation and testing with a variety of adversarial attacks including a dataset collected from physical testbed experiments, demonstrate that integrating LLM analysis can significantly improve the resilience of GNN-based NIDS against challenges, showcasing the potential of LLM agent as a complementary layer in intrusion detection architectures.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the poster paper:

**Summary:**

The poster proposes enhancing the robustness of Graph Neural Network (GNN)-based Network Intrusion Detection Systems (NIDS) by integrating Large Language Models (LLMs) as cybersecurity expert agents. These LLM agents analyze network flow graph structures, identifying and potentially mitigating suspicious or adversarially perturbed elements before they reach the GNN. The paper addresses two main issues: 1) the performance degradation of GNNs under distribution drift (using a unified dataset from multiple sources) and 2) the lack of robustness against realistic adversarial attacks (simulating node injection attacks).  Experimental results suggest that LLM analysis can improve GNN resilience against simulated attacks, showing promise for a more robust NIDS deployment.  The paper tests various LLMs (including GPT-40 and LLaMA) for their ability to identify injected malicious nodes, demonstrating significant improvements in GNN classification accuracy when LLM-based mitigation is applied.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a Real Problem:** The paper tackles a critical and relevant issue in GNN-based NIDS: the vulnerability to distribution drift and realistic adversarial attacks.  Many existing evaluations rely on unrealistic assumptions.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The idea of using LLMs as expert agents to pre-process network graph data for GNN-based NIDS is a novel and potentially effective approach. It leverages the reasoning capabilities of LLMs to enhance GNN robustness.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** The paper provides experimental results demonstrating the effectiveness of LLM-based mitigation against node injection attacks. The use of a unified dataset to simulate distribution drift is also a strong point.
    *   **Well-Defined Methodology:** The paper outlines a clear methodology for integrating LLMs into the NIDS pipeline, including task definition, prompting strategy, and workflow.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Attack Scenarios:** The evaluation primarily focuses on node injection attacks. While important, it doesn't cover the full spectrum of potential adversarial attacks in network intrusion detection.  The paper acknowledges this limitation but doesn't offer extensive justification for focusing *solely* on node injection.
    *   **Synthetic Attacks:** Even with the unified dataset, the attacks are still simulated. While node injection is more realistic than some synthetic methods, evaluation on real-world, captured network traffic with known attacks would significantly strengthen the findings.
    *   **Scalability and Cost:** The computational cost of LLM inference, especially for real-time NIDS, is a potential bottleneck.  The paper mentions optimizing efficiency but doesn't provide concrete details on how this will be achieved. Cost-effectiveness is important for a practical NIDS deployment.
    *   **LLM Prompt Engineering:**  The success of the LLM approach heavily relies on prompt engineering. The paper provides some information but lacks a detailed discussion of prompt design, optimization, and sensitivity analysis.  Different prompts or LLM parameter settings could yield vastly different results.  The prompts themselves are not included, hindering reproducibility.
    *   **Limited Comparison:** While the paper compares performance with and without LLM mitigation, there is no direct comparison to other state-of-the-art robustness techniques specifically tailored for GNNs in the NIDS context (e.g., adversarial training).
    *   **Overstated Claims:** The claims might be slightly overstated based on the limited experimental scope. For example, the statement regarding handling "novel, zero-day attacks" is speculative and not sufficiently supported by the experiments.
    *   **Lack of Statistical Significance:** No statistical significance measures (e.g., p-values) are provided for the performance improvements reported.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    The combination of LLMs and GNNs for enhanced NIDS robustness is a promising direction. It offers a novel way to address the limitations of traditional GNN-based approaches. The concept of LLM acting as a cybersecurity expert analyzing network traffic is interesting and has the potential to improve detection rates and reduce false positives.  The work is relevant as it tackles the issues of drift and robustness.

*   **Justification of Score:**

    The paper presents a novel idea with some supporting experimental results, but has limitations in terms of attack scenarios, realism, and cost analysis. The lack of comparison against other robust GNN methods and details surrounding prompt engineering are significant drawbacks.  While the experimental work is promising, it's not fully conclusive, and needs further work to demonstrate applicability in real-world settings.

**Score: 6**

*Reasoning for Score:*  The work shows promising initial results on an important problem with a novel approach, but lacks the depth and breadth of evaluation, comparison, and analysis necessary to justify a higher score.  The synthetic attacks and lack of real-world validation are the key detractors. There is some novelty, but its potential impact is limited by these methodological shortcomings. The idea itself is valuable, but the execution needs further refinement.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 6/10

### MultiFinRAG: An Optimized Multimodal Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) Framework for Financial Question Answering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20821v1)
- **Authors**: Chinmay Gondhalekar, Urjitkumar Patel, Fang-Chun Yeh
- **Abstract**: Financial documents--such as 10-Ks, 10-Qs, and investor presentations--span hundreds of pages and combine diverse modalities, including dense narrative text, structured tables, and complex figures. Answering questions over such content often requires joint reasoning across modalities, which strains traditional large language models (LLMs) and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) pipelines due to token limitations, layout loss, and fragmented cross-modal context. We introduce MultiFinRAG, a retrieval-augmented generation framework purpose-built for financial QA. MultiFinRAG first performs multimodal extraction by grouping table and figure images into batches and sending them to a lightweight, quantized open-source multimodal LLM, which produces both structured JSON outputs and concise textual summaries. These outputs, along with narrative text, are embedded and indexed with modality-aware similarity thresholds for precise retrieval. A tiered fallback strategy then dynamically escalates from text-only to text+table+image contexts when necessary, enabling cross-modal reasoning while reducing irrelevant context. Despite running on commodity hardware, MultiFinRAG achieves 19 percentage points higher accuracy than ChatGPT-4o (free-tier) on complex financial QA tasks involving text, tables, images, and combined multimodal reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MultiFinRAG, a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) framework optimized for financial question answering (QA) from multimodal documents like 10-Ks and investor presentations. MultiFinRAG addresses challenges of traditional RAG pipelines, like token limits, layout loss, and fragmented context. It incorporates batch multimodal extraction using a lightweight LLM to generate structured JSON and summaries from tables and figures. Modality-aware similarity thresholds are used for retrieval, and a tiered fallback strategy escalates from text-only to text+table+image contexts when needed. The authors demonstrate improved accuracy compared to ChatGPT-4o on complex financial QA tasks, even when running on commodity hardware.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The paper presents a practical and well-engineered system, but its novelty is incremental rather than groundbreaking.  While the specific combination of techniques is tailored for financial documents, the individual components (batch multimodal extraction, semantic chunking/merging, tiered retrieval) have precedents in the broader RAG literature. The application to financial data adds value given the domain's unique characteristics, but the core architectural choices are not entirely new. The novelty lies in the system's thoughtful integration of existing RAG components to address the specific needs of multimodal financial document QA.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a significant and practical problem: accurately answering questions from complex financial documents. The improved accuracy compared to a strong baseline (ChatGPT-4o) is a compelling result, demonstrating the value of a domain-specific, multimodal RAG approach. The use of open-source LLMs and commodity hardware makes the framework more accessible and practical. By improving financial QA, this system has potential impact on areas like risk monitoring, compliance, and investment decision-making.

**Strengths:**

*   **Strong Empirical Results:**  The paper provides clear and convincing evidence of MultiFinRAG's improved accuracy compared to a baseline, particularly on questions requiring multimodal reasoning.
*   **Practical Focus:** The framework is designed for real-world deployment, using open-source components and commodity hardware.
*   **Clear Architecture:** The paper provides a well-defined description of the system's architecture and components.
*   **Addressing a Real-World Problem:** Financial document QA is an important application area.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Incremental Novelty:** As mentioned earlier, the core ideas are evolutionary rather than revolutionary. The framework builds on existing techniques.
*   **Limited Ablation Studies:** While the overall performance is impressive, the paper lacks a detailed ablation study to quantify the impact of individual components (e.g., semantic chunking, tiered retrieval). It would be helpful to understand which specific components contribute most to the overall performance gain.
*   **Evaluation Metrics:** While focusing on accuracy is appropriate, including some information retrieval metrics (precision, recall) for the retrieval components would enhance the evaluation, especially since retrieval component is core to the RAG approach. The authors explicitly state these were collected and planned for a follow-up, but the exclusion weakens this paper.
*   **Efficiency Discussion:** The discussion of efficiency primarily focuses on inference cost reduction. It would also be beneficial to include a more detailed analysis of indexing time and the overall cost of building the knowledge base.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper can influence researchers and practitioners working on RAG systems, particularly those focused on multimodal document understanding and financial applications. It showcases the value of domain-specific RAG architectures and provides a practical example of how to integrate open-source LLMs for complex QA tasks.

**Justification of Score:**

Given the incremental novelty, solid empirical results, and practical significance, a score of **7** is appropriate. The paper presents a well-engineered system with demonstrable improvements over a baseline, but it builds on existing techniques and lacks a deep dive into individual component contributions.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Uncovering Hidden Violent Tendencies in LLMs: A Demographic Analysis via Behavioral Vignettes
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20822v1)
- **Authors**: Quintin Myers, Yanjun Gao
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly proposed for detecting and responding to violent content online, yet their ability to reason about morally ambiguous, real-world scenarios remains underexamined. We present the first study to evaluate LLMs using a validated social science instrument designed to measure human response to everyday conflict, namely the Violent Behavior Vignette Questionnaire (VBVQ). To assess potential bias, we introduce persona-based prompting that varies race, age, and geographic identity within the United States. Six LLMs developed across different geopolitical and organizational contexts are evaluated under a unified zero-shot setting. Our study reveals two key findings: (1) LLMs surface-level text generation often diverges from their internal preference for violent responses; (2) their violent tendencies vary across demographics, frequently contradicting established findings in criminology, social science, and psychology.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the potential biases in Large Language Models (LLMs) regarding violent tendencies by analyzing their responses to morally ambiguous, real-world conflict scenarios from the Violent Behavior Vignette Questionnaire (VBVQ). It evaluates six LLMs using persona-based prompts varying race, age, and geographic location within the U.S., observing both surface-level textual outputs and internal preference (likelihood) towards violent responses. The study reveals a divergence between LLMs' surface-level alignment and internal preferences, and demographic variations in their violent tendencies often contradict established criminological and social science findings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper is the first to use the VBVQ, a validated social science instrument, to evaluate LLMs' responses to conflict scenarios and assess potential bias.  Examining LLMs beyond simple fact retrieval or content generation, and probing their moral reasoning in nuanced, real-world situations, is a valuable direction. Using demographic prompts to unveil biases adds another layer of innovation.

*   **Significance:** The findings have implications for deploying LLMs in high-stakes domains like violence prevention. The revelation that LLMs' surface-level outputs often camouflage underlying biases raises serious concerns about their reliability and safety. The demographic variations, and especially the contradictions with existing social science research, highlight the importance of rigorous evaluation before deploying such models.
    The study also emphasizes the need to look beyond surface level textual outputs. The likelihood method reveals some deeper inclinations of the LLMs that were not apparent at first glance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Uses a well-established and validated social science instrument.
    *   Employs a robust methodology with persona-based prompting and analysis of both surface text and sequence likelihood.
    *   Evaluates LLMs from diverse organizational and geopolitical backgrounds.
    *   Identifies critical discrepancies between LLM behavior and criminological expectations.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited scope: While the US-based analysis is a strength for contextual understanding, it is limited to a specific culture and institution; extending this analysis requires some attention to the cultural and institutional differences.
    *   The LLMs used are not state-of-the-art.
    *   Small sample size of LLMs: Six LLMs, while a reasonable start, might not be fully representative of the broader LLM landscape.
    *   Potential for vignette interpretation bias: The interpretation of vignettes by LLMs may not perfectly align with human understanding, impacting the validity of the comparisons.
    *   The study does not attempt to mitigate the biases it uncovers, focusing primarily on detection.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The paper can inform future LLM development and alignment strategies to mitigate bias in sensitive domains.
    *   It can encourage the development of more robust evaluation methods for LLMs that go beyond standard benchmarks.
    *   It can raise awareness among policymakers and practitioners about the limitations of LLMs in applications involving moral reasoning and social justice.

**Justification for the Score:**

This paper represents a significant contribution to the evaluation of LLMs beyond traditional benchmarks. While the paper is novel in its approach and has important findings, the scope could be broadened to consider more LLMs and mitigate limitations, such as biases in the generation of response paraphrases. The identified bias and the contradictions with social science expectations are both surprising and valuable.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Efficacy of Temporal Fusion Transformers for Runoff Simulation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20831v1)
- **Authors**: Sinan Rasiya Koya, Tirthankar Roy
- **Abstract**: Combining attention with recurrence has shown to be valuable in sequence modeling, including hydrological predictions. Here, we explore the strength of Temporal Fusion Transformers (TFTs) over Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks in rainfall-runoff modeling. We train ten randomly initialized models, TFT and LSTM, for 531 CAMELS catchments in the US. We repeat the experiment with five subsets of the Caravan dataset, each representing catchments in the US, Australia, Brazil, Great Britain, and Chile. Then, the performance of the models, their variability regarding the catchment attributes, and the difference according to the datasets are assessed. Our findings show that TFT slightly outperforms LSTM, especially in simulating the midsection and peak of hydrographs. Furthermore, we show the ability of TFT to handle longer sequences and why it can be a better candidate for higher or larger catchments. Being an explainable AI technique, TFT identifies the key dynamic and static variables, providing valuable scientific insights. However, both TFT and LSTM exhibit a considerable drop in performance with the Caravan dataset, indicating possible data quality issues. Overall, the study highlights the potential of TFT in improving hydrological modeling and understanding.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Efficacy of Temporal Fusion Transformers for Runoff Simulation":

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the performance of Temporal Fusion Transformers (TFTs), a hybrid recurrent and attention-based deep learning architecture, for rainfall-runoff modeling. It compares TFTs against Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks, a standard recurrent architecture used in hydrological modeling. The authors train and evaluate both models on the CAMELS dataset (containing US catchments) and the Caravan dataset (a global dataset). The study assesses model performance based on various hydrological metrics, investigates performance variability with respect to catchment attributes, and examines the ability of TFTs to learn from longer sequences. The authors also explore the explainability of TFTs through variable importance analysis. The results suggest that TFTs slightly outperform LSTMs, especially in simulating peak flows, and benefit from longer input sequences. However, both models struggle on the Caravan dataset, potentially due to data quality issues.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty is moderate. While the use of TFTs in streamflow *forecasting* has been explored (as the authors themselves have previously done), this paper extends that exploration to rainfall-runoff *modeling*. This is a valuable distinction as it focuses on understanding the process itself rather than just predicting future states. However, the overall experimental setup (comparing against LSTM, using CAMELS/Caravan) is quite standard within the deep learning hydrology literature.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance lies in providing further evidence for the applicability of attention-based models in hydrology. It highlights the potential benefits of TFTs, particularly their ability to handle longer sequences and potentially capture longer-term dependencies within the hydrological process. The explainability aspect, demonstrated through variable importance plots, offers valuable insights for hydrologists.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly states the research question and motivation, highlighting the need to assess TFT's capabilities in rainfall-runoff modeling beyond streamflow forecasting.
*   **Thorough evaluation:** The paper uses a comprehensive set of hydrological metrics to evaluate model performance. It goes beyond simply reporting aggregate metrics and investigates performance variability with respect to catchment attributes and dataset characteristics.
*   **Explainability:** The variable importance analysis provides useful insights into the model's inner workings and the key factors driving runoff generation.
*   **Discussion of limitations:** The authors acknowledge the potential limitations of the Caravan dataset and its impact on model performance.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Marginal performance gains:** While TFTs show slight improvement over LSTMs, the performance gains are not dramatic, especially when using the Caravan dataset. This raises questions about the added complexity of TFTs versus their practical benefit.
*   **Standard experimental setup:** The evaluation of TFTs within a framework already used for similar studies does somewhat dilute the value of the findings.
*   **Data quality issues:** A clear weakness is the reliance on ERA5-Land reanalysis for the Caravan dataset. These types of data sets can have significant biases, particularly at the scale of individual catchments, and this may confound the analysis.
*   **Limited novel hydrological insights:** The paper mainly focuses on the algorithmic performance of TFTs. While the variable importance analysis is helpful, it doesn't provide significant new hydrological insights or a deep dive into the specific mechanisms captured by the attention mechanism.

**Potential Influence:**

The paper is likely to contribute to the growing body of literature on deep learning in hydrology. The study may encourage other researchers to explore TFTs or similar attention-based models for rainfall-runoff modeling and to carefully consider the impact of data quality on model performance. It also reinforces the importance of explainable AI techniques in hydrological applications.

**Justification of Score:**

Based on the critical evaluation, I assign the paper a score of **6/10**. While the paper provides a valuable contribution by extending the application of TFTs to rainfall-runoff modeling and exploring the explainability of TFTs, the marginal performance gains, reliance on ERA5 data, the mostly standard experimental setup, and limited novel hydrological insights restrict the overall significance of the findings. The conclusions, while sound, are not strongly supported by the data due to the potentially questionable quality of the ERA5-Land data. Therefore, while a valuable contribution, it does not constitute a major breakthrough in the field.

### Leveraging Vision-Language Models to Select Trustworthy Super-Resolution Samples Generated by Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20832v1)
- **Authors**: Cansu Korkmaz, Ahmet Murat Tekalp, Zafer Dogan
- **Abstract**: Super-resolution (SR) is an ill-posed inverse problem with many feasible solutions consistent with a given low-resolution image. On one hand, regressive SR models aim to balance fidelity and perceptual quality to yield a single solution, but this trade-off often introduces artifacts that create ambiguity in information-critical applications such as recognizing digits or letters. On the other hand, diffusion models generate a diverse set of SR images, but selecting the most trustworthy solution from this set remains a challenge. This paper introduces a robust, automated framework for identifying the most trustworthy SR sample from a diffusion-generated set by leveraging the semantic reasoning capabilities of vision-language models (VLMs). Specifically, VLMs such as BLIP-2, GPT-4o, and their variants are prompted with structured queries to assess semantic correctness, visual quality, and artifact presence. The top-ranked SR candidates are then ensembled to yield a single trustworthy output in a cost-effective manner. To rigorously assess the validity of VLM-selected samples, we propose a novel Trustworthiness Score (TWS) a hybrid metric that quantifies SR reliability based on three complementary components: semantic similarity via CLIP embeddings, structural integrity using SSIM on edge maps, and artifact sensitivity through multi-level wavelet decomposition. We empirically show that TWS correlates strongly with human preference in both ambiguous and natural images, and that VLM-guided selections consistently yield high TWS values. Compared to conventional metrics like PSNR, LPIPS, which fail to reflect information fidelity, our approach offers a principled, scalable, and generalizable solution for navigating the uncertainty of the diffusion SR space. By aligning outputs with human expectations and semantic correctness, this work sets a new benchmark for trustworthiness in generative SR.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of selecting the most trustworthy super-resolution (SR) sample from a set of diverse outputs generated by diffusion models, particularly in applications where information fidelity (e.g., digit recognition) is paramount.  The authors propose a framework that leverages vision-language models (VLMs) to automatically evaluate SR samples based on semantic correctness, visual quality, and artifact presence. They introduce a Trustworthiness Score (TWS), a hybrid metric that combines semantic similarity, edge consistency, and artifact sensitivity measured through wavelet decomposition. They demonstrate that VLM-guided selection yields high TWS values and correlates strongly with human preference, outperforming traditional metrics like PSNR, LPIPS, and DISTS in scenarios requiring information fidelity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The key novel aspect is the use of VLMs for *automatic* and *reliable* evaluation of SR outputs, especially in the context of diffusion models. Existing work primarily focuses on either generating diverse SR samples or manually selecting/ensembling them. The authors' use of structured prompts to elicit information from VLMs regarding quality and semantic correctness, specifically for selecting the most trustworthy result, is a novel contribution.  The TWS metric is also a valuable contribution as it provides an objective way to assess the trustworthiness of SR outputs, something which standard fidelity metrics often fail to capture. Replacing human feedback, previously needed in their prior work, with a scalable VLM evaluation pipeline is another significant step forward.

*   **Significance:**  This work is significant because it offers a practical and scalable solution to a crucial problem in SR: selecting the *right* solution from a space of many possibilities, especially in information-critical applications. The limitations of traditional metrics like PSNR and LPIPS are well-documented. By aligning model outputs with human expectations and semantic correctness, the paper sets a new benchmark for trustworthiness in generative SR tasks. The framework's ability to navigate the uncertainty of the diffusion SR space is highly relevant.
The use of VLM has a great effect on lowering the time consumption and labor force.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly identifies the limitations of existing SR methods and evaluation metrics in tasks requiring high information fidelity.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The use of VLMs for SR evaluation and sample selection is innovative and well-motivated.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The experimental results are comprehensive, including comparisons with state-of-the-art methods, human evaluation, and prompt robustness analysis.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The framework is practical and scalable, making it suitable for real-world applications.
    *   **Well-written and Organized:** The paper is well-written, clearly structured, and easy to follow.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** The paper could benefit from a more detailed discussion of the computational cost associated with using VLMs. While the authors claim cost-effectiveness, specific benchmarking of inference time and resource utilization would strengthen this claim.
    *   **VLM Dependency:** The approach relies on the availability and performance of VLMs. The robustness of the system could be affected if the VLM is poorly trained or biased. A more detailed discussion on the sensitivity of the framework to the choice of VLM would be valuable.
    *   **Limited Diversity of VLMs:** While the authors evaluate different VLMs, a more comprehensive analysis of the influence of VLM choice on the quality of the SR outputs would be beneficial.

*   **Impact:**  The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of SR by providing a more reliable and trustworthy approach for selecting SR samples, particularly in applications where information fidelity is critical. The TWS metric can also serve as a valuable tool for evaluating and comparing different SR methods.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a compelling and well-executed approach, a few points prevent it from achieving a higher score. The lack of detailed analysis regarding VLM dependency, limited exploration of other VLMs and potential cost issues detract from the novelty score. Nevertheless, the proposed framework exhibits significant promise and addresses a crucial challenge in the field, suggesting a substantial potential impact.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Leaner Training, Lower Leakage: Revisiting Memorization in LLM Fine-Tuning with LoRA
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20856v1)
- **Authors**: Fei Wang, Baochun Li
- **Abstract**: Memorization in large language models (LLMs) makes them vulnerable to data extraction attacks. While pre-training memorization has been extensively studied, fewer works have explored its impact in fine-tuning, particularly for LoRA fine-tuning, a widely adopted parameter-efficient method. In this work, we re-examine memorization in fine-tuning and uncover a surprising divergence from prior findings across different fine-tuning strategies. Factors such as model scale and data duplication, which strongly influence memorization in pre-training and full fine-tuning, do not follow the same trend in LoRA fine-tuning. Using a more relaxed similarity-based memorization metric, we demonstrate that LoRA significantly reduces memorization risks compared to full fine-tuning, while still maintaining strong task performance.
- **Summary**: Okay, I've reviewed the paper and can provide a summary and a critical evaluation.

**Summary**

The paper "Leaner Training, Lower Leakage: Revisiting Memorization in LLM Fine-Tuning with LoRA" investigates memorization in large language models (LLMs) during fine-tuning, specifically focusing on LoRA (Low-Rank Adaptation). The authors re-examine memorization across different fine-tuning strategies (full fine-tuning, head-only tuning, and LoRA) and observe that the impact of factors like model scale and data duplication differs significantly for LoRA compared to full fine-tuning.  They find that LoRA fine-tuning significantly reduces memorization risks compared to full fine-tuning, while still maintaining strong task performance. The study uses a plagiarism-based memorization metric and also a more relaxed similarity-based metric to demonstrate LoRA's advantages in mitigating data extraction risks.  The results are shown on GPT-2 and Llama 3 models.

**Critical Evaluation**

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in its systematic exploration of memorization in LoRA fine-tuning.
