While memorization in pre-training and full fine-tuning has received attention, LoRA's impact was relatively unexplored. The paper identifies a divergence in memorization behavior between LoRA and full fine-tuning, which is a new and interesting finding. It also analyzes the influence of LoRA hyperparameters on memorization, which provides actionable insights.

*   **Significance:** The paper's findings are significant because LoRA is a widely adopted parameter-efficient fine-tuning method.  Demonstrating that LoRA inherently reduces memorization risks compared to full fine-tuning has practical implications for privacy-preserving fine-tuning of LLMs, especially when dealing with sensitive or proprietary data.  The relaxed similarity metric is also important in this context, as verbatim plagiarism isn't the only concern for LLM memorization issues.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear Research Question:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of memorization in LLM fine-tuning and the need to evaluate LoRA's impact.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors conduct experiments using different model scales (GPT-2 and Llama families), data duplication levels, and LoRA hyperparameter settings, providing a robust evaluation.
    *   **Relevant Metrics:** The use of both plagiarism-based and similarity-based metrics provides a comprehensive assessment of memorization. The relaxed similarity metric is well justified.
    *   **Actionable Insights:** The study provides guidance on how to configure LoRA hyperparameters to balance task performance and memorization mitigation.
    *   **Comparison:** It provides a clear comparison with full fine-tuning and head-only fine-tuning, showing where LoRA excels.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited to specific Models:** While the models used are representative, the findings might not fully generalize to all LLM architectures. More extensive tests across a broader range of LLMs would add greater confidence to the conclusions.
    *   **Simplified Data Extraction:** The data extraction attacks used in the experiments are relatively basic. It remains unclear how LoRA would fare against more sophisticated attacks.
    *   **Arxiver dataset choice**: While the paper argues that this dataset reduces the likelihood of the finetuning data being seen in pre-training, there is still a non-zero chance, which could skew the results.

*   **Potential Influence:** This work could influence the adoption of LoRA as a preferred fine-tuning method when privacy is a concern.  It could also inspire further research into developing even more privacy-preserving fine-tuning techniques or more robust memorization evaluation methods.  It opens up an important avenue in understanding the relationship between model architecture and memorization.
*   **Justification for score**: The score reflects a contribution that addresses an underexplored aspect of LLM security (LoRA fine-tuning and memorization). The results are significant from a practical standpoint, given that LoRA has become a ubiquitous fine-tuning technique. The work is well-structured and the conclusions are reasonably supported. However, the identified weaknesses suggest that there are opportunities for further research to solidify and generalize the findings. Thus, an 7 is an appropriate score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Engineering RAG Systems for Real-World Applications: Design, Development, and Evaluation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20869v1)
- **Authors**: Md Toufique Hasan, Muhammad Waseem, Kai-Kristian Kemell, Ayman Asad Khan, Mika Saari, Pekka Abrahamsson
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems are emerging as a key approach for grounding Large Language Models (LLMs) in external knowledge, addressing limitations in factual accuracy and contextual relevance. However, there is a lack of empirical studies that report on the development of RAG-based implementations grounded in real-world use cases, evaluated through general user involvement, and accompanied by systematic documentation of lessons learned. This paper presents five domain-specific RAG applications developed for real-world scenarios across governance, cybersecurity, agriculture, industrial research, and medical diagnostics. Each system incorporates multilingual OCR, semantic retrieval via vector embeddings, and domain-adapted LLMs, deployed through local servers or cloud APIs to meet distinct user needs. A web-based evaluation involving a total of 100 participants assessed the systems across six dimensions: (i) Ease of Use, (ii) Relevance, (iii) Transparency, (iv) Responsiveness, (v) Accuracy, and (vi) Likelihood of Recommendation. Based on user feedback and our development experience, we documented twelve key lessons learned, highlighting technical, operational, and ethical challenges affecting the reliability and usability of RAG systems in practice.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the engineering of Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) systems for real-world applications. It details the design, development, and user evaluation of five domain-specific RAG implementations spanning municipal governance, cybersecurity, agriculture, industrial research, and medical diagnostics. The systems use multilingual OCR, semantic retrieval via vector embeddings, and domain-adapted LLMs.  A web-based evaluation with 100 participants assessed the systems on ease of use, relevance, transparency, responsiveness, accuracy, and likelihood of recommendation.  The paper presents twelve lessons learned based on user feedback and development experience, highlighting technical, operational, and ethical challenges in RAG system deployment.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its empirical approach and comprehensive evaluation of RAG systems across a diverse set of real-world applications. While RAG itself is a well-established technique, most existing research focuses on improving retrieval architectures or reducing hallucinations in controlled environments. This paper moves beyond that by examining the practical challenges of deploying and evaluating RAG in complex, domain-specific settings. The documented lessons learned offer practical insights that are often missing in purely theoretical or benchmark-driven studies.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a gap in the existing literature by providing concrete examples of RAG implementations tailored to different application domains. The user-centered evaluation offers valuable insights into the perceived usability and effectiveness of these systems, highlighting areas where RAG systems can be improved to better meet user needs. The identified technical, operational, and ethical challenges provide a practical roadmap for researchers and practitioners interested in deploying RAG in real-world environments.

**Strengths:**

*   **Empirical Focus:** The paper's strength is its focus on real-world applications and user evaluation. It moves beyond theoretical evaluations and provides tangible evidence of the challenges and benefits of RAG in practice.
*   **Diverse Applications:** The variety of application domains provides a broad perspective on the applicability and adaptability of RAG.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The user-centered evaluation covers multiple dimensions, providing a holistic assessment of the systems' performance.
*   **Practical Lessons Learned:** The documented lessons learned are valuable for practitioners and researchers, offering practical guidance for RAG system development and deployment.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Technical Depth:** The paper provides limited technical details on the specific implementation choices and optimization strategies employed for each RAG system. More detail on the architectural nuances and parameter tuning could enhance the paper's contribution.
*   **Subjectivity of User Evaluation:** User feedback is inherently subjective and may be influenced by factors such as prior experience, expectations, and task complexity. While the paper acknowledges this limitation, it could benefit from more robust statistical analysis to control for confounding variables.
*   **Generalizability:** The findings may not be fully generalizable to all real-world RAG deployments due to the specific characteristics of the selected application domains and user populations.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper's emphasis on empirical evaluation, diverse applications, and practical lessons learned contributes valuable insights to the field of RAG research. While the paper could benefit from more technical detail and robust statistical analysis, its practical focus and comprehensive evaluation make it a significant contribution. This paper will likely be of significant interest to practitioners looking to leverage RAG in their own systems. It is also valuable to researchers in this area, as the research will help to create more realistic expectations about the performance of RAG in real-world environments. As a result, the lessons learned from this project could significantly shape future system designs.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### MultiHuman-Testbench: Benchmarking Image Generation for Multiple Humans
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20879v1)
- **Authors**: Shubhankar Borse, Seokeon Choi, Sunghyun Park, Jeongho Kim, Shreya Kadambi, Risheek Garrepalli, Sungrack Yun, Munawar Hayat, Fatih Porikli
- **Abstract**: Generation of images containing multiple humans, performing complex actions, while preserving their facial identities, is a significant challenge. A major factor contributing to this is the lack of a a dedicated benchmark. To address this, we introduce MultiHuman-Testbench, a novel benchmark for rigorously evaluating generative models for multi-human generation. The benchmark comprises 1800 samples, including carefully curated text prompts, describing a range of simple to complex human actions. These prompts are matched with a total of 5,550 unique human face images, sampled uniformly to ensure diversity across age, ethnic background, and gender. Alongside captions, we provide human-selected pose conditioning images which accurately match the prompt. We propose a multi-faceted evaluation suite employing four key metrics to quantify face count, ID similarity, prompt alignment, and action detection. We conduct a thorough evaluation of a diverse set of models, including zero-shot approaches and training-based methods, with and without regional priors. We also propose novel techniques to incorporate image and region isolation using human segmentation and Hungarian matching, significantly improving ID similarity. Our proposed benchmark and key findings provide valuable insights and a standardized tool for advancing research in multi-human image generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MultiHuman-Testbench, a new benchmark dataset for evaluating generative models in their ability to generate images containing multiple humans performing actions while preserving individual identities. The dataset consists of 1800 carefully curated text prompts describing scenes with 1-5 humans, along with 5550 unique face images to ensure diversity in age, ethnicity, and gender. The benchmark also includes pose conditioning images.  The authors propose a multi-faceted evaluation suite employing metrics to quantify face count, ID similarity, prompt alignment, and action detection. They benchmark existing generative models, analyze their strengths and weaknesses, and propose novel techniques for improving identity preservation using regional isolation and Hungarian matching.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the creation of a dedicated benchmark specifically designed for multi-human image generation, a task that has been relatively under-addressed.  The inclusion of identity preservation as a key evaluation metric, along with the diversity of faces and action prompts, significantly enhances the benchmark's value. The regional isolation method is an incremental improvement over existing techniques.

* **Significance:** The creation of MultiHuman-Testbench directly addresses a critical gap in the field, which is the lack of a standardized and comprehensive evaluation resource.  This enables fair comparisons of different generative models and facilitates progress in generating realistic and diverse multi-human scenes. The analysis of existing models highlights key challenges, such as identity blending and inaccurate human counts, providing valuable directions for future research. However, the proposed enhancements (regional isolation and Hungarian matching) while improving results, are somewhat incremental and not revolutionary breakthroughs. The study of biases, which is crucial, adds to the societal impact of the work.

* **Strengths:**
    * Comprehensive and well-curated benchmark dataset.
    * Multi-faceted evaluation suite with relevant metrics.
    * Thorough benchmarking of existing generative models.
    * Identification of key challenges in multi-human image generation.
    * Study of biases within the models
    * Introduction of training-free improvement method.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * The proposed techniques for improving identity preservation are somewhat incremental.
    * While the dataset is diverse, the scale is still limited compared to datasets used in other image generation tasks (e.g., general image generation).
    * The improvement through pose priors (used in many existing methods) shows that more innovative approach to improve the base model is needed.

* **Potential Influence:** The MultiHuman-Testbench is poised to become a widely adopted benchmark for multi-human image generation research. The detailed analysis of current methods, coupled with the identified challenges, will stimulate further research in areas like identity preservation, scene composition, and bias mitigation. The proposed techniques, while incremental, can be valuable additions to existing generative models. The work’s influence is primarily in providing a needed evaluation tool rather than a radical shift in methodology.

**Justification:**

The score reflects the balance between the paper's strengths and weaknesses. The creation of a dedicated, high-quality benchmark fills a significant need in the field and will undoubtedly drive progress. The comprehensive evaluation and insightful analysis of current methods are valuable contributions. However, the incremental nature of the proposed techniques prevents a higher score. The impact is likely to be a steady improvement in multi-human image generation driven by the benchmarking tool.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Omniwise: Predicting GPU Kernels Performance with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20886v1)
- **Authors**: Zixian Wang, Cole Ramos, Muhammad A. Awad, Keith Lowery
- **Abstract**: In recent years, the rapid advancement of deep neural networks (DNNs) has revolutionized artificial intelligence, enabling models with unprecedented capabilities in understanding, generating, and processing complex data. These powerful architectures have transformed a wide range of downstream applications, tackling tasks beyond human reach. In this paper, we introduce Omniwise, the first end-to-end, self-supervised fine-tuning pipeline that applies large language models (LLMs) to GPU kernel performance prediction--a novel use case in performance profiling. Omniwise is model-agnostic and lightweight, achieving strong results even with a small 3B-parameter model. It can predict key performance metrics, including memory bandwidth, cache hit rates, GFLOPs, and arithmetic intensity, directly from kernel code without the need for code execution or profiling tools. Our approach achieves over 90% of predictions within 10% relative error on GPU kernels executed on AMD MI250 and MI300X architectures. In addition to the pipeline, we develop an online inference server and a Visual Studio Code plugin that seamlessly integrate LLM-based performance prediction into developers' workflows.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Omniwise, a novel end-to-end pipeline leveraging large language models (LLMs) for predicting GPU kernel performance. Instead of relying on traditional profiling methods, which are time-consuming and require deep understanding of GPU microarchitectures, Omniwise directly predicts performance counters (memory bandwidth, cache hit rates, GFLOPs, arithmetic intensity) from kernel source code. The pipeline involves data generation and augmentation (synthetic kernels, AI-generated code, custom kernels), performance counter collection, model training (fine-tuning LLAMA 3.2 3B), and a Visual Studio Code plugin for serving the model. The authors demonstrate that their fine-tuned LLM can achieve high accuracy (90% of predictions within 10% relative error) on AMD MI250 and MI300X architectures, offering a potential alternative to traditional GPU profiling workflows.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of using LLMs to predict GPU kernel performance *is* novel, although related work exists in using LLMs for compiler optimization (Meta LLM Compiler) and code analysis. The end-to-end pipeline aspect, encompassing data generation, fine-tuning and a VS Code plugin makes the work fairly complete. The idea of not just doing optimization, but of using the LLM as a direct proxy for performance is also new.

*   **Significance:** If Omniwise's performance translates to more complex real-world applications, it can significantly impact GPU software development. The ability to rapidly predict performance without execution/profiling can enable:
    *   Faster iteration cycles for developers.
    *   Early identification of performance bottlenecks.
    *   Compiler optimization guidance.
    *   Automated design space exploration.

    However, several aspects warrant consideration:

    *   **Dataset Dependence:** The model's performance hinges on the quality and representativeness of the training dataset. While the paper details a diverse data generation process, it's unclear how well the model will generalize to kernels with drastically different code patterns or workloads not covered during training. The assumption of having access to performance counters to build the original datasets makes the approach less impactful in cases where such counters are not available.
    *   **Code Complexity:** The current results are presented on relatively simple kernels. Real-world applications often involve complex interactions and dependencies, which may be difficult for LLMs to capture without substantial fine-tuning and larger datasets.
    *   **Hardware Specificity:**  The model is currently evaluated on AMD GPUs. Generalization to other GPU architectures (Nvidia, Intel) would be crucial for broader adoption. It is also not specified what the computational cost to perform model inference is. Given the potential use case for development, a low cost may be more desirable than the high accuracy.
    *   **Lack of comparison:** A comprehensive study against the state-of-the-art traditional profiling tools (like AMD's profiler or NVIDIA's Nsight Compute) is not performed. The comparison would show what scenarios Omniwise is better suited for.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined end-to-end pipeline.
    *   Demonstrated high accuracy on target architectures.
    *   Practical integration with a VS Code plugin.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Limited generalizability to complex applications and other architectures.
    *   Lack of a comprehensive comparison to traditional GPU profiling tools, to quantify improvements in time or accuracy.
    *   The reliance on a specific set of code patterns for the synthetic data generation approach.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's methodology could inspire new research directions in using LLMs for performance analysis and optimization. The VS Code integration could influence developer workflows, making performance prediction more accessible and integrated.

**Score: 7**

**Rationale:**

The paper presents a genuinely novel application of LLMs with potentially significant implications for GPU software development. The demonstrated high accuracy on the targeted architecture is promising. However, the limitations regarding dataset dependence, code complexity, hardware specificity, and lack of a comparative study significantly temper the overall assessment.  The reliance on data generation also limits the model's generalizability. While the idea is innovative, its broad impact is still uncertain without addressing these shortcomings. Therefore, a score of 7 reflects the paper's novelty and promise while acknowledging the limitations that need to be overcome for it to be considered a truly groundbreaking contribution.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### FaSTA$^*$: Fast-Slow Toolpath Agent with Subroutine Mining for Efficient Multi-turn Image Editing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20911v1)
- **Authors**: Advait Gupta, Rishie Raj, Dang Nguyen, Tianyi Zhou
- **Abstract**: We develop a cost-efficient neurosymbolic agent to address challenging multi-turn image editing tasks such as "Detect the bench in the image while recoloring it to pink. Also, remove the cat for a clearer view and recolor the wall to yellow.'' It combines the fast, high-level subtask planning by large language models (LLMs) with the slow, accurate, tool-use, and local A$^*$ search per subtask to find a cost-efficient toolpath -- a sequence of calls to AI tools. To save the cost of A$^*$ on similar subtasks, we perform inductive reasoning on previously successful toolpaths via LLMs to continuously extract/refine frequently used subroutines and reuse them as new tools for future tasks in an adaptive fast-slow planning, where the higher-level subroutines are explored first, and only when they fail, the low-level A$^*$ search is activated. The reusable symbolic subroutines considerably save exploration cost on the same types of subtasks applied to similar images, yielding a human-like fast-slow toolpath agent "FaSTA$^*$'': fast subtask planning followed by rule-based subroutine selection per subtask is attempted by LLMs at first, which is expected to cover most tasks, while slow A$^*$ search is only triggered for novel and challenging subtasks. By comparing with recent image editing approaches, we demonstrate FaSTA$^*$ is significantly more computationally efficient while remaining competitive with the state-of-the-art baseline in terms of success rate.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "FaSTA*: Fast-Slow Toolpath Agent with Subroutine Mining for Efficient Multi-turn Image Editing":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FaSTA*, a novel neurosymbolic agent designed for multi-turn image editing tasks. It aims to improve efficiency by combining fast, high-level subtask planning using Large Language Models (LLMs) with slow, accurate, tool-use planning using A* search. A key innovation is the use of LLMs to perform inductive reasoning on previously successful toolpaths, extracting and refining reusable subroutines. These subroutines are then used as high-level actions for fast planning, and A* search is only invoked when the fast plan fails. This "fast-slow" approach, reminiscent of human cognition, aims to reduce computational costs significantly while maintaining competitive image editing quality. The paper presents experiments demonstrating FaSTA*'s efficiency gains over the state-of-the-art baseline, CoSTA*, and demonstrates improved cost-quality trade-offs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a reasonably novel approach by integrating LLMs with classical A* search in a more refined way than previous works. Specifically, the idea of learning symbolic subroutines from previous experiences using LLMs for inductive reasoning is a valuable contribution. Building a memory of reusable actions in image editing agents addresses a key limitation of existing methods that treat each task in isolation. The fast-slow planning scheme is also a good analogy to human problem solving. It moves beyond naive LLM-based planning or purely search-based methods.

*   **Significance:** The primary significance lies in addressing the computational bottleneck of existing tool-using agents for image editing. By significantly reducing the reliance on A* search, FaSTA* makes multi-turn image editing more practical and scalable. The demonstrated efficiency gains, compared to CoSTA*, are substantial, indicating a tangible improvement in resource utilization. The concept of reusable subroutines could have broader implications for other complex task-solving domains involving tool use.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly identifies the computational cost limitations of existing image editing agents.
    *   **Technically sound:** The approach seems well-engineered and grounded in established techniques (LLMs, A* search, symbolic reasoning).
    *   **Empirical validation:** The experiments demonstrate clear improvements over CoSTA*, providing strong evidence for the effectiveness of the proposed approach. The ablation studies also successfully isolate the contributions of key components.
    *   **Complete Approach:** The methodology is well described. The data logging, periodic refinement of routines, the algorithm and the verification step are well formulated in the appendix.
    *   **Good Generalization:** They even provided results demonstrating how their routines generalized over a whole new benchmark dataset that they did not train with.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLMs:** Like many contemporary approaches, FaSTA* is heavily dependent on the capabilities of LLMs for both planning and inductive reasoning. The performance is thus tied to the evolving strengths and weaknesses of LLMs, which could be a limitation. Specifically, the reliance on Chain of Thought Reasoning can be problematic when LLMs are not properly trained.
    *   **Cold Start:** The method has a cold start problem, where the performance on an unseen task suffers until enough data is gathered.
    *   **Dataset Bias:** Dataset Bias can affect the quality of subroutines that are created.
    *   **Qualitative examples:** While the paper reports quantitative improvements, the qualitative comparison could have been more detailed. More visual examples showcasing how subroutines are reused and the specific toolpaths chosen under different conditions would have been illuminating.

*   **Potential Influence:** FaSTA*'s approach of learning and reusing subroutines could influence the development of more efficient and adaptable tool-using agents in image editing and potentially other domains. The fast-slow planning paradigm could also inspire further research into combining the strengths of LLMs and classical search algorithms.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

FaSTA* is a solid and worthwhile contribution with good novelty and practical value. It effectively addresses a key limitation of existing image editing agents. The weaknesses regarding LLM dependency and cold-start problems are common limitations in the field, and the benefits demonstrated in the experiments outweigh these drawbacks. A score of 7 reflects the significant advancements made in efficiency and the potential influence of the approach, while acknowledging the limitations and dependencies inherent in the method.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### ZKPROV: A Zero-Knowledge Approach to Dataset Provenance for Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20915v1)
- **Authors**: Mina Namazi, Alexander Nemecek, Erman Ayday
- **Abstract**: As the deployment of large language models (LLMs) grows in sensitive domains, ensuring the integrity of their computational provenance becomes a critical challenge, particularly in regulated sectors such as healthcare, where strict requirements are applied in dataset usage. We introduce ZKPROV, a novel cryptographic framework that enables zero-knowledge proofs of LLM provenance. It allows users to verify that a model is trained on a reliable dataset without revealing sensitive information about it or its parameters. Unlike prior approaches that focus on complete verification of the training process (incurring significant computational cost) or depend on trusted execution environments, ZKPROV offers a distinct balance. Our method cryptographically binds a trained model to its authorized training dataset(s) through zero-knowledge proofs while avoiding proof of every training step. By leveraging dataset-signed metadata and compact model parameter commitments, ZKPROV provides sound and privacy-preserving assurances that the result of the LLM is derived from a model trained on the claimed authorized and relevant dataset. Experimental results demonstrate the efficiency and scalability of the ZKPROV in generating this proof and verifying it, achieving a practical solution for real-world deployments. We also provide formal security guarantees, proving that our approach preserves dataset confidentiality while ensuring trustworthy dataset provenance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the ZKPROV paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ZKPROV, a novel zero-knowledge cryptographic framework designed to ensure the provenance of Large Language Model (LLM) training data.  It addresses the challenge of verifying that an LLM was trained on authorized datasets without revealing sensitive information about the dataset itself or the model's parameters. ZKPROV uses zero-knowledge proofs (ZKPs) to cryptographically bind a trained model to its authorized training data, avoiding the computational overhead of proving every training step or reliance on trusted execution environments.  The system leverages dataset-signed metadata, compact model parameter commitments, and recursive ZK proof systems like HyperNova to achieve efficiency and scalability.  The paper provides formal security guarantees and experimental results that demonstrate the feasibility of the approach for real-world LLM deployments.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in addressing the specific problem of *dataset provenance* in LLMs using zero-knowledge proofs.  While prior work has focused on verifying the *correctness* of LLM training or inference, ZKPROV uniquely addresses the need to prove *which* data was used for training without compromising data privacy or model confidentiality. Combining cryptographic primitives such as BLS signatures, Kate commitments, and recursive zero-knowledge proofs to solve this problem is novel.

*   **Significance:** Dataset provenance is a pressing concern in regulated industries such as healthcare, finance, and law, where models must demonstrably rely on approved data sources. ZKPROV's ability to provide verifiable assurances in these contexts is highly significant. It provides a mechanism for auditing and compliance, allowing organizations to confidently deploy LLMs while meeting stringent data governance requirements. The use of HyperNova to achieve sublinear scaling also represents a significant efficiency gain over naive ZK proof approaches that would be prohibitively expensive for large models. While existing approaches prove the inference accuracy of machine learning models, proving that the correct training dataset was used provides a unique addition to the body of literature.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of dataset provenance and its relevance to real-world LLM deployments.
    *   **Novel Solution:** ZKPROV provides a unique, cryptographic means of verifying data provenance without compromising data or model privacy.
    *   **Strong Cryptographic Foundation:** The framework relies on well-established cryptographic primitives with solid security guarantees.
    *   **Formal Security Analysis:** The paper provides rigorous formal security proofs demonstrating the soundness, zero-knowledge property, and dataset exposure binding of the system.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** Experimental results demonstrate the practical scalability of ZKPROV for reasonably-sized LLMs and multi-dataset scenarios.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Computational Cost:** While the paper demonstrates sublinear scaling, the computational overhead of ZK proof generation and verification remains a significant concern. The experiments are performed on LLaMA-3.1-8B, so scalability concerns may be more pronounced for models with significantly more parameters.
    *   **Implementation Complexity:** ZK proofs, especially recursive ones, are complex to implement correctly and securely. The complexity of implementation may hinder adoption.
    *   **Limited Scope:** The current implementation focuses on fine-tuning scenarios. Integration with other training methods and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) remains a topic for future work.
    *   **Lack of comparison to relevant metrics from existing work:** It may be helpful for the paper to compare latency and throughput metrics with existing methods to quantify the potential overheads.

*   **Potential Influence:** ZKPROV could influence the adoption of LLMs in regulated industries by providing a critical component for compliance and auditability. It could also inspire further research in verifiable machine learning, particularly in developing more efficient and privacy-preserving techniques for data provenance.

**Score: 7.5/10**

**Justification:**

ZKPROV represents a significant and novel contribution to the field of verifiable machine learning by addressing the critical problem of dataset provenance. The paper provides a well-defined solution, a strong cryptographic foundation, formal security guarantees, and experimental validation. It addresses a real-world need, particularly in the regulatory domains where trustworthy AI is essential.

However, the significant computational overhead and implementation complexity prevent a higher score. The paper's reliance on sophisticated cryptographic techniques makes it challenging to deploy in practice, and requires further investigation to make it accessible. Also, while novel, the paper does build upon well-established cryptographic primitives rather than introducing groundbreaking techniques, and its scope is currently limited to fine-tuning scenarios. Further efficiency improvements, broader applicability, and demonstration with larger, more complex models are needed to achieve a higher rating. Therefore, while ZKPROV marks a solid step, it doesn't quite reach the exceptional level due to practical limitations.

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### Metadata Enrichment of Long Text Documents using Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20918v1)
- **Authors**: Manika Lamba, You Peng, Sophie Nikolov, Glen Layne-Worthey, J. Stephen Downie
- **Abstract**: In this project, we semantically enriched and enhanced the metadata of long text documents, theses and dissertations, retrieved from the HathiTrust Digital Library in English published from 1920 to 2020 through a combination of manual efforts and large language models. This dataset provides a valuable resource for advancing research in areas such as computational social science, digital humanities, and information science. Our paper shows that enriching metadata using LLMs is particularly beneficial for digital repositories by introducing additional metadata access points that may not have originally been foreseen to accommodate various content types. This approach is particularly effective for repositories that have significant missing data in their existing metadata fields, enhancing search results and improving the accessibility of the digital repository.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a hybrid approach (manual curation plus LLMs) to enrich the metadata of long text documents (theses and dissertations) from the HathiTrust Digital Library, specifically focusing on English language documents published between 1920 and 2020. The authors describe a process of manually cleaning and normalizing existing metadata, adding new metadata fields (using Dublin Core standards), and employing LLMs for keyword extraction and text summarization to generate abstracts. The goal is to improve the discoverability and accessibility of these documents within the digital repository.  The paper outlines the challenges encountered during metadata cleaning (multiple authors, degrees, date format variations, duplicates) and describes the LLM-based methods used for semantic enrichment (KeyLLM and PaperQA).  The resulting dataset and code are made publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The combination of manual curation with LLM-based enrichment is a somewhat novel approach.  While previous work has explored metadata enhancement using either manual or automated techniques, a hybrid strategy combining the strengths of both is less common.  The use of KeyLLM and PaperQA is not particularly groundbreaking, as these are established LLM tools, but their application in this specific context of enriching thesis and dissertation metadata is a useful contribution. The data set made available to the research community is valuable to continue the research.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a practical and important problem in digital libraries: improving the quality and accessibility of metadata for long text documents. The ability to enrich metadata semantically increases the likelihood of content discovery which improves the usefulness of the repository.  The paper's contribution is particularly relevant for repositories with incomplete or inconsistent metadata. The findings provide insights into the challenges and opportunities of using LLMs for metadata enhancement in resource-constrained settings.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Methodology:** The paper provides a well-defined and replicable methodology, outlining the data collection, processing, and enrichment steps.
*   **Practical Relevance:** The research addresses a real-world problem faced by many digital libraries and archives.
*   **Open Data and Code:**  The availability of the dataset and code promotes reproducibility and further research.
*   **Detailed Challenges and Solutions:**  The authors clearly describe the challenges encountered during data cleaning and the solutions they implemented.
*   **Good use of LLM's** The research applied LLM models to overcome the challenges of metadata enrichment.
*   **Data enrichment** The use of existing technology was able to successfully enrich the data for more use.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Evaluation of LLM Performance:** The paper mentions manually reviewing only 10% of the LLM-generated content. A more comprehensive quantitative evaluation of the accuracy and quality of the LLM-generated keywords and abstracts would strengthen the findings.  How does the abstract generated by the LLM compare against manually generated abstracts? Are there biases introduced by the models?
*   **Lack of Specificity about the Manual Process:** While the paper describes the challenges of manual curation, it lacks detail on the precise rules and criteria used for manual metadata creation and cleaning. More detailed guidelines would improve the reproducibility of this manual component.
*   **Modest Impact:** The use of LLMs for this kind of enrichment work has become increasingly common and therefore this manuscript is on the cutting edge but not incredibly novel.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a practical, well-documented approach to metadata enrichment using a hybrid methodology that combines manual curation with LLM-based techniques. While the individual components (manual curation, KeyLLM, PaperQA) are not inherently novel, the combination and application to the specific problem of enriching thesis and dissertation metadata, along with the open data and code, constitutes a useful contribution to the field. The paper is well-structured, clearly written, and addresses an important problem for digital libraries. However, the limited evaluation of LLM performance and the lack of detailed guidelines for the manual curation process slightly reduce the overall impact.

Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.DL
- **Score**: 6/10

### FineWeb2: One Pipeline to Scale Them All -- Adapting Pre-Training Data Processing to Every Language
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20920v1)
- **Authors**: Guilherme Penedo, Hynek Kydlíček, Vinko Sabolčec, Bettina Messmer, Negar Foroutan, Amir Hossein Kargaran, Colin Raffel, Martin Jaggi, Leandro Von Werra, Thomas Wolf
- **Abstract**: Pre-training state-of-the-art large language models (LLMs) requires vast amounts of clean and diverse text data. While the open development of large high-quality English pre-training datasets has seen substantial recent progress, training performant multilingual LLMs remains a challenge, in large part due to the inherent difficulty of tailoring filtering and deduplication pipelines to a large number of languages. In this work, we introduce a new pre-training dataset curation pipeline based on FineWeb that can be automatically adapted to support any language. We extensively ablate our pipeline design choices on a set of nine diverse languages, guided by a set of meaningful and informative evaluation tasks that were chosen through a novel selection process based on measurable criteria. Ultimately, we show that our pipeline can be used to create non-English corpora that produce more performant models than prior datasets. We additionally introduce a straightforward and principled approach to rebalance datasets that takes into consideration both duplication count and quality, providing an additional performance uplift. Finally, we scale our pipeline to over 1000 languages using almost 100 Common Crawl snapshots to produce FineWeb2, a new 20 terabyte (5 billion document) multilingual dataset which we release along with our pipeline, training, and evaluation codebases.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces FineWeb2, a new multilingual pre-training dataset curation pipeline designed to automatically adapt to any language. Building on the FineWeb dataset, the pipeline addresses challenges in training performant multilingual Large Language Models (LLMs) due to the difficulty of tailoring filtering and deduplication for a wide range of languages. The authors extensively ablate design choices on nine diverse languages using a novel evaluation task selection process. Key pipeline components include language identification (LID), deduplication, filtering, and a duplication-aware upsampling (rehydration) strategy.  They demonstrate that models trained on their pipeline's output outperform those trained on existing datasets. The paper culminates in the creation of FineWeb2, a 20TB dataset spanning over 1000 languages, along with open-source release of the pipeline and evaluation codebase.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's core novelty lies in its automated and adaptable multilingual pre-training data processing pipeline.  While individual components like LID, deduplication, and filtering are not new in themselves, the **automatic tailoring** of these processes to individual languages based on language-specific statistics is a significant advance. The approach to rebalancing datasets using duplication and quality signals is also noteworthy.  However,  the reliance on existing components (like fastText for initial LID) and the incremental improvement upon FineWeb temper the overall novelty.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical bottleneck in multilingual LLM development: the lack of high-quality, appropriately processed data for many languages. By providing a pipeline that can be applied to virtually any language, FineWeb2 could democratize LLM development, enabling models that better serve a wider range of linguistic communities. The extensive ablation experiments and rigorous evaluation framework contribute substantially to our understanding of data curation for multilingual models.  The release of the dataset and codebase has the potential to stimulate further research and development in this area.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Comprehensive evaluation:** The authors conduct extensive experiments on a diverse set of languages, including both high- and low-resource ones.
    * **Principled design:** The pipeline components are well-motivated, and the ablation studies provide evidence for the effectiveness of each step. The task selection procedure adds rigor to the eval process.
    * **Open Source:** The open-sourcing of the pipeline, dataset, and evaluation tools is a significant contribution to the community.
    * **Scalability:** The pipeline's design allows it to scale to over 1000 languages using existing Common Crawl snapshots.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Dependency on Common Crawl:** The pipeline relies heavily on Common Crawl data, which may not be representative of all languages or domains.
    * **Limited task selection:** The evaluation tasks, while chosen with care, may not capture the full range of LLM capabilities in all languages. The results in Figure 3 showing FineWeb2 producing worse performing results when compared to models trained on language-specific dataset highlights the need for expert knowledge and tasks when designing an appropriate dataset.
    * **Bible/Wikipedia dominance**: Manual review shows a tendency to be composed primarily of the Bible and Wikipedia datasets.

* **Potential influence:** FineWeb2's adaptability and comprehensive language support has the potential to change the way large language models are designed and trained across languages.

* **Score Justification:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field by addressing a significant obstacle in multilingual LLM development. The automatic adaptation of the pipeline, the principled design choices, and the extensive evaluation framework all contribute to its strength. The release of the dataset and code will be particularly impactful. However, the reliance on Common Crawl, the limitations of the evaluation tasks, as well as its incremental evolution from existing datasets (FineWeb, specifically) prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking contribution.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### CodeGuard: A Generalized and Stealthy Backdoor Watermarking for Generative Code Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20926v1)
- **Authors**: Haoxuan Li, Jiale Zhang, Xiaobing Sun, Xiapu Luo
- **Abstract**: Generative code models (GCMs) significantly enhance development efficiency through automated code generation and code summarization. However, building and training these models require computational resources and time, necessitating effective digital copyright protection to prevent unauthorized leaks and misuse. Backdoor watermarking, by embedding hidden identifiers, simplifies copyright verification by breaking the model's black-box nature. Current backdoor watermarking techniques face two main challenges: first, limited generalization across different tasks and datasets, causing fluctuating verification rates; second, insufficient stealthiness, as watermarks are easily detected and removed by automated methods. To address these issues, we propose CodeGuard, a novel watermarking method combining attention mechanisms with distributed trigger embedding strategies. Specifically, CodeGuard employs attention mechanisms to identify watermark embedding positions, ensuring verifiability. Moreover, by using homomorphic character replacement, it avoids manual detection, while distributed trigger embedding reduces the likelihood of automated detection. Experimental results demonstrate that CodeGuard achieves up to 100% watermark verification rates in both code summarization and code generation tasks, with no impact on the primary task performance. In terms of stealthiness, CodeGuard performs exceptionally, with a maximum detection rate of only 0.078 against ONION detection methods, significantly lower than baseline methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "CodeGuard: A Generalized and Stealthy Backdoor Watermarking for Generative Code Models" introduces a new method for watermarking generative code models (GCMs) to protect intellectual property.  CodeGuard combines attention mechanisms with distributed trigger embedding strategies to address limitations in existing watermarking techniques like limited generalization and susceptibility to automated detection.  It uses attention to identify optimal watermark locations, homomorphic character replacement to avoid manual detection, and distributed trigger embedding to reduce automated detection. Experiments on code summarization and generation tasks demonstrate CodeGuard's high verification rates, minimal performance impact, and strong stealth against automated detection methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The novelty lies in the combination of several existing techniques in a specific way tailored for the GCM domain, and a clear demonstration that this specific combination yields a measurable improvement over alternatives. The individual components (attention mechanisms, homograph substitution, and distributed embeddings) are not new in themselves. The attention mechanism to identify suitable embedding positions is a significant contribution. It helps to improve verifiability across datasets and tasks, addressing a key weakness in previous methods. Also, the explicit combination with dispersed embedding and homoglyph substitution appears novel.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem: protecting the intellectual property of valuable GCMs. Successful deployment of GCMs relies on effective copyright protection. CodeGuard, by offering a stealthy and generalizable solution, takes an important step toward practical and secure deployment. The improvements in both verification accuracy and stealth are significant and address well-identified shortcomings in prior work. This can make the difference between deploying GCMs with a feeling of secure ownership or not.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper presents compelling experimental results demonstrating CodeGuard's high watermark verification rates and stealth. The comparisons with existing methods clearly highlight the advantages of the proposed approach.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges in watermarking GCMs and the limitations of current solutions.
    *   **Well-Explained Methodology:**  The methodology is clearly explained and well-motivated.
    *   **Addresses Generalization:**  The paper tackles the important issue of generalization across different tasks and datasets, which is a significant advancement over prior work.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Evaluation of LLMs:** The experiments, although comprehensive, do not fully explore the method's efficacy on very large language models (LLMs).  The paper acknowledges that further validation is needed in this area.
    *   **Threat Model:** The paper assumes a reasonably strong attacker. Although onion attack and spectral analysis attacks are considered, but doesn't explicitly consider adaptive attacks that might be specific to the homoglyph encoding used. This may be because such attacks are not likely at the current level of deployment of such technologies, but should be considered when there is a clear signal that the technology is becoming more widely deployed.
    *   **Limited Exploration of Parameter Settings:** The paper examines only 3 watermarking strengths (5%, 10%, and 15%). A wider range of parameter values could provide greater insight into the trade-offs between effectiveness, harmlessness, and stealthiness.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research on watermarking GCMs. The combination of attention mechanisms and distributed trigger embedding provides a promising direction for developing more robust and stealthy watermarking techniques. The results could encourage further exploration of attention-based techniques and the development of adaptive trigger designs.

**Justification of Score:**

I assign a score of **8** to this paper. The paper offers a significant contribution to the field of GCM watermarking by addressing key limitations of prior work and providing a novel, empirically validated solution. The techniques have been shown to improve over the state of the art by combining different aspects of related technologies together. While the individual components are not entirely new, the particular combination, especially the attention mechanism for watermark placement, and the robust evaluation are both high points of the paper. However, the limitations regarding LLM evaluation, the specific threat model considered, and parameter settings prevent it from reaching a higher score. Future work addressing these limitations could further enhance the impact of this research.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 8/10

### ParEval-Repo: A Benchmark Suite for Evaluating LLMs with Repository-level HPC Translation Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20938v1)
- **Authors**: Joshua H. Davis, Daniel Nichols, Ishan Khillan, Abhinav Bhatele
- **Abstract**: GPGPU architectures have become significantly diverse in recent years, which has led to an emergence of a variety of specialized programming models and software stacks to support them. While portable execution models exist, they still require significant developer effort to port to and optimize for different hardware architectures. Recent advances in large language models (LLMs) can help us reduce some of this programmer burden. In this paper, we present a novel benchmark and testing framework, ParEval-Repo, which can be used to evaluate the efficacy of LLM-based approaches in automatically translating entire codebases across GPGPU execution models. ParEval-Repo includes several scientific computing and AI mini-applications in a range of programming models, and levels of repository complexity. We use ParEval-Repo to evaluate a range of state-of-the-art open-source and commercial LLMs, with both a non-agentic and a top-down agentic approach. We assess code generated by the LLMs and approaches in terms of compilability, functional correctness, categories of build errors, and the cost of translation in terms of the number of inference tokens. Our results demonstrate that LLM translation of scientific applications is feasible for small programs but difficulty with generating functional build systems and cross-file dependencies pose challenges in scaling to larger codebases.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PAREVAL-REPO, a benchmark suite designed to evaluate the effectiveness of large language models (LLMs) in automatically translating entire codebases between different GPGPU execution models (CUDA, OpenMP, Kokkos). The benchmark includes scientific computing and AI mini-applications of varying sizes and complexities. The authors evaluate state-of-the-art open-source and commercial LLMs using both non-agentic and top-down agentic approaches, analyzing the resulting code for compilability, functional correctness, and translation cost (token usage).  The results show that while LLMs can handle small programs, generating functional build systems and managing cross-file dependencies pose significant challenges for larger codebases.  The paper also introduces a new metric, *Expected Token Cost* (Ek), to compare the inference costs of different translation techniques and LLMs.  The error analysis identifies common compilation failures that occur, providing insights for future improvement.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The benchmark itself, PAREVAL-REPO, is a novel and valuable contribution. While prior work has examined LLMs for code generation and translation, this paper significantly extends the scope to *repository-level* translation between different GPGPU programming models. The focus on translating *entire applications*, rather than isolated kernels or functions, introduces complexities related to build systems, data structures, and inter-file dependencies that are not captured by existing benchmarks. The introduction of Expected Token Cost metric is also a nice addition, providing a single metric for comparison across LLMs. However, it builds upon previous efforts for code generation and isn't a drastic departure.

*   **Significance:**  The problem addressed is highly significant.  The increasing diversity of GPGPU architectures and the need for portable code execution make automated translation highly desirable. This paper's systematic evaluation provides a much-needed understanding of the current capabilities and limitations of LLMs in this domain.  The error analysis and the identification of key challenges (e.g., build system generation, dependency management) can guide future research efforts. The insight that the 'overall' score is consistently lower than the 'code-only' score is a critical finding. This highlights that current LLMs are better at translating code snippets than creating full, runnable projects. This is a very concrete observation, and is useful for targeting future research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Benchmark:** PAREVAL-REPO provides a diverse set of applications with varying complexity levels, covering a range of scientific computing and AI workloads.
    *   **Systematic Evaluation:** The authors conduct a rigorous evaluation using multiple LLMs and translation techniques, providing a comprehensive view of the landscape.
    *   **Detailed Error Analysis:** The clustering of compilation errors provides valuable insights into the common pitfalls of LLM-based code translation.
    *   **Practical Focus:**  The paper addresses a real-world problem with significant practical implications for HPC application developers.
    *   **Well-Defined Metrics:** The pass@k, build@k and Expected Token Cost metrics provide a clear and quantifiable way to assess the performance of LLM-based translation techniques.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Application Size:** The largest application, llm.c, is still relatively small compared to many real-world HPC applications, meaning generalizability may be limited.
    *   **SWE-agent results are limited** Due to technical problems, the SWE-agent only tested against one LLM model.
    *   **Data Contamination:** While the paper acknowledges the issue of potential data contamination, more rigorous analysis on XSBench (the application known to have public ports) could have been done to quantify the effect of this issue.
    *   **Limited Scope of SWE-Agent Evaluation:**  The authors encountered limitations with SWE-agent due to its primary focus on Python repositories and incompatibility with Makefiles. This restricts the full exploration of agentic approaches. The usefulness of SWE-agent is undermined because it replaces tabs with spaces in Makefiles.
    *  **Absence of Human Baseline**: Ideally the paper would compare LLM performance against human manual translation performance.

*   **Impact:**  The PAREVAL-REPO benchmark has the potential to become a standard tool for evaluating LLM-based code translation techniques in the HPC domain.  The insights from this paper can guide the development of more effective LLM-based translation tools and inform future research directions.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is a valuable contribution to the field, but has some weaknesses that detract from its overall impact. The development of the PAREVAL-REPO benchmark and the systematic evaluation of different LLMs and translation techniques are significant. The detailed error analysis provides practical insights for future research. However, the limited application size and the issues with existing tools like SWE-agent reduce the generalizability of the results and prevent a deeper exploration of agentic approaches. While the work is novel in its specific combination of repository-level translation and GPGPU programming models, it builds upon existing research in code generation and translation with LLMs. For these reasons, a score of 7 reflects the novelty and significance of the work, acknowledging both its strengths and weaknesses.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.DC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Model State Arithmetic for Machine Unlearning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20941v1)
- **Authors**: Keivan Rezaei, Mehrdad Saberi, Abhilasha Ravichander, Soheil Feizi
- **Abstract**: Large language models are trained on massive corpora of web data, which may include private data, copyrighted material, factually inaccurate data, or data that degrades model performance. Eliminating the influence of such problematic datapoints through complete retraining -- by repeatedly pretraining the model on datasets that exclude these specific instances -- is computationally prohibitive. For this reason, unlearning algorithms have emerged that aim to eliminate the influence of particular datapoints, while otherwise preserving the model -- at a low computational cost. However, precisely estimating and undoing the influence of individual datapoints has proved to be challenging. In this work, we propose a new algorithm, MSA, for estimating and undoing the influence of datapoints -- by leveraging model checkpoints i.e. artifacts capturing model states at different stages of pretraining. Our experimental results demonstrate that MSA consistently outperforms existing machine unlearning algorithms across multiple benchmarks, models, and evaluation metrics, suggesting that MSA could be an effective approach towards more flexible large language models that are capable of data erasure.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Model State Arithmetic (MSA), a novel machine unlearning algorithm. Unlike existing methods that primarily focus on updating the final model parameters, MSA leverages intermediate model checkpoints during pretraining to estimate and undo the influence of specific data points. It computes a "forget vector" by finetuning a checkpoint (prior to exposure to the data to be unlearned) on the data to be unlearned, and then applies this vector to the final model parameters. The authors demonstrate that MSA consistently outperforms existing machine unlearning algorithms across various benchmarks (TOFU and RESTOR), models (Llama-3.2 family, OLMo-2-1B), and evaluation metrics. The paper also explores how the effectiveness of MSA depends on the closeness of the checkpoint to the unlearning target and shows that even distant checkpoints can be effective.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of leveraging intermediate checkpoints for unlearning is relatively novel. Existing unlearning techniques typically focus on directly modifying the final model's parameters or its training process. The connection to task arithmetic, while mentioned, isn't deeply explored, but the application to unlearning is a distinct contribution. The framing of unlearning as arithmetic in the parameter space, particularly using checkpoints *before* seeing the data to be unlearned, is a good approach.

*   **Significance:** Machine unlearning is becoming increasingly important due to privacy regulations (like GDPR/Right to be Forgotten) and ethical concerns related to training data. The practical advantages of MSA, especially the ability to use existing checkpoints (which are often stored for other reasons like fault tolerance), make it a potentially significant contribution. The empirical results are also compelling, demonstrating consistent improvements over existing methods across different scenarios.
    However, the paper has some limitations. First, the computational overhead of finetuning a checkpoint to derive the 'forget vector' should be further examined relative to other methods. Although the paper mentions that the overhead is limited to the forget set size, the actual training time and resource requirements should be compared against directly updating the final model's parameters or modifying the training loop. Second, the mechanism underlying the effectiveness of MSA is not deeply analyzed. Why using earlier checkpoints leads to more precise estimation and undoing of the influence of specific data points could be further elucidated. Third, a more rigorous treatment of hyperparameter selection and sensitivity analysis would bolster the findings. The paper details validation procedures but could benefit from exploring how sensitive MSA is to specific choices of `alpha`, `beta`, and checkpoint selection.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong empirical results across multiple benchmarks and models.
    *   Novel approach of leveraging intermediate checkpoints.
    *   Demonstrated effectiveness even with distant checkpoints.
    *   Practical applicability due to the use of readily available checkpoints.
    *   Demonstrates efficacy even without a retain set, reducing the tuning overhead.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited analysis of the computational cost of finetuning checkpoints compared to alternatives.
    *   Lack of deep mechanistic explanation for why earlier checkpoints are more effective.
    *   Could benefit from a more detailed hyperparameter sensitivity analysis.
    *   Limited discussion on scaling of the technique to larger models.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a more practical and effective approach to machine unlearning. It suggests a new direction for research, focusing on leveraging existing training artifacts for unlearning.

* **Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:**

MSA presents a significant step forward in practical unlearning, justified by strong empirical performance, and leveraging readily available model checkpoints for more effective removal of the influence of harmful data. The approach effectively frames unlearning as an arithmetic operation performed in parameter space. The method is also effective even in the absence of a retain set, reducing the tuning burden. The score reflects this novelty and significance, but also considers the areas where the paper could be strengthened, specifically in providing deeper mechanistic insight into why MSA works and thoroughly evaluating the computational overhead with respect to other approaches. The paper's claims are well supported, but the evaluation could also benefit from scaling to larger models. Finally, while the approach of leveraging historical training states makes sense, a more thorough treatment of the effect of checkpoint age (distance) on performance would be valuable.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### E-FreeM2: Efficient Training-Free Multi-Scale and Cross-Modal News Verification via MLLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20944v1)
- **Authors**: Van-Hoang Phan, Long-Khanh Pham, Dang Vu, Anh-Duy Tran, Minh-Son Dao
- **Abstract**: The rapid spread of misinformation in mobile and wireless networks presents critical security challenges. This study introduces a training-free, retrieval-based multimodal fact verification system that leverages pretrained vision-language models and large language models for credibility assessment. By dynamically retrieving and cross-referencing trusted data sources, our approach mitigates vulnerabilities of traditional training-based models, such as adversarial attacks and data poisoning. Additionally, its lightweight design enables seamless edge device integration without extensive on-device processing. Experiments on two fact-checking benchmarks achieve SOTA results, confirming its effectiveness in misinformation detection and its robustness against various attack vectors, highlighting its potential to enhance security in mobile and wireless communication environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces E-FreeM², a training-free system for detecting out-of-context (OOC) misinformation in news. The system utilizes pre-trained vision-language models and large language models. It retrieves supporting evidence from external sources, filters this evidence for relevance and reliability, and then uses a two-stage reasoning process to determine if a given image-text pair represents OOC misinformation. The system outperforms existing methods without requiring any training data or extensive computational resources. The authors emphasize the system's robustness against adversarial attacks and data poisoning, making it suitable for deployment on edge devices.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in combining a training-free approach with multi-scale cross-modal retrieval and a two-stage chain-of-thought reasoning process specifically tailored for OOC misinformation detection. While retrieval-based and chain-of-thought methods are not entirely new, their specific application and integration within the E-FreeM² framework, particularly without requiring training data, presents a novel contribution.
*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from its ability to detect OOC misinformation effectively without the need for extensive training. This is particularly important given the rapidly evolving nature of misinformation and the challenges associated with maintaining up-to-date training datasets and the sensitivity to adversarial attacks. The efficiency of the system also enables deployment on edge devices, making it practical for real-world applications in mobile and wireless environments.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Training-Free Approach:** Eliminates the need for large, labeled datasets and frequent retraining, addressing a key limitation of many existing methods.
    *   **Cross-Modal Retrieval:** Leveraging both visual and textual cues for evidence retrieval enhances the accuracy and robustness of the system.
    *   **Multi-Scale Filtering:** Hierarchical filtering strategies ensures high-quality of retrieved evidence, reducing noise and improving downstream reasoning.
    *   **Strong Performance:** Achieves state-of-the-art results on a benchmark dataset (NewsCLIPpings) compared to other methods.
    *   **Efficiency:** Operates with zero trainable parameters, allowing for efficient deployment and scaling.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on External Search Engines:** The system's performance is tied to the availability and quality of external search engines. Future work should explore strategies to mitigate reliance on external sources.
    *   **Language Bias:** Current implementation is largely focused on English, limiting its direct applicability to other languages.
    *   **Limited Evaluation on Sophisticated Attacks:** While the authors mention robustness against adversarial attacks, they don't provide an extensive evaluation against sophisticated deepfake attacks, which might be more challenging.
    *   **Visual-Centric Ranking Bias:** The prioritization of visual similarity might cause failure in cases that textual evidences are critical.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable contribution to the field of misinformation detection by proposing a novel, effective, and efficient training-free approach to address the critical challenge of OOC misinformation. While the paper has some limitations that could be addressed in future work, its strengths outweigh its weaknesses. The significance of training-free methods in dealing with dynamic misinformation landscapes makes this work of considerable value.
Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.MM
- **Score**: 7/10

### Hierarchical Sub-action Tree for Continuous Sign Language Recognition
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20947v1)
- **Authors**: Dejie Yang, Zhu Xu, Xinjie Gao, Yang Liu
- **Abstract**: Continuous sign language recognition (CSLR) aims to transcribe untrimmed videos into glosses, which are typically textual words. Recent studies indicate that the lack of large datasets and precise annotations has become a bottleneck for CSLR due to insufficient training data. To address this, some works have developed cross-modal solutions to align visual and textual modalities. However, they typically extract textual features from glosses without fully utilizing their knowledge. In this paper, we propose the Hierarchical Sub-action Tree (HST), termed HST-CSLR, to efficiently combine gloss knowledge with visual representation learning. By incorporating gloss-specific knowledge from large language models, our approach leverages textual information more effectively. Specifically, we construct an HST for textual information representation, aligning visual and textual modalities step-by-step and benefiting from the tree structure to reduce computational complexity. Additionally, we impose a contrastive alignment enhancement to bridge the gap between the two modalities. Experiments on four datasets (PHOENIX-2014, PHOENIX-2014T, CSL-Daily, and Sign Language Gesture) demonstrate the effectiveness of our HST-CSLR.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach called Hierarchical Sub-action Tree for Continuous Sign Language Recognition (HST-CSLR). The core idea is to address the lack of fine-grained annotations in existing CSLR datasets by leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate detailed sub-action descriptions for each gloss.  These descriptions are then organized into a hierarchical tree structure, enabling both temporal modeling and multi-level cross-modal alignment between video features and gloss representations. The method also includes a contrastive alignment enhancement module to further bridge the gap between visual and textual modalities.  Experiments on four datasets demonstrate improved performance compared to state-of-the-art methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Strengths:**
    * **Addressing a key limitation:** The paper directly tackles the problem of limited fine-grained annotations in CSLR, which is a recognized bottleneck.
    * **Innovative use of LLMs:** Using LLMs to generate sub-action descriptions is a creative way to augment existing datasets with semantic information.
    * **Hierarchical tree structure:** The HST provides a structured way to integrate and utilize the generated descriptions, allowing for both temporal and hierarchical reasoning. This attempts to model the inherent structure in sign language execution.
    * **Cross-modal alignment:** The contrastive alignment loss explicitly encourages consistency between visual and textual modalities, which is crucial for CSLR.
    * **Empirical validation:** Experiments on multiple datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method, showing consistent improvements over existing approaches.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Dependency on LLM quality:** The performance of HST-CSLR heavily relies on the quality of the sub-action descriptions generated by the LLM.  The paper acknowledges the issue of "low-quality gloss statements" but only addresses it with manual filtering. This raises concerns about scalability and reproducibility. The reliance on GPT-4 (or similar models) also makes the method more difficult to replicate without access to these resources.
    * **Lack of detail on prompt engineering:** While the paper mentions crafting prompts for the LLM, it lacks a comprehensive explanation of the prompt engineering process.  The specific prompts used are presented as an example, but it's unclear how sensitive the performance is to prompt variations and whether there's a systematic approach to prompt design.  Different prompts could elicit significantly different results.
    * **Limited ablation studies:** While some ablation studies are provided, they are not exhaustive. For example, it would be beneficial to investigate the impact of different clustering algorithms on HST construction.
    * **Incremental improvement:** Although the paper reports improved performance, the gains over state-of-the-art methods on some datasets (especially PHOENIX-2014T) are relatively small, suggesting that the method might be reaching a point of diminishing returns on those benchmarks.

* **Novelty and Significance:**

The paper demonstrates novelty in its integrated approach to CSLR. The combination of LLM-generated sub-action descriptions, a hierarchical tree structure, and cross-modal contrastive alignment provides a more comprehensive way to model and understand sign language videos. The significance of this work lies in its potential to address the data scarcity problem in CSLR and improve recognition accuracy, which can ultimately lead to better communication tools for the deaf community.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and well-motivated approach to CSLR, addressing a critical limitation in the field. The innovative use of LLMs for sub-action description generation and the hierarchical tree structure are strengths. However, the heavy reliance on LLM quality, the lack of detailed prompt engineering, and the incremental improvements compared to the state-of-the-art prevent it from achieving a higher score. The manual filtering process raises concerns about scalability and reproducibility. The paper also lacks an analysis of computational resources required for this LLM augmented architecture. A more systematic exploration of prompt design and a thorough analysis of the LLM's impact on overall performance would further strengthen the work.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Antibody Design and Optimization with Multi-scale Equivariant Graph Diffusion Models for Accurate Complex Antigen Binding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.20957v1)
- **Authors**: Jiameng Chen, Xiantao Cai, Jia Wu, Wenbin Hu
- **Abstract**: Antibody design remains a critical challenge in therapeutic and diagnostic development, particularly for complex antigens with diverse binding interfaces. Current computational methods face two main limitations: (1) capturing geometric features while preserving symmetries, and (2) generalizing novel antigen interfaces. Despite recent advancements, these methods often fail to accurately capture molecular interactions and maintain structural integrity. To address these challenges, we propose \textbf{AbMEGD}, an end-to-end framework integrating \textbf{M}ulti-scale \textbf{E}quivariant \textbf{G}raph \textbf{D}iffusion for antibody sequence and structure co-design.
