### Enhancing LLM Tool Use with High-quality Instruction Data from Knowledge Graph
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21071v1)
- **Authors**: Jingwei Wang, Zai Zhang, Hao Qian, Chunjing Gan, Binbin Hu, Ziqi Liu, Zhiqiang Zhang, Jun Zhou, Bin Shi, Bo Dong
- **Abstract**: Teaching large language models (LLMs) to use tools is crucial for improving their problem-solving abilities and expanding their applications. However, effectively using tools is challenging because it requires a deep understanding of tool functionalities and user intentions. Previous methods relied mainly on LLMs to generate instruction data, but the quality of these data was often insufficient. In this paper, we propose a new method that uses knowledge graphs to generate high-quality instruction data for LLMs. Knowledge graphs are manually curated datasets rich in semantic information. We begin by extracting various query pathways from a given knowledge graph, which are transformed into a broad spectrum of user queries. We then translate the relationships between entities into actionable tools and parse the pathways of each query into detailed solution steps, thereby creating high-quality instruction data. Our experiments show that fine-tuning on just a small sample of this synthetic data can significantly improve the tool utilization and overall capabilities of LLMs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Enhancing LLM Tool Use with High-quality Instruction Data from Knowledge Graph":

**Summary:**

This paper introduces a new approach to enhance the tool-use capabilities of Large Language Models (LLMs) through instruction tuning. The core idea is to leverage Knowledge Graphs (KGs) to generate high-quality instruction data. The method involves: 1) extracting query pathways from KGs, 2) translating these pathways into user queries and corresponding tool-execution steps, and 3) fine-tuning LLMs on the resulting data. The authors demonstrate that fine-tuning on a relatively small dataset generated through this approach can significantly improve LLM performance on tool utilization benchmarks, surpassing even larger models in some cases. The method avoids the common pitfalls of relying on LLM-generated data (potentially low quality) or extensive human annotation (expensive). They introduce a framework called KG2Tool and evaluate it by fine-tuning different LLMs, observing performance improvements on the T-Eval benchmark and demonstrating the effectiveness of the synthesized data.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Novelty:** The use of knowledge graphs to generate instruction data for tool use is a good idea. KGs provide a structured, verified source of knowledge that can overcome the limitations of LLM-generated data, which can be noisy and require extensive human review. It's an innovative approach. The use of First-Order Logic (FOL) is also a strength, providing a structured way to create complex queries with guaranteed answer quality.
    *   **Significance:** Improving LLM tool use is a crucial area of research. LLMs that can effectively leverage external tools have the potential to solve more complex real-world problems. By addressing the issue of data quality and cost, the paper makes a potentially significant contribution to the practical application of LLM tool use. The experiments showing improvements even with smaller models suggest that this approach is resource-efficient.
    *   **Clarity:** The paper is generally well-written and explains the methodology clearly. The diagrams, especially Figure 1, are helpful in understanding the data generation process.
    *   **Experimental Results:** The experiments are thorough and include comparisons with various baseline models, including both open-source and closed-source LLMs. The results on the T-Eval benchmark are promising and support the claim that KG2Tool improves tool utilization.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Scope of KGs:** The paper does not discuss what the limitation might be if the used knowledge graph is itself incomplete, biased, or not up to date. The paper does not cover what should be done in cases where the target LLMs do not align with the facts from the KGs. The authors could benefit from acknowledging possible drawbacks.
    *   **Specific KG Dependency:** The approach relies on having access to a suitable knowledge graph for the target task. While many KGs exist, creating or adapting one for a specific domain could still be a significant undertaking.
    *   **Translation Prompt Engineering details:** Not enough details have been provided with prompt engineering, which is key to data synthesis for instruction fine-tuning.
    *   **Generality:** The framework has only been demonstrated in one benchmark and for one use case, and therefore is limited in the paper's ability to make generalized claims about the utility of the methodology.
    *   **Lack of ablation study**: With no ablation study done over the contribution of FOL instantiation, the novelty over the integration of FOL is underpowered.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The paper could inspire further research into using structured knowledge sources for instruction tuning.
    *   The KG2Tool framework could be adopted by others to generate high-quality training data for LLM tool use.
    *   The findings could encourage the development of more resource-efficient approaches to LLM tool utilization.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and potentially significant approach to improving LLM tool use. The use of KGs to generate high-quality instruction data is a good idea, and the experimental results are promising. However, there are certain limitations, especially related to the scope and details of the prompt engineering, and the dependency on task-specific KGs. While the approach addresses important practical concerns about data quality and cost, certain elements are missing to make generalized claims. Because the paper makes a strong contribution to a critical area of LLM research and addresses an issue that has implications for broader applications, it has significant value.

**Score: 7**
- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Chain-of-Thought Enhanced Shallow Transformers for Wireless Symbol Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21093v1)
- **Authors**: Li Fan, Peng Wang, Jing Yang, Cong Shen
- **Abstract**: Transformers have shown potential in solving wireless communication problems, particularly via in-context learning (ICL), where models adapt to new tasks through prompts without requiring model updates. However, prior ICL-based Transformer models rely on deep architectures with many layers to achieve satisfactory performance, resulting in substantial storage and computational costs. In this work, we propose CHain Of thOught Symbol dEtection (CHOOSE), a CoT-enhanced shallow Transformer framework for wireless symbol detection. By introducing autoregressive latent reasoning steps within the hidden space, CHOOSE significantly improves the reasoning capacity of shallow models (1-2 layers) without increasing model depth. This design enables lightweight Transformers to achieve detection performance comparable to much deeper models, making them well-suited for deployment on resource-constrained mobile devices. Experimental results demonstrate that our approach outperforms conventional shallow Transformers and achieves performance comparable to that of deep Transformers, while maintaining storage and computational efficiency. This represents a promising direction for implementing Transformer-based algorithms in wireless receivers with limited computational resources.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces CHOOSE (Chain Of thought Symbol detection), a novel Transformer architecture designed for wireless symbol detection, specifically targeting resource-constrained environments. CHOOSE enhances shallow Transformers (1-2 layers) by incorporating autoregressive latent reasoning steps (CoT) within the hidden space. This allows the shallow model to emulate the reasoning capacity of deeper models without increasing model depth, reducing storage and computational costs. The authors demonstrate through experiments that CHOOSE outperforms conventional shallow Transformers and achieves performance comparable to much deeper models while maintaining significant storage and computational efficiency. The paper emphasizes the architectural compatibility with existing Transformer pipelines and introduces a training strategy that enables latent reasoning without explicit supervision on intermediate steps.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the integration of Chain-of-Thought reasoning *within the latent space* of a shallow Transformer for wireless symbol detection. While CoT has been explored in NLP, its application in this specific domain and the implementation within the latent space (avoiding the need for explicit symbolic outputs) offers a unique contribution. The idea of using CoT to enhance the *expressiveness* of *shallow* models, specifically to reduce the resource footprint in wireless communication is also a valuable and innovative concept. The training methodology that enables latent reasoning without direct supervision on intermediate steps also enhances the novelty.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the work stems from its practical applicability. Wireless communication systems are often deployed on resource-constrained devices (mobile phones, IoT devices). The ability to use lightweight Transformer models without sacrificing performance has a direct impact on reducing energy consumption, latency, and storage requirements. The paper provides experimental validation demonstrating the performance gains relative to both shallow and deeper models. Additionally, the seamless architectural integration with existing Transformer-based wireless systems suggests practical adoptability. The comparative evaluation against MMSE provides a useful benchmark demonstrating how close they approach theoretical performance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of high computational cost associated with deep Transformer models in wireless communication.
    *   **Novel Approach:** The latent CoT approach within a shallow Transformer represents a distinct and well-motivated solution.
    *   **Strong Experimental Validation:** The experimental results, comparing CHOOSE against relevant baselines, are compelling and demonstrate the performance improvements achieved. The inclusion of MMSE, a theoretical limit, provides a valuable benchmark. Analysis of the intermediate reasoning steps offers interesting insights into the model's behavior.
    *   **Practical Relevance:** The reduced model size and computational complexity make CHOOSE well-suited for edge deployment, addressing a real-world challenge in wireless communication.
    *   **Comprehensive Analysis:** Provides the experiments which clearly justify the need for a lightweight model in resource-constrained environments.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Exploration of Hyperparameters:**  The paper reports results for a specific configuration of CHOOSE (embedding dimension, number of layers, CoT steps). A more thorough exploration of these hyperparameters could strengthen the analysis. A broader range of CoT values beyond 4 can strengthen the arguments.
    *   **Synthetic Data:** The models are trained on synthetic data, and performance in real-world wireless environments is not demonstrated. While the channel model is standard, real-world channel impairments are complex and diverse. The generalization capability of CHOOSE to real-world wireless datasets needs to be validated.
    *   **Limited Comparison to Alternative Compression Techniques:**  While the paper compares against vanilla Transformer architectures, it lacks comparison with other compression or model reduction techniques like knowledge distillation, pruning, or quantization. These methods could potentially also lead to lightweight models for wireless symbol detection.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more efficient and practical Transformer-based solutions for wireless communication. The latent CoT approach could be adopted in other resource-constrained applications as well.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

A score of 7 reflects the paper's solid contribution to the field. The novelty of latent CoT reasoning within a shallow Transformer for wireless symbol detection is well-established, and the experimental results are compelling. The practical relevance and potential for edge deployment are significant strengths. However, the limited exploration of hyperparameters, lack of real-world data validation, and absence of comparison with other model compression techniques prevent a higher score. The paper presents a valuable and novel approach, but further investigation is needed to fully assess its robustness and generalizability in real-world scenarios. The work is significant and represents a promising direction but is not a complete solution.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Learning to Skip the Middle Layers of Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21103v1)
- **Authors**: Tim Lawson, Laurence Aitchison
- **Abstract**: Conditional computation is a popular strategy to make Transformers more efficient. Existing methods often target individual modules (e.g., mixture-of-experts layers) or skip layers independently of one another. However, interpretability research has demonstrated that the middle layers of Transformers exhibit greater redundancy, and that early layers aggregate information into token positions. Guided by these insights, we propose a novel architecture that dynamically skips a variable number of layers from the middle outward. In particular, a learned gating mechanism determines whether to bypass a symmetric span of central blocks based on the input, and a gated attention mechanism prevents subsequent tokens from attending to skipped token positions. Residual norms are controlled with a 'sandwich' or 'perilayernorm' scheme and gate sparsity with an adaptive regularization loss. We had aimed to reduce compute requirements for 'simpler' tokens and potentially foster an emergent multi-level representational hierarchy but, at the scales investigated, our approach does not achieve improvements in the trade-off between validation cross-entropy and estimated FLOPs compared to dense baselines with fewer layers. We release our code at https://github.com/tim-lawson/skip-middle.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Learning to Skip the Middle Layers of Transformers":

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel Transformer architecture designed to improve efficiency by dynamically skipping middle layers based on the input token. The core idea leverages the observation that middle layers in Transformers often exhibit redundancy.  A learned gating mechanism determines, for each token, whether a symmetric span of central blocks should be bypassed. To prevent skipped tokens from disrupting attention, a gated attention mechanism prevents subsequent tokens from attending to the skipped positions.  The architecture also uses a 'sandwich' or 'peri-layernorm' scheme to control residual norms, and an adaptive regularization loss to encourage gate sparsity. The authors hypothesized that this approach would reduce computation for "simpler" tokens and foster a multi-level representational hierarchy. However, experimental results at the scales investigated, show that the method doesn't improve the trade-off between validation cross-entropy and estimated FLOPs compared to dense baselines with fewer layers.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of selectively skipping *middle* layers based on token-specific gating is a relatively novel approach within the broader literature on conditional computation and efficient Transformers. While techniques like early exiting and MoEs exist, they typically focus on depth reduction or module-level sparsity rather than specifically targeting redundancy in the center of the network. The combination of the gating mechanism with the gated attention and 'peri-layernorm' scheme adds another layer of novelty.

*   **Significance:**  The potential significance lies in its connection to interpretability research and the desire to create more efficient and potentially more interpretable Transformer architectures. If successful, this approach could have led to faster inference and a better understanding of how Transformers process information at different layers.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The approach is well-motivated by existing interpretability research on Transformer redundancy.
    *   The architecture is relatively well-designed, combining several techniques (gating, gated attention, peri-layernorm, regularization) to address the challenges of dynamic layer skipping.
    *   The paper is clearly written and presents the ideas in a logical fashion.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The main weakness is the **negative experimental result**. The method doesn't outperform simpler baselines.  While the authors acknowledge this upfront, it significantly diminishes the paper's immediate practical impact.
    *   The authors did not investigate sufficiently larger-scale models, and claim that "anticipated benefits of this architectural prior may only become apparent at significantly larger model scales, where the redundancy of middle layers is more pronounced and the relative overhead of the gating mechanism is smaller."
    *   The paper could have benefited from a more thorough ablation study to understand why the proposed architecture failed to deliver the expected performance gains. It's unclear if the gating mechanism, gated attention, or regularization strategy was the primary bottleneck.
    *   The explanation of why the design choices failed to achieve the intended impact could be stronger. The discussion of the reasons for negative results feels somewhat incomplete. More analysis of the emergent sparsity pattern and its effect on feature representations could be very helpful.

*   **Potential Influence:**  Despite the negative results, the paper could still be influential in inspiring future research. The exploration of middle-layer skipping is a valuable direction, and the challenges identified in this paper could guide the development of improved techniques. In particular, the analysis of the failed experiment could be highly informative for others. The study could serve as a cautionary tale, highlighting the difficulty of translating insights from interpretability research into practical efficiency gains.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **5**. While the paper introduces a novel architecture motivated by sound principles and combines several interesting techniques, the lack of positive experimental results limits its impact. The paper's value primarily lies in its contribution to the body of knowledge on efficient Transformers and in its potential to inspire future research. However, the negative results and lack of sufficient scale investigation make this an ultimately un-impactful paper, which should have been investigated more thoroughly with larger scale models.

**Score: 5**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 5/10

### Unlasting: Unpaired Single-Cell Multi-Perturbation Estimation by Dual Conditional Diffusion Implicit Bridges
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21107v1)
- **Authors**: Changxi Chi, Jun Xia, Yufei Huang, Jingbo Zhou, Siyuan Li, Yunfan Liu, Chang Yu, Stan Z. Li
- **Abstract**: Estimating single-cell responses across various perturbations facilitates the identification of key genes and enhances drug screening, significantly boosting experimental efficiency. However, single-cell sequencing is a destructive process, making it impossible to capture the same cell's phenotype before and after perturbation. Consequently, data collected under perturbed and unperturbed conditions are inherently unpaired. Existing methods either attempt to forcibly pair unpaired data using random sampling, or neglect the inherent relationship between unperturbed and perturbed cells during the modeling. In this work, we propose a framework based on Dual Diffusion Implicit Bridges (DDIB) to learn the mapping between different data distributions, effectively addressing the challenge of unpaired data. We further interpret this framework as a form of data augmentation. We integrate gene regulatory network (GRN) information to propagate perturbation signals in a biologically meaningful way, and further incorporate a masking mechanism to predict silent genes, improving the quality of generated profiles. Moreover, gene expression under the same perturbation often varies significantly across cells, frequently exhibiting a bimodal distribution that reflects intrinsic heterogeneity. To capture this, we introduce a more suitable evaluation metric. We propose Unlasting, dual conditional diffusion models that overcome the problem of unpaired single-cell perturbation data and strengthen the model's insight into perturbations under the guidance of the GRN, with a dedicated mask model designed to improve generation quality by predicting silent genes. In addition, we introduce a biologically grounded evaluation metric that better reflects the inherent heterogeneity in single-cell responses.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Unlasting," a novel framework for predicting single-cell responses to various perturbations (genetic or molecular) in situations where pre- and post-perturbation data cannot be obtained from the same cells (i.e., unpaired data). Unlasting leverages Dual Diffusion Implicit Bridges (DDIB) to learn mappings between the distributions of perturbed and unperturbed cell states without requiring explicit pairing. The model incorporates gene regulatory network (GRN) information to guide the perturbation modeling and employs a mask model to predict silenced genes, enhancing the quality of the generated profiles. Furthermore, the authors address the issue of cellular heterogeneity by introducing a more suitable evaluation metric (Energy Distance (E-distance) and Earth Mover's Distance (EMD)) that captures distributional characteristics, surpassing traditional expectation-based measures. Experimental results on public datasets demonstrate that Unlasting effectively captures the diversity of single-cell perturbations and achieves state-of-the-art performance.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a few key novel contributions:
    *   **DDIB for Unpaired Single-Cell Perturbation:** The application of DDIB to the unpaired single-cell perturbation problem is a significant step. Existing methods either force pairing or ignore the inherent relationships.  DDIB provides a more principled approach to handle unpaired data by modeling the underlying distributions separately while maintaining a connection through a shared latent space. This is a definite advance.
    *   **GRN-Guided Perturbation Modeling:**  Incorporating GRN information is not entirely new, but Unlasting's approach seems to integrate this knowledge more effectively into the diffusion modeling process. This allows for a more biologically informed propagation of perturbation signals.
    *   **Mask Model for Silenced Genes:** The dedicated mask model to predict gene silencing is a valuable addition, addressing the sparsity of gene expression data and potentially improving the accuracy of the generated profiles. This is a practical and innovative technique.
    *   **Distribution-Aware Evaluation Metrics:**  The use of Energy Distance (E-distance) and Earth Mover's Distance (EMD) to assess the distributional alignment between predicted and true responses is a much-needed advancement. Given the acknowledged cellular heterogeneity and non-Gaussian expression patterns, relying on metrics that capture distributional similarity is crucial.

*   **Significance:** The significance of the paper stems from its potential to improve the accuracy and reliability of single-cell perturbation predictions.
    *   **Addressing a Real-World Problem:**  Single-cell perturbation data is inherently unpaired, making this a practical challenge for researchers. Unlasting addresses this limitation effectively.
    *   **Enhancing Biological Interpretability:** The GRN-guided approach adds a layer of biological interpretability to the predictions, which is essential for understanding the mechanisms underlying cellular responses to perturbations.
    *   **Improving Experimental Efficiency:**  Accurate perturbation prediction models can reduce the need for extensive and costly experiments. This will greatly boost experimental efficiency.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The technical approach (DDIB, GRN, mask model) is well-motivated and clearly explained.
    *   The introduction of distribution-aware evaluation metrics is a significant step forward.
    *   The experimental results on publicly available datasets demonstrate the effectiveness of the model.
    *   Ablation studies provide insights into the contributions of different components of the model.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper relies on publicly available datasets, but more rigorous validation on diverse datasets is still necessary to prove its generalizability.
    *   The complexity of the model might make it challenging to implement and train in certain settings.
    *   Although the results on different datasets show improvement, the datasets only consists of a few types of perturbations. It will be more impressive if the method can also handle complex single-cell perturbation experiments such as Perturb-Seq.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a more robust and biologically informed approach to single-cell perturbation prediction.  The integration of DDIB, GRN information, and a mask model, combined with the use of distribution-aware evaluation metrics, could become a standard practice in the field.  The work could also inspire further research on developing more sophisticated methods for handling unpaired data and capturing cellular heterogeneity.

*   **Justification for Score:**

While Unlasting presents a clear improvement over existing methods, it's important to acknowledge that DDIB, GRNs, and mask modeling, are existing techniques combined in a novel way. Given the novelty of the approach and significant advancement in performance compared with other models, and potential impact on improving experiment efficiency and data generation, a score of 8 is warranted.

**Score: 8**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 8/10

### IPFormer-VideoLLM: Enhancing Multi-modal Video Understanding for Multi-shot Scenes
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21116v1)
- **Authors**: Yujia Liang, Jile Jiao, Zhicheng Wang, Xuetao Feng, Zixuan Ye, Yuan Wang, Hao Lu
- **Abstract**: Video Large Language Models (VideoLLMs) have demonstrated remarkable understanding capabilities, but are found struggling to tackle multi-shot scenarios,e.g., video clips with varying camera angles or scene changes. This challenge can render failures such as instance identity forgetting and key frame negligence. In this work, we first attribute the challenge to the lack of multi-shot annotations among existing datasets and therefore we introduce a new dataset termed MultiClip-Bench, featuring dense descriptions and instruction-based question-answering pairs tailored for multi-shot scenarios. We empirically find that the training set significantly boosts the multi-shot performance, while the testing benchmark provides a reliable measure of the model capability in multi-shot scenarios. By further analyzing and discovering that current models only encode instance features in a discrete or lossy manner, at the risk of missing identity information, we then contribute a new model IPFormer-VideoLLM. Its key idea is the injection of instance-level features as instance prompts through an efficient attention-based connector. This allows for the aggregation of instance-specific information across scenes. Experiments demonstrate that our proposed dataset and model not only enhance the multi-scene video understanding significantly, but also offer distinct advantages across various video benchmarks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "IPFormer-VideoLLM: Enhancing Multi-modal Video Understanding for Multi-shot Scenes" addresses the limitations of current Video Large Language Models (VideoLLMs) in handling multi-shot video scenarios (videos with varying camera angles or scene changes). The authors argue that existing models struggle with instance identity preservation and keyframe neglect in these scenarios due to data limitations and model architecture constraints. To address this, they introduce:

1.  **MultiClip-Bench:** A new dataset specifically designed for multi-shot video understanding, featuring dense descriptions and instruction-based question-answering pairs.

2.  **IPFormer-VideoLLM:** A new model architecture that injects instance-level features as instance prompts using an attention-based connector. This allows for better aggregation of instance-specific information across scenes.

The paper demonstrates that the proposed dataset and model enhance multi-scene video understanding significantly, improving performance on both existing benchmarks and the new MultiClip-Bench.  The model also reduces token count, training time, and boosts inference speed.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper introduces two key novelties: a new dataset tailored for a specific and challenging video understanding task (multi-shot scenes) and a model architecture that leverages instance prompts to better handle identity preservation across scene changes. The idea of using instance prompts to guide attention is interesting, although the technical implementation builds upon existing attention mechanisms. The contribution of dataset, while incremental in dataset creation, address a particular gap and has value.

*   **Significance:** The limitations of current VideoLLMs in handling multi-shot scenes is a valid and important problem. The MultiClip-Bench dataset fills a gap in the available resources for training and evaluating models on this task.  The IPFormer-VideoLLM model offers a promising approach to improving performance in these challenging scenarios, demonstrating significant improvements over existing methods. The focus on reducing token count and improving efficiency is also a valuable contribution, addressing a practical constraint for real-time applications. The paper contributes meaningfully towards improving video understanding.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Problem Definition:**  The paper clearly identifies and articulates the challenges associated with multi-shot video understanding.
    *   **Dataset Contribution:** The MultiClip-Bench dataset is a valuable addition to the field, providing a benchmark specifically designed for this task.  The detailed annotation process and QA generation are a strength.
    *   **Model Design:** The IPFormer-VideoLLM architecture offers a plausible solution to the identified challenges, with experimental results supporting its effectiveness.
    *   **Experimental Evaluation:** The paper provides a comprehensive experimental evaluation, demonstrating the model's performance on both existing benchmarks and the new dataset.  The ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of different components.
    *   **Efficiency:** The work also addresses efficiency, which is an important consideration for real-world deployments.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Architectural Novelty:** While the idea of instance prompts is interesting, the underlying architecture is based on existing VideoLLM frameworks.  The novelty lies more in the application of this concept.
    *   **Dataset Scale:** While valuable, the dataset's size (particularly the 2.75k test set) may be a limitation, and future research could benefit from a larger dataset.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in video understanding, particularly in the area of multi-shot video analysis. The MultiClip-Bench dataset could become a standard benchmark for this task, and the IPFormer-VideoLLM architecture provides a valuable starting point for developing more robust and efficient models. It could influence the development of better video understanding in real-world applications like surveillance and video editing.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of video understanding, addressing a specific and important challenge. The introduction of the MultiClip-Bench dataset and the IPFormer-VideoLLM model are both valuable contributions. The weaknesses, mainly the relatively incremental architectural novelty and the dataset scale, prevent a higher score. Overall, the paper is well-written, thoroughly evaluated, and has the potential to influence future research in the area.
Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Learning to See in the Extremely Dark
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21132v1)
- **Authors**: Hai Jiang, Binhao Guan, Zhen Liu, Xiaohong Liu, Jian Yu, Zheng Liu, Songchen Han, Shuaicheng Liu
- **Abstract**: Learning-based methods have made promising advances in low-light RAW image enhancement, while their capability to extremely dark scenes where the environmental illuminance drops as low as 0.0001 lux remains to be explored due to the lack of corresponding datasets. To this end, we propose a paired-to-paired data synthesis pipeline capable of generating well-calibrated extremely low-light RAW images at three precise illuminance ranges of 0.01-0.1 lux, 0.001-0.01 lux, and 0.0001-0.001 lux, together with high-quality sRGB references to comprise a large-scale paired dataset named See-in-the-Extremely-Dark (SIED) to benchmark low-light RAW image enhancement approaches. Furthermore, we propose a diffusion-based framework that leverages the generative ability and intrinsic denoising property of diffusion models to restore visually pleasing results from extremely low-SNR RAW inputs, in which an Adaptive Illumination Correction Module (AICM) and a color consistency loss are introduced to ensure accurate exposure correction and color restoration. Extensive experiments on the proposed SIED and publicly available benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of our method. The code and dataset are available at https://github.com/JianghaiSCU/SIED.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of low-light RAW image enhancement (LLIE) in extremely dark conditions (as low as 0.0001 lux). The authors tackle the lack of appropriate training data by introducing a new paired-to-paired data synthesis pipeline to generate a large-scale dataset called See-in-the-Extremely-Dark (SIED). This dataset includes well-calibrated low-light RAW images at three precise illuminance ranges alongside high-quality sRGB references. The paper also presents a diffusion-based framework for LLIE, incorporating an Adaptive Illumination Correction Module (AICM) and a color consistency loss to ensure accurate exposure correction and color restoration.  Experiments on the SIED dataset and publicly available benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper has several novel aspects. First, the data synthesis pipeline for generating realistic extremely low-light RAW images paired with sRGB references is a significant contribution. Most existing datasets don't reach these extreme illuminance levels and can suffer from inaccuracies due to long exposure times in real-world capture. The paired-to-paired approach helps to better calibrate the data.  The Adaptive Illumination Correction Module (AICM) is another novel element, offering a way to handle exposure correction within a diffusion framework without relying on explicit exposure information from reference images. The integration of color consistency loss to guide the diffusion model in generating accurate color mapping is also a novel addition.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a real problem in LLIE: the lack of high-quality training data for extremely dark scenes. The SIED dataset could become a valuable resource for the community. The proposed diffusion-based framework offers a promising direction for tackling the challenges of noise, detail recovery, and color accuracy in these scenarios.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Dataset:** The SIED dataset fills a gap in the existing LLIE resources. The careful data synthesis and calibration contribute to its value.
    *   **Method:** The diffusion-based framework with AICM and color consistency loss is a well-designed approach, addressing key challenges in LLIE.
    *   **Experiments:**  The results on both the SIED dataset and public benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed method.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Data Synthesis Pipeline Dependence:**  The performance of any method trained on SIED will be bounded by the realism of the data synthesis. There is a risk that the model will learn synthesis artifacts, even with the improvements made over prior techniques. How the synthetic nature of the data affects the model's generalization to real, extremely low-light images needs to be addressed more comprehensively. Although the authors performed experiments on real images and showed promising results, the number of such images is insufficient.
    *   **Computational Cost:** Diffusion models are computationally expensive.  While the paper mentions the implementation details, a more detailed discussion of the runtime performance would be valuable.
    *   **AICM Complexity:** While the AICM is novel, the description of its architecture could be more detailed. Also, it's not explicitly clarified whether there are other illumination correction techniques employed in conjunction with AICM.
*   **Potential Impact:** The SIED dataset and the proposed method have the potential to advance research in LLIE for extremely dark environments. It offers a basis for developing more effective algorithms for night-time surveillance, autonomous driving, and other applications.

**Rationale for Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field by tackling the data scarcity problem in extremely low-light RAW image enhancement. The proposed dataset and method are both novel and show promising results. Although the reliance on data synthesis and computational cost are valid concerns, the authors have carefully addressed them. Overall, the paper represents a well-executed and valuable contribution to the field.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### How Good Are Synthetic Requirements ? Evaluating LLM-Generated Datasets for AI4RE
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21138v1)
- **Authors**: Abdelkarim El-Hajjami, Camille Salinesi
- **Abstract**: The shortage of publicly available, labeled requirements datasets remains a major barrier to advancing Artificial Intelligence for Requirements Engineering (AI4RE). While Large Language Models offer promising capabilities for synthetic data generation, systematic approaches to control and optimize the quality of generated requirements remain underexplored. This paper presents Synthline v1, an enhanced Product Line approach for generating synthetic requirements data that extends our earlier v0 version with advanced generation strategies and curation techniques. We investigate four research questions assessing how prompting strategies, automated prompt optimization, and post-generation curation affect data quality across four classification tasks: defect detection, functional vs. non-functional, quality vs. non-quality, and security vs. non-security. Our evaluation shows that multi-sample prompting significantly boosts both utility and diversity over single-sample generation, with F1-score gains from 6 to 44 points. The use of PACE (Prompt Actor-Critic Editing) for automated prompt optimization yields task-dependent results, greatly improving functional classification (+32.5 points) but reducing performance on others. Interestingly, similarity-based curation improves diversity but often harms classification performance, indicating that some redundancy may help ML models. Most importantly, our results show that synthetic requirements can match or outperform human-authored ones for specific tasks, with synthetic data surpassing human data for security (+7.8 points) and defect classification (+15.4 points). These findings offer practical insights for AI4RE and chart a viable path to mitigating dataset scarcity through systematic synthetic generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the lack of publicly available labeled datasets for AI in Requirements Engineering (AI4RE) by exploring the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) for synthetic data generation. The authors present Synthline v1, an improved product line approach for generating synthetic requirements data. They investigate the impact of prompting strategies, prompt optimization using PACE (Prompt Actor-Critic Editing), and post-generation curation on synthetic data quality across four requirements classification tasks: defects detection, functional vs. non-functional, quality vs. non-quality, and security vs. non-security. The results show that multi-sample prompting improves utility and diversity, PACE provides task-dependent improvements, and similarity-based curation can improve diversity but often hurts classification performance.  Notably, synthetic requirements can match or surpass human-authored requirements for security and defects classification.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper builds upon the authors' previous work on Synthline, which itself was a novel contribution. The advancements in Synthline v1, including PACE integration and multi-sample prompting, represent incremental but significant improvements. The application of PACE to synthetic data generation for AI4RE appears to be novel. The finding that similarity-based curation can be detrimental is also a non-trivial contribution. The paper’s claim of first systematically applying LLM-based synthetic data generation *in the context of RE* appears supported.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical bottleneck in AI4RE research: the scarcity of labeled data. Demonstrating that synthetic data can be a viable alternative, even surpassing human-authored data in specific tasks, is highly significant. This could significantly reduce the dependency on limited and potentially biased real-world datasets. This contribution has the potential to lower the barrier to entry for researchers in the field and promote faster progress. The paper provides actionable insights and a concrete, open-access tool (Synthline v1) for the AI4RE community. The negative finding related to similarity-based curation is equally important as it redirects efforts away from naive approaches.  However, the evaluation is limited to English, certain domains, and a subset of RE tasks. The long-term impact depends on the adoption and further development by other researchers.

* **Strengths:**
    *   Well-defined research questions.
    *   Systematic experimental design with controlled variables.
    *   Use of established real-world datasets for comparison.
    *   Open-access implementation for reproducibility.
    *   Clear presentation of results and discussion of limitations.
    *   Focus on both diversity and utility metrics.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited generalizability due to language, domain, and task constraints.
    *   PACE parameter configuration wasn't empirically optimized for the task.
    *   Similarity threshold for curation was arbitrarily chosen.
    *   Reliance on a single LLM (GPT-4.1 nano) could introduce model-specific biases.
    *   The relatively small size of the real datasets.
    *   Limited evaluation of other curation techniques, it mainly focuses on similarity.

* **Justification for the score:**

The paper presents valuable advancements in synthetic data generation for AI4RE, addressing a crucial problem in the field. The systematic approach, the integration of PACE, and the non-trivial findings regarding curation contribute to the existing body of knowledge. While the limitations regarding generalizability and experimental design choices temper the impact, the potential to democratize AI4RE research through accessible, high-quality synthetic data is significant. Therefore, the paper deserves a score that reflects its real contribution despite the limitations.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 7/10

### Geometry and Perception Guided Gaussians for Multiview-consistent 3D Generation from a Single Image
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21152v1)
- **Authors**: Pufan Li, Bi'an Du, Wei Hu
- **Abstract**: Generating realistic 3D objects from single-view images requires natural appearance, 3D consistency, and the ability to capture multiple plausible interpretations of unseen regions. Existing approaches often rely on fine-tuning pretrained 2D diffusion models or directly generating 3D information through fast network inference or 3D Gaussian Splatting, but their results generally suffer from poor multiview consistency and lack geometric detail. To takle these issues, we present a novel method that seamlessly integrates geometry and perception priors without requiring additional model training to reconstruct detailed 3D objects from a single image. Specifically, we train three different Gaussian branches initialized from the geometry prior, perception prior and Gaussian noise, respectively. The geometry prior captures the rough 3D shapes, while the perception prior utilizes the 2D pretrained diffusion model to enhance multiview information. Subsequently, we refine 3D Gaussian branches through mutual interaction between geometry and perception priors, further enhanced by a reprojection-based strategy that enforces depth consistency. Experiments demonstrate the higher-fidelity reconstruction results of our method, outperforming existing methods on novel view synthesis and 3D reconstruction, demonstrating robust and consistent 3D object generation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel method for generating consistent 3D objects from a single 2D image, aiming to overcome limitations in existing approaches regarding multiview consistency and geometric detail.  The method leverages 3D Gaussian Splatting as its 3D representation and integrates geometry and perception priors without requiring additional training. Three Gaussian branches, initialized from geometry, perception, and noise, respectively, are mutually refined using a co-SDS loss and a reprojection-based depth consistency strategy. Experiments demonstrate improved reconstruction fidelity and multiview consistency compared to existing methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the integrated approach using multiple Gaussian branches guided by different priors and the mutual refinement mechanism (co-SDS). The use of Gaussian splatting itself isn't new (DreamGaussian), nor are the individual priors (geometry retrieval and 2D diffusion models).  However, the specific combination, the co-SDS loss formulation for branch interaction, and the reprojection loss for depth consistency represent a novel contribution.

*   **Significance:**  Single-view 3D reconstruction is a fundamental problem in computer vision. The paper's ability to generate higher-fidelity and more consistent 3D models from a single image has potential impact in areas like virtual reality, augmented reality, and 3D content creation. The ablation studies provide insights into the importance of the different components, which is beneficial for future research.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and motivation.
    *   Well-defined architecture and training strategy.
    *   Ablation studies support the effectiveness of the proposed components.
    *   Improved performance compared to existing methods on the Google Scanned Objects dataset, as evidenced by both quantitative and qualitative results.
    *   The paper addresses a clear weakness of previous gaussian splatting approaches which suffer from inconsistent views.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Reliance on the Google Scanned Objects dataset, which might not fully represent the diversity of real-world objects.
    *   Limited discussion of computational complexity.  How does the runtime scale compared to DreamGaussian, particularly during the optimization phase?
    *   The qualitative results, while showing improvements, still exhibit artifacts in some cases.
    *   While the core idea is interesting, the individual modules (Zero123, depth estimation using Marigold, 3D shape retreival) are taken from existing works which reduces the impact of the novel component which is the co-SDS loss and multi branch approach.
    *   There is very little justification for the precise combination of loss functions used in the framework, and how this was tested.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper can influence future research on single-view 3D reconstruction by demonstrating the benefits of integrating multiple priors and employing a mutual refinement mechanism. The co-SDS loss could be adapted to other 3D generative models. However, the impact will be limited if the method is not scalable or generalizable to more complex and diverse datasets.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of 6. The paper presents a novel and reasonably effective approach to a relevant problem. It builds on existing techniques but introduces a unique combination of priors and a mutual refinement mechanism. However, the reliance on existing modules, the limited experimental evaluation, and the lack of a comprehensive discussion of computational costs limit its overall impact. It represents a solid incremental improvement rather than a paradigm shift.

### Compressed and Smooth Latent Space for Text Diffusion Modeling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21170v1)
- **Authors**: Viacheslav Meshchaninov, Egor Chimbulatov, Alexander Shabalin, Aleksandr Abramov, Dmitry Vetrov
- **Abstract**: Autoregressive language models dominate modern text generation, yet their sequential nature introduces fundamental limitations: decoding is slow, and maintaining global coherence remains challenging. Diffusion models offer a promising alternative by enabling parallel generation and flexible control; however, their application to text generation is hindered by the high dimensionality of token-level representations. We introduce Cosmos, a novel approach to text generation that operates entirely in a compressed, smooth latent space tailored specifically for diffusion. This space is learned using an autoencoder trained simultaneously for token-level reconstruction and alignment with frozen activations from a pretrained language encoder, providing robust semantic grounding and enabling effective perturbation-based augmentations. Empirically, we demonstrate that text representations can be compressed by $8\times$ while maintaining generation quality comparable to token-level diffusion models. Furthermore, increasing the latent sequence length allows Cosmos to surpass both diffusion-based and autoregressive baselines. We evaluate Cosmos on four diverse generative tasks including story generation, question generation, summarization, and detoxification and compare it with various generative paradigms. Cosmos achieves comparable or superior generation quality while offering more than $2\times$ faster inference.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces COSMOS, a novel approach to text generation using diffusion models operating in a compressed and smooth latent space.  It addresses the limitations of autoregressive language models (slow decoding, difficulty in maintaining global coherence) and token-level diffusion models (high dimensionality).  COSMOS uses an autoencoder, trained with specific objectives that enforce smoothness and robustness, to map text into a compact latent space. This latent space is then used for diffusion-based generation. The paper shows empirically that COSMOS can compress text representations significantly (up to 8x) while maintaining or even exceeding the generation quality of token-level diffusion models and autoregressive baselines. COSMOS also demonstrates faster inference speeds. The approach is evaluated on story generation, question generation, summarization, and detoxification tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of performing diffusion in a compressed, "smooth," latent space for *text* generation is novel. While latent diffusion is well-established in the image domain, its application to text requires addressing specific challenges, notably the discrete nature of text and the importance of semantic coherence. The authors' training recipe for the autoencoder, focusing on smoothness, robustness through perturbations, and alignment with a pre-trained language encoder, constitutes a valuable contribution. The specific combination of MSE loss for encoder activation preservation, random masking, Gaussian noising, and latent dropout is a novel and effective method for learning a diffusable latent space.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the potential to overcome key limitations of autoregressive models. Faster inference is a major advantage. The demonstrated ability to achieve comparable or superior generation quality with significantly compressed representations is impressive. This could enable more efficient training and deployment of text generation models. The paper challenges the prevailing assumption that token-level representations are necessary for high-quality text generation.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive evaluation:** The paper evaluates COSMOS on diverse generation tasks and compares it with strong baselines.
    *   **Ablation studies:** Detailed ablation studies demonstrate the impact of each component of the autoencoder training procedure. These studies help to understand the importance of smoothness and robustness in the latent space.
    *   **Analysis of latent space properties:** The paper investigates the properties of the learned latent space, providing insights into why the proposed approach is effective.  The experiments examining the impact of interpolation in the latent space and decoder robustness are particularly insightful.
    *   **Faster Inference:** The reported faster inference times make a compelling case for the practical benefits of this approach.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Dependency on a frozen pre-trained encoder:** The approach still relies on a pre-trained language model (BERT) for initial feature extraction. This raises questions about the generalizability of the method to settings where such a powerful pre-trained model is not available or desirable.
    *   **Limited exploration of hyperparameter sensitivity:** Although there are ablations, a more exhaustive exploration of hyperparameter sensitivity (e.g., varying the level of compression) would strengthen the findings.  While the paper explores different numbers of latent vectors, a full sweep of the embedding dimension isn't performed.
    *   **Architectural Simplifications:** The method simplifies the text generation task by using a decoder mirror in the auto-encoder. While this approach enables efficiency, it's unclear whether similar methods can be extended to tasks requiring a finer level of control or a more complex generation scheme.
    *   **Limited novel architectural components:** The primary novelty lies in the *training* recipe for the autoencoder, not in the architecture itself. While effective, this focus might limit the longer-term impact of the work.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in text generation by promoting the use of latent-space diffusion models. It could also lead to the development of more efficient and scalable text generation systems. The findings about the importance of smoothness and robustness in latent spaces are valuable for the broader field of generative modeling.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper has some limitations, the novelty and significance of the approach, supported by strong empirical results and insightful analysis, warrant a high score. The faster inference times, the compression capabilities, and the demonstrated generation quality make this a compelling contribution. Although the reliance on a frozen BERT encoder and the architectural simplifications are factors that slightly limit the score, the results clearly demonstrate the viability of diffusion models operating within compressed, smooth latent spaces for text.

- **Score**: 8/10

### Task-Aware KV Compression For Cost-Effective Long Video Understanding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21184v1)
- **Authors**: Minghao Qin, Yan Shu, Peitian Zhang, Kun Lun, Huaying Yuan, Juenjie Zhou, Shitao Xiao, Bo Zhao, Zheng Liu
- **Abstract**: Long-video understanding (LVU) remains a severe challenge for existing multimodal large language models (MLLMs), primarily due to the prohibitive computational cost. Recent approaches have explored KV compression to mitigate this issue, but they often suffer from significant information loss at high compression ratios. In this paper, we introduce Video-X^2L, which flexibly preserves critical video information for each LVU task. Video-X^2L involves two key operations. The first one is called bi-level KV compression. During the MLLM's pre-filling stage, Video-X^2L generates two types of compressed KVs: low-compression KVs (L-KVs) to capture fine-grained video details and high-compression KVs (H-KVs) to offer compact video representations. The second one is called selective KV re-loading. During the MLLM's decoding stage, Video-X^2L selectively re-loads L-KVs for the most critical video chunks while using H-KVs for other less important ones. This allows the MLLM to fully utilize task-specific information while maintaining the overall compactness. Video-X^2L is simple yet effective: it is free from additional training and directly compatible with existing KV-compressible MLLMs. We evaluate Video-X^2L with a variety of popular LVU benchmarks, including VideoMME, MLVU, LongVideoBench, and VNBench. Our experiment result shows that Video-X^2L outperforms existing KV-compression methods by a huge advantage while substantially saving the computation cost.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Video-X2L, a framework for cost-effective long video understanding (LVU) using bi-level KV compression and selective KV re-loading. During the MLLM's pre-filling stage, Video-X2L generates low-compression KVs (L-KVs) for fine-grained details and high-compression KVs (H-KVs) for compact video representations. During decoding, it selectively reloads L-KVs for critical video chunks and H-KVs for less important ones. It aims to improve MLLM performance on LVU tasks by preserving crucial information while reducing computational costs. The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of Video-X2L on several LVU benchmarks, showing improvements over existing KV-compression methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Novel Approach:** The core idea of bi-level KV compression with selective re-loading based on task relevance appears novel.  Existing methods often apply uniform compression or pre-LLM token reduction, which can lead to significant information loss. Video-X2L's approach tries to dynamically balance compression and information retention.
    *   **Training-Free:**  The method's ability to work without additional training and be compatible with existing KV-compressible MLLMs is a significant advantage, making it easily adoptable.
    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The experiments are comprehensive, using multiple popular LVU benchmarks (VideoMME, MLVU, LongVideoBench, VNBench). The ablation studies add further insights into the components of the framework.
    *   **Performance Gains:** Results show consistent performance improvement compared to baselines, particularly in MLVU and LongVideoBench, while also demonstrating cost-effectiveness. The NIAH evaluation highlights the framework's suitability for very long videos.
    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and structured, making it relatively easy to understand the proposed method and the experimental setup.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Relevance Oracle:**  The performance of Video-X2L depends heavily on the accuracy of the relevance oracle. While the paper shows results with different oracles, the reliance on an external estimator is a potential bottleneck and limits the end-to-end optimality of the method. A poor relevance oracle can lead to the selection of irrelevant or redundant KVs and impact the performance.
    *   **Incremental Improvement:**  While improvements are demonstrated, the reported margins over Video-XL are often moderate (a few percentage points). The question is whether this improvement justifies the added complexity of bi-level compression and selective re-loading.
    *   **Limited Novelty of Individual Components:**  KV compression and attention-based importance estimation are not new ideas. The novelty lies in combining them in this specific bi-level selective re-loading framework.
    *   **Scalability:** The efficiency gains are mostly focused on the decoding stage. It would be good to explore how to accelerate the pre-filling stage with bi-level compression in future researches
    *   **Dependency on existing KV-compressible MLLMs:** The method can only be applied to the architectures that has KV compression design, which may make it have limited applications.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The work addresses a critical challenge in LVU: computational cost.
    *   The proposed framework could serve as a foundation for future research on adaptive and task-aware compression techniques in MLLMs.
    *   By making LVU more accessible, it could facilitate the development of more sophisticated video-based applications.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a well-executed and empirically supported method for cost-effective long video understanding.  However, the reliance on a relevance oracle, the incremental nature of the improvements, and the limited novelty in some of its components temper its overall impact. While the combination of techniques is novel and leads to tangible gains, the dependence on external modules and the modest improvements prevent it from being a truly transformative contribution.

### Prompt-Guided Turn-Taking Prediction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21191v1)
- **Authors**: Koji Inoue, Mikey Elmers, Yahui Fu, Zi Haur Pang, Divesh Lala, Keiko Ochi, Tatsuya Kawahara
- **Abstract**: Turn-taking prediction models are essential components in spoken dialogue systems and conversational robots. Recent approaches leverage transformer-based architectures to predict speech activity continuously and in real-time. In this study, we propose a novel model that enables turn-taking prediction to be dynamically controlled via textual prompts. This approach allows intuitive and explicit control through instructions such as "faster" or "calmer" adapting dynamically to conversational partners and contexts. The proposed model builds upon a transformer-based voice activity projection (VAP) model, incorporating textual prompt embeddings into both channel-wise transformers and a cross-channel transformer. We evaluated the feasibility of our approach using over 950 hours of human-human spoken dialogue data. Since textual prompt data for the proposed approach was not available in existing datasets, we utilized a large language model (LLM) to generate synthetic prompt sentences. Experimental results demonstrated that the proposed model improved prediction accuracy and effectively varied turn-taking timing behaviors according to the textual prompts.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel method for dynamically controlling turn-taking behavior in a dialogue system using textual prompts. The proposed model extends the Voice Activity Projection (VAP) architecture by incorporating prompt embeddings into both channel-wise and cross-channel transformers. This allows for intuitive control over turn-taking timing through instructions such as "faster" or "calmer." The authors evaluated their approach using a large Japanese spoken dialogue dataset and synthetically generated prompt sentences using a Large Language Model (LLM). Experimental results show improved prediction accuracy and effective variation in turn-taking timing based on the prompts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The explicit use of textual prompts to control turn-taking behavior is the main novelty. While previous works have explored turn-taking prediction and control, they haven't directly linked textual prompts to modulate the timing and style of responses. The integration of prompts within a VAP architecture is a specific implementation, adding to the overall novelty. However, leveraging LLMs for synthetic data generation is becoming increasingly common, so the prompt generation part itself isn't ground-breaking.

*   **Significance:** The ability to dynamically adjust turn-taking behavior based on prompts has the potential to improve the naturalness and adaptability of dialogue systems. This could lead to more personalized and engaging interactions, especially in scenarios where adapting to user characteristics (e.g., age, personality) is crucial. The work also contributes to research on human-robot interaction, enabling robots to participate in conversations more effectively.  It provides a tangible method for directly manipulating a key aspect of dialogue.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper clearly presents the proposed method and its architecture.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate improved prediction accuracy compared to the baseline VAP model.
    *   The qualitative evaluation provides evidence that the model responds to different textual prompts as intended.
    *   The paper addresses limitations and ethical considerations, demonstrating awareness of potential issues.
    *   The architecture and training methodology are grounded in previous, well-established work.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The reliance on synthetically generated prompt sentences raises concerns about the generalizability of the results to real-world scenarios where prompts might be more varied or ambiguous. The LLM might introduce biases or generate unrealistic instructions.
    *   The evaluation primarily focuses on quantitative metrics like VAP loss and balanced accuracy, but lacks a thorough user study to assess the perceived naturalness and quality of the generated dialogues with different prompts.
    *   The paper doesn't provide a detailed analysis of the generated prompts themselves, such as their diversity, complexity, or correlation with specific turn-taking patterns. It would be helpful to better understand the characteristics of the generated data.
    *   The lack of comparisons to other methods of controllable dialogue generation (e.g., fine-tuning for specific personas) weakens the claims of its relative effectiveness.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's contribution is primarily focused on improving the control of turn-taking behavior through text prompts, which could lead to improvements in spoken dialog systems and conversational robots. The approach presents a potential solution for improving the naturalness of AI-driven conversations.  However, the limitations related to synthetic prompts and lack of user studies temper the overall impact.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a novel approach and demonstrates improved performance, several limitations temper its impact. The reliance on synthetic data, the absence of user studies, and a need for further detailed analysis of the quality and diversity of generated prompts lead to a lower score. Considering the paper's novelty and impact, along with the described limitations, a score of **6** is warranted. While it's a worthwhile contribution to the area of controllable dialog generation, more validation is necessary.

### BitMark for Infinity: Watermarking Bitwise Autoregressive Image Generative Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21209v1)
- **Authors**: Louis Kerner, Michel Meintz, Bihe Zhao, Franziska Boenisch, Adam Dziedzic
- **Abstract**: State-of-the-art text-to-image models like Infinity generate photorealistic images at an unprecedented speed. These models operate in a bitwise autoregressive manner over a discrete set of tokens that is practically infinite in size. However, their impressive generative power comes with a growing risk: as their outputs increasingly populate the Internet, they are likely to be scraped and reused as training data-potentially by the very same models. This phenomenon has been shown to lead to model collapse, where repeated training on generated content, especially from the models' own previous versions, causes a gradual degradation in performance. A promising mitigation strategy is watermarking, which embeds human-imperceptible yet detectable signals into generated images-enabling the identification of generated content. In this work, we introduce BitMark, a robust bitwise watermarking framework for Infinity. Our method embeds a watermark directly at the bit level of the token stream across multiple scales (also referred to as resolutions) during Infinity's image generation process. Our bitwise watermark subtly influences the bits to preserve visual fidelity and generation speed while remaining robust against a spectrum of removal techniques. Furthermore, it exhibits high radioactivity, i.e., when watermarked generated images are used to train another image generative model, this second model's outputs will also carry the watermark. The radioactive traces remain detectable even when only fine-tuning diffusion or image autoregressive models on images watermarked with our BitMark. Overall, our approach provides a principled step toward preventing model collapse in image generative models by enabling reliable detection of generated outputs.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "BitMark for Infinity: Watermarking Bitwise Autoregressive Image Generative Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces BitMark, a novel watermarking framework specifically designed for bitwise autoregressive image generative models like Infinity. Unlike previous watermarking techniques, BitMark operates directly at the bit level of the token stream during the image generation process. This approach aims to achieve robustness and preserve image quality and generation speed.  The watermark is embedded by subtly influencing bits towards a "green list" of bit sequences. Detection is based on counting the occurrence of these sequences in re-encoded images. The authors demonstrate BitMark's robustness against various attacks, including standard image manipulations and dedicated watermark removal techniques, and showcase its "radioactivity," meaning that models trained on watermarked images also produce watermarked outputs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

*   **Strengths:** The most significant novelty lies in the bitwise watermarking approach tailored to the specific architecture of Infinity. Existing watermarking methods for diffusion models are not directly applicable, and token-level watermarking proves sub-optimal in this setting. Addressing this gap is a meaningful contribution.  The radioactivity of the watermark is also a valuable property.
*   **Weaknesses:** The core concept of biasing token/bit probabilities based on "green" and "red" lists is inspired by watermarking techniques for language models, which tempers the originality. The core algorithm isn't especially complex (dividing and biasing a stream of bits based on some conditions).

**Significance and Impact:**

*   **Strengths:** The paper addresses a critical problem: preventing model collapse due to models being retrained on their own generated data. By enabling the detection of generated images, BitMark provides a mechanism for filtering such data from training sets. The practical demonstration of radioactivity is significant, implying the potential for tracing generated content across model lineages. Given the increased use of commercial closed-source models like Infinity, any mechanism that provides even partial traceability is extremely valuable. The results from removing attacks and novel bit flipper attacks show decent robustness. The paper is well-motivated, and the experiments appear thorough.
*   **Weaknesses:** The analysis of different scales is insightful, but there is no clear discussion of its effectiveness. Some of the robustness claims, while supported by experimental results, might be fragile. As with any watermark, an adaptive attacker with knowledge of the specific watermarking method could potentially devise more effective removal techniques. While BitMark shows reasonably good robustness, some of the attacks cause drops in accuracy that could be unacceptable for certain use cases. Also, there is more focus on removing the water mark but less on the visual quality and perception. The reliance on private keys may provide more security, however this comes with a trade-off with complexity.

**Justification for the Score:**

BitMark represents a solid engineering contribution by adapting watermarking techniques to a relatively new type of generative model architecture. Its strengths lie in the practical demonstration of its radioactivity and robustness, and the addressing of a real-world problem with generative models. The novelty is good, but it builds on existing concepts. Despite these limitations, the paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by demonstrating a practical and reasonably robust watermarking method for bitwise autoregressive image generation. The significance lies in tackling the model collapse problem in a closed model like infinity.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### $T^3$: Multi-level Tree-based Automatic Program Repair with Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21211v1)
- **Authors**: Quanming Liu, Xupeng Bu, Zhichao Yan, Ru Li
- **Abstract**: Automatic Program Repair (APR) is a core technology in software development and maintenance, with aims to enable automated defect repair with minimal human intervention. In recent years, the substantial advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) and the Chain-of-Thought (CoT) techniques have significantly enhanced the reasoning capabilities of these models. However, due to the complex logic and multi-step reasoning ability needed, the application of CoT techniques in the APR domain remains insufficient. This study systematically evaluates the performance of several common CoT techniques in APR tasks and proposes an innovative framework $T^3$, which integrates the powerful reasoning capabilities of LLMs with tree search, effectively improving the precision of generating candidate repair solutions. Furthermore, $T^3$ provides valuable guidance for optimizing sample selection and repair strategies in APR tasks, establishing a robust framework for achieving efficient automated debugging.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces T³, a novel multi-level tree-based framework for automatic program repair (APR) that leverages large language models (LLMs). T³ decomposes the repair process into four stages: sample retrieval, cause analysis, repair plan generation, and patch generation.  A key innovation is the "Forest of Thinking" approach used in cause analysis and repair plan generation, which constructs multiple parallel reasoning trees to enhance comprehensiveness and robustness.  The framework also features a novel result selection method that balances diversity and consistency in the selected repair plans. Experiments demonstrate that T³ improves repair accuracy compared to existing LLM-based APR methods, including those based on Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel aspects. The multi-level tree-based reasoning structure ("Forest of Thinking") is a significant departure from traditional CoT methods.  The integration of tree search with LLMs in the APR domain is relatively new.  The result selection method, which balances diversity and consistency, is another valuable contribution. However, the individual components (LLMs, CoT, BM25 retrieval) are not novel in themselves; the novelty lies in their specific integration and application within T³.

*   **Significance:** The results show that T³ improves repair accuracy over existing methods, suggesting its potential to advance the state-of-the-art in automated debugging.  The ablation studies provide valuable insights into the contribution of each component, guiding future research in this area. The paper also offers valuable insights into optimizing sample selection and repair strategies within the APR domain.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The "Forest of Thinking" approach seems promising for exploring diverse reasoning paths.
    *   The experimental results convincingly demonstrate the improved performance of T³.
    *   The ablation studies offer useful insights into the importance of the different components.
    *   The framework is well-structured and clearly presented.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The paper relies on existing LLMs (gpt-3.5-turbo and gpt-4o-mini) and does not explore how to tailor LLMs specifically for APR, which could potentially lead to even greater performance gains.
    *   The reliance on the MODIT dataset, while standard, limits the generalizability of the results. Testing on more diverse and larger datasets would strengthen the claims.
    *   The case study, while illustrative, is limited to a single example. A more in-depth analysis of the types of bugs that T³ can and cannot repair would be valuable.
    *   While the method improves repair *accuracy*, the paper doesn't deeply explore other critical aspects of repair such as *plausibility* and *correctness with respect to all test cases*.
