### Domain Knowledge-Enhanced LLMs for Fraud and Concept Drift Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21443v1)
- **Authors**: Ali Şenol, Garima Agrawal, Huan Liu
- **Abstract**: Detecting deceptive conversations on dynamic platforms is increasingly difficult due to evolving language patterns and Concept Drift (CD)-i.e., semantic or topical shifts that alter the context or intent of interactions over time. These shifts can obscure malicious intent or mimic normal dialogue, making accurate classification challenging. While Large Language Models (LLMs) show strong performance in natural language tasks, they often struggle with contextual ambiguity and hallucinations in risk-sensitive scenarios. To address these challenges, we present a Domain Knowledge (DK)-Enhanced LLM framework that integrates pretrained LLMs with structured, task-specific insights to perform fraud and concept drift detection. The proposed architecture consists of three main components: (1) a DK-LLM module to detect fake or deceptive conversations; (2) a drift detection unit (OCDD) to determine whether a semantic shift has occurred; and (3) a second DK-LLM module to classify the drift as either benign or fraudulent. We first validate the value of domain knowledge using a fake review dataset and then apply our full framework to SEConvo, a multiturn dialogue dataset that includes various types of fraud and spam attacks. Results show that our system detects fake conversations with high accuracy and effectively classifies the nature of drift. Guided by structured prompts, the LLaMA-based implementation achieves 98% classification accuracy. Comparative studies against zero-shot baselines demonstrate that incorporating domain knowledge and drift awareness significantly improves performance, interpretability, and robustness in high-stakes NLP applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a Domain Knowledge (DK)-Enhanced LLM framework to improve fraud and concept drift detection in dynamic online conversations. The framework consists of three main components: a DK-LLM for initial fraud detection, an One-Class Concept Drift Detector (OCDD) to identify semantic shifts, and a second DK-LLM to classify the drift as benign or adversarial.  The authors validate their approach using a fake review dataset (Yelp) and the SEConvo dataset (multi-turn conversations) and compare performance across various LLMs (ChatGPT, Claude, LLaMA, DeepSeek) under zero-shot and domain-guided settings. The results show that incorporating domain knowledge significantly boosts performance, with LLaMA achieving the best results.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in the integration of domain knowledge and a two-stage LLM architecture to jointly detect fraud and concept drift in conversations. While LLMs have been used for fraud detection before, explicitly addressing the issue of concept drift and classifying its nature (benign or adversarial) using a structured, domain-aware approach is a valuable contribution. The use of OCDD for drift detection and the subsequent classification of the drift type using a second LLM is an interesting approach.
*   **Significance:** The significance stems from the practical implications of improving fraud detection in dynamic online environments. By better understanding and responding to concept drift, the framework can reduce false positives and missed detections.  The paper's findings are particularly relevant for high-stakes applications like cybersecurity and online marketplaces where accurate and interpretable fraud detection is crucial. Demonstrating that carefully crafted domain-specific prompts can dramatically improve LLM performance in these scenarios is an important takeaway.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the problem of fraud detection in dynamic conversational settings, emphasizing the importance of considering concept drift.
    *   **Well-Defined Framework:** The proposed framework is well-structured and modular, allowing for flexibility and adaptability.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors conduct a thorough evaluation, comparing different LLMs and benchmarking against traditional methods. The use of two datasets (Yelp and SEConvo) strengthens the evaluation.
    *   **Focus on Interpretability:** The emphasis on domain knowledge and structured prompting enhances interpretability, addressing a key concern in high-stakes applications of LLMs.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Dataset Size:** The SEConvo dataset is relatively small (400 conversations), which might limit the generalizability of the results.
    *   **Computational Cost:** The authors acknowledge the higher computational cost of LLM-based approaches compared to traditional methods. While acceptable in some contexts, it is a relevant limitation.
    *   **Prompt Engineering Dependency:** The framework's performance is highly dependent on the quality of the domain-specific prompts. While the paper describes how the prompts were developed, further investigation into prompt robustness and generalizability would be beneficial.
    *   **Lack of adaptive correction** The system operates in a feed-forward mode, without adaptive correction.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing a practical and effective framework for fraud detection in dynamic conversational settings. It demonstrates the value of combining LLMs with domain knowledge and statistical methods to address real-world challenges. The emphasis on interpretability and robustness is likely to resonate with practitioners in high-stakes domains.
*   **Rigour:** The paper is well-written and technically sound. The experimental design is appropriate, and the results are clearly presented and analyzed. The comparative analysis against baseline models strengthens the validity of the findings.

**Justification for Score:**

While the paper presents a valuable and innovative approach to fraud and concept drift detection, some limitations, particularly the dataset size and computational cost, prevent it from achieving a higher score. However, the clear problem definition, well-defined framework, comprehensive evaluation, focus on interpretability, and potential influence warrant a significant contribution score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Text2Cypher Across Languages: Evaluating Foundational Models Beyond English
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21445v1)
- **Authors**: Makbule Gulcin Ozsoy, William Tai
- **Abstract**: Recent advances in large language models have enabled natural language interfaces that translate user questions into database queries, such as Text2SQL, Text2SPARQL, and Text2Cypher. While these interfaces enhance database accessibility, most research today focuses solely on English, with limited evaluation in other languages. This paper investigates the performance of foundational LLMs on the Text2Cypher task across multiple languages. We create and release a multilingual test set by translating English questions into Spanish and Turkish while preserving the original Cypher queries, enabling fair cross-lingual comparison. We evaluate multiple foundational models using standardized prompts and metrics. Our results show a consistent performance pattern: highest on English, then Spanish, and lowest on Turkish. We attribute this to differences in training data availability and linguistic characteristics. Additionally, we explore the impact of translating task prompts into Spanish and Turkish. Results show little to no change in evaluation metrics, suggesting prompt translation has minor impact. Our findings highlight the need for more inclusive evaluation and development in multilingual query generation. Future work includes schema localization and fine-tuning across diverse languages.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Text2Cypher Across Languages: Evaluating Foundational Models Beyond English":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the lack of multilingual evaluation in the Text2Cypher task, which involves translating natural language questions into Cypher queries for graph databases. The authors create and release a multilingual test set by translating English questions from an existing dataset into Spanish and Turkish while keeping the Cypher queries the same.  They evaluate several foundational large language models (LLMs) on this translated dataset, comparing performance across the three languages using standardized prompts and metrics (ROUGE-L and Exact Match based on query execution). The results consistently show better performance on English, followed by Spanish, and then Turkish. The authors attribute this to differences in language resourced-ness and linguistic characteristics.  They also investigate the impact of translating the task prompts into Spanish and Turkish, finding that it has a minimal effect on the overall results. The paper concludes by highlighting the need for more inclusive evaluation and development in multilingual query generation and suggests future work including schema localization and fine-tuning across diverse languages.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies in its direct evaluation of LLMs on the Text2Cypher task *across multiple languages*.  While there has been some work in multilingual Text-to-SQL, the Text2Cypher task, especially evaluated on Turkish is a less-explored area. The creation and release of the translated test set are also a positive contribution. The idea of exploring the impact of translating prompts is interesting, though the results are somewhat unsurprising.
* **Significance:** The paper makes a significant contribution by highlighting the English-centric bias in LLM development for database query generation. It demonstrates that even strong foundational models exhibit a clear performance degradation when dealing with non-English languages. This observation is important because it underscores the need for more balanced datasets and training strategies to improve LLMs' cross-lingual capabilities, making graph databases more accessible to a wider range of users. Further, this evaluation stresses the importance of multilingual capabilities beyond basic translation in the context of complex tasks such as Cypher generation.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Statement:** The paper clearly identifies the gap in multilingual evaluation in the Text2Cypher domain.
    * **Well-Defined Methodology:** The creation of the translated dataset and the use of standardized prompts and metrics make the evaluation relatively fair and reproducible.
    * **Relevant Experiments:** The experiments directly address the research questions, investigating the impact of question language and prompt language.
    * **Practical Implications:** The findings have practical implications for developers of Text2Cypher systems, suggesting the need for more focus on multilingual support and better adaptation of LLMs to different language characteristics.
    * **Data Availability:** Releasing the translated dataset promotes further research in this area.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited Scope:** The study is limited to only two additional languages (Spanish and Turkish). While the choice is justified, exploring more diverse languages could provide a broader perspective.
    * **Prompt Engineering:** The paper uses one single prompt, while recent literature demonstrates the impact of prompt engineering on final results. A more comprehensive evaluation should explore a variety of prompting strategies, adapted to each language.
    * **Model Choice:** While the foundational models used are relevant, the rapid pace of LLM development means that newer and potentially more powerful models might have been used. Also, the inclusion of other non-English language models could provide a more holistic understanding of cross-lingual capabilities.
    * **Shallow Error Analysis:** The paper mentions some masking issues, but lacks a deeper qualitative analysis of the types of errors the models make in different languages.  Understanding these errors could provide valuable insights for improvement.  For example, what kind of grammatical constructions in Turkish are problematic? Do models struggle with specific entity types in Spanish?
    * **Minimal Impact of Prompt Translation:** The limited impact of prompt translation calls into question whether the experimental setup is truly isolating the impact of *question* language. The fact that the schema and, presumably, the underlying data remain in English is a confounding factor.
    * **Lack of Consideration of Linguistic Features:** The paper mentions linguistic differences but does not delve deeply into *how* these differences affect performance. For example, a more detailed analysis of how agglutination in Turkish affects Cypher generation would be beneficial.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of **6.5**. The paper makes a solid contribution by highlighting the multilingual gap in Text2Cypher and providing a valuable dataset for further research. The methodology is sound, and the results are clearly presented. However, the scope is somewhat limited, the error analysis is shallow, and the conclusions drawn from the prompt translation experiments are not particularly strong. Further exploration of prompt engineering, linguistic features and schema localization would considerably increase the paper's impact.

Score: 6.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Controllable 3D Placement of Objects with Scene-Aware Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21446v1)
- **Authors**: Mohamed Omran, Dimitris Kalatzis, Jens Petersen, Amirhossein Habibian, Auke Wiggers
- **Abstract**: Image editing approaches have become more powerful and flexible with the advent of powerful text-conditioned generative models. However, placing objects in an environment with a precise location and orientation still remains a challenge, as this typically requires carefully crafted inpainting masks or prompts. In this work, we show that a carefully designed visual map, combined with coarse object masks, is sufficient for high quality object placement. We design a conditioning signal that resolves ambiguities, while being flexible enough to allow for changing of shapes or object orientations. By building on an inpainting model, we leave the background intact by design, in contrast to methods that model objects and background jointly. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our method in the automotive setting, where we compare different conditioning signals in novel object placement tasks. These tasks are designed to measure edit quality not only in terms of appearance, but also in terms of pose and location accuracy, including cases that require non-trivial shape changes. Lastly, we show that fine location control can be combined with appearance control to place existing objects in precise locations in a scene.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a novel method for precisely controlling the placement of 3D objects within images using scene-aware diffusion models.  It tackles the challenge of accurately specifying object location and orientation in a way that overcomes limitations of existing text-based or depth-map-based conditioning. The core idea is to use a carefully designed visual map derived from projecting a 3D bounding box onto the image plane as the conditioning signal for an inpainting diffusion model. This visual map is designed to resolve ambiguities and allow changes in object shape or orientation while preserving the background. The approach is evaluated extensively in the context of autonomous driving datasets (nuScenes), demonstrating high pose fidelity, realism, and the ability to combine location control with appearance control (using an exemplar encoder). The authors compare their method to several baselines, showing improved performance in terms of pose accuracy and visual quality metrics.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its specific visual conditioning approach using a 3D bounding box projection. While using diffusion models for image editing and object insertion isn't new, the authors' method of encoding object pose and location information directly into a visual map for conditioning, specifically designed to address ambiguities, is a valuable contribution. Projecting the 3D box ensures pose is explicitly and geometrically encoded, alleviating issues with text-based prompts or simpler 2D representations like depth maps. The use of an instance segmentation method to produce occlusion-aware bounding box shaped masks is also a nice technique to preserve image detail.

*   **Significance:** The paper is significant for several reasons:

    *   **Precise Control:** The method achieves a higher degree of control over object placement and orientation compared to existing techniques, particularly text-based approaches. This is crucial for applications like autonomous driving simulation and synthetic data generation.
    *   **High-Quality Results:** The approach produces realistic and high-resolution images, a notable improvement over methods that generate full frames at lower resolutions.
    *   **Background Preservation:** The method is an inpainting approach and therefore maintains the background, unlike methods generating full images which alter or regenerate it.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The rigorous evaluation with quantitative metrics (mATE, mAOE, FID, "flips") and comparisons to strong baselines validates the effectiveness of the proposed method. The ablation studies further clarify the importance of different components.
    *   **Exemplar encoder.** The proposed framework can incorporate exemplar encoding and therefore preserve object identity.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Clear problem definition and well-motivated approach.
    *   Carefully designed visual conditioning signal.
    *   Extensive experiments with quantitative and qualitative results.
    *   Detailed ablation studies and comparisons to baselines.
    *   Adaptability: The framework has the capability of incorporating an exemplar encoder.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on 3D bounding box annotations:** The method relies on accurate 3D bounding box annotations, which may not be available in all datasets. However, the authors acknowledge this and suggest using pretrained detectors to obtain pseudo-groundtruth.
    *   **Computational cost:** The single-object-at-a-time editing approach is less computationally efficient compared to methods that generate full scenes in one pass, which may have implications for large-scale applications. Despite this, the authors argue its scalability lies in the flexible allocation of computational budget and the potential for easy parallelization.
    *   **Limited scope:** While the paper demonstrates object placement in the context of autonomous driving, the generalizability of the approach to other domains or more complex scene layouts could be explored further.
    *   **Complex system.** Requires training UNet decoder parameters, ControlNet, vision backbone, transformer blocks, pose encoding, occlusion awareness, etc. The combination of all of these techniques lead to state-of-the-art results.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in the areas of:

    *   Controllable image editing and object insertion.
    *   Synthetic data generation for autonomous driving and robotics.
    *   Diffusion models for scene understanding and manipulation.
    *   Methods that combine visual and text-based conditioning signals.

**Score: 8**

**Justification:**

The paper offers a solid contribution with a novel and well-engineered approach to 3D object placement. The thorough evaluation demonstrates the effectiveness of the method and its advantages over existing techniques.  The ability to preserve the background, control object pose with high fidelity, and incorporate the shape and identity of the object make it a valuable contribution to the field.

The score reflects both the strengths and limitations. While the reliance on 3D bounding boxes and the computational cost represent potential drawbacks, the overall novelty, significance, and potential influence of the paper justify a score of 8.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 8/10

### ThinkSound: Chain-of-Thought Reasoning in Multimodal Large Language Models for Audio Generation and Editing
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21448v1)
- **Authors**: Huadai Liu, Jialei Wang, Kaicheng Luo, Wen Wang, Qian Chen, Zhou Zhao, Wei Xue
- **Abstract**: While end-to-end video-to-audio generation has greatly improved, producing high-fidelity audio that authentically captures the nuances of visual content remains challenging. Like professionals in the creative industries, such generation requires sophisticated reasoning about items such as visual dynamics, acoustic environments, and temporal relationships. We present \textbf{ThinkSound}, a novel framework that leverages Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning to enable stepwise, interactive audio generation and editing for videos. Our approach decomposes the process into three complementary stages: foundational foley generation that creates semantically coherent soundscapes, interactive object-centric refinement through precise user interactions, and targeted editing guided by natural language instructions. At each stage, a multimodal large language model generates contextually aligned CoT reasoning that guides a unified audio foundation model. Furthermore, we introduce \textbf{AudioCoT}, a comprehensive dataset with structured reasoning annotations that establishes connections between visual content, textual descriptions, and sound synthesis. Experiments demonstrate that ThinkSound achieves state-of-the-art performance in video-to-audio generation across both audio metrics and CoT metrics and excels in out-of-distribution Movie Gen Audio benchmark. The demo page is available at https://ThinkSound-Demo.github.io.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the ThinkSound paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ThinkSound, a novel framework for video-to-audio (V2A) generation and editing that leverages Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning within multimodal large language models (MLLMs).  It decomposes the audio generation process into three stages: foundational foley generation, interactive object-centric refinement via user clicks, and targeted audio editing guided by natural language instructions. At each stage, an MLLM generates CoT to guide a unified audio foundation model. The authors also present AudioCoT, a large-scale dataset with structured CoT annotations linking visual content, text, and sound synthesis. Experiments demonstrate state-of-the-art performance in V2A and out-of-distribution generalization.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper presents several elements of novelty:

*   **CoT Reasoning in V2A:** While previous works have explored MLLMs for V2A, the explicit use of CoT to guide each stage of the audio generation process is a key contribution. This allows for more controlled and context-aware sound design.
*   **Interactive and Stepwise Approach:** The interactive editing stages (object-centric refinement and instruction-based editing) allow users to progressively shape the audio, contrasting with end-to-end methods.
*   **Unified Audio Foundation Model:** The use of a single model based on flow matching for all three stages, conditioned on multimodal inputs and CoT instructions, represents a technical contribution.
*   **AudioCoT Dataset:** The creation of a large-scale dataset with structured reasoning annotations is valuable for training and benchmarking V2A models that leverage CoT.

**Significance:**

The significance of this work lies in its potential to:

*   **Improve V2A Realism and Control:** By incorporating explicit reasoning steps, ThinkSound addresses the challenge of generating high-fidelity audio that authentically captures the nuances of visual content.
*   **Democratize Sound Design:** The interactive editing stages, guided by natural language, could make sophisticated audio manipulation accessible to non-experts.
*   **Advance MLLMs for Audio:** The paper demonstrates the effectiveness of MLLMs and CoT in the audio domain, paving the way for future research in intelligent audio generation and editing.

**Strengths:**

*   **Strong Technical Approach:** The three-stage framework, CoT integration, and unified audio foundation model are well-motivated and technically sound.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes thorough objective and subjective evaluations on multiple datasets, demonstrating state-of-the-art performance. Ablation studies effectively highlight the importance of CoT reasoning.
*   **High-Quality Dataset:** The AudioCoT dataset is a valuable resource for the community, enabling further research in CoT-driven audio generation.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the technical details and experimental results.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost:** The use of large MLLMs and flow-matching models likely entails significant computational costs for training and inference. The paper could provide more details on the hardware requirements and training time.
*   **Limited Novelty in Foundation Model:** While the unified foundation model is a valuable component, the core architecture builds upon existing flow-matching techniques. The primary innovation is in the CoT-guided conditioning.
*   **Dataset biases/limitations:** The performance on MovieGen indicates a degree of out-of-domain robustness, but the diversity of AudioCoT itself may have limitations. More analysis on potential biases within the dataset would strengthen the work.
*   **Qualitative Evaluation Limited:** While the paper does include qualitative examples via the figures, a more extensive qualitative analysis, perhaps showcasing various generated audio clips on a demo page (which is present), and detailing the nuances of the CoT’s influence, would further highlight the method's advantages.

**Potential Influence:**

ThinkSound has the potential to influence future research in:

*   **CoT-driven audio generation:** The paper provides a strong case for using CoT to guide audio generation and editing tasks.
*   **Interactive V2A systems:** The interactive editing stages could inspire new approaches to user-centered audio design.
*   **Multimodal foundation models for audio:** The unified audio foundation model could serve as a building block for future multimodal audio models.

**Score:** 8

**Justification:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field of video-to-audio generation. The novel application of CoT reasoning within a three-stage framework, the creation of the AudioCoT dataset, and the demonstrated state-of-the-art results justify a high score. While the core architecture of the foundation model relies on existing techniques and computational costs may be high, the overall approach is innovative and has the potential to significantly advance the field. It also presents a clear pathway for future research, particularly in dataset creation and exploring diverse multimodal modeling approaches. A higher score (9+) would likely require a more disruptive architectural contribution within the foundation model itself, or demonstrable generalizability beyond video-to-audio.

- **Classification**: eess.AS
- **Score**: 8/10

### Rethinking Oversaturation in Classifier-Free Guidance via Low Frequency
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21452v1)
- **Authors**: Kaiyu Song, Hanjiang Lai
- **Abstract**: Classifier-free guidance (CFG) succeeds in condition diffusion models that use a guidance scale to balance the influence of conditional and unconditional terms. A high guidance scale is used to enhance the performance of the conditional term. However, the high guidance scale often results in oversaturation and unrealistic artifacts. In this paper, we introduce a new perspective based on low-frequency signals, identifying the accumulation of redundant information in these signals as the key factor behind oversaturation and unrealistic artifacts. Building on this insight, we propose low-frequency improved classifier-free guidance (LF-CFG) to mitigate these issues. Specifically, we introduce an adaptive threshold-based measurement to pinpoint the locations of redundant information. We determine a reasonable threshold by analyzing the change rate of low-frequency information between prior and current steps. We then apply a down-weight strategy to reduce the impact of redundant information in the low-frequency signals. Experimental results demonstrate that LF-CFG effectively alleviates oversaturation and unrealistic artifacts across various diffusion models, including Stable Diffusion-XL, Stable Diffusion 2.1, 3.0, 3.5, and SiT-XL.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, focusing on novelty, significance, and a justified score.

**Summary:**

The paper "Rethinking Oversaturation in Classifier-Free Guidance via Low Frequency" addresses the problem of oversaturation and unrealistic artifacts that often occur when using high guidance scales in Classifier-Free Guidance (CFG) for conditional diffusion models.  The authors propose a new perspective based on low-frequency signals, arguing that the accumulation of redundant information in these signals is a key factor contributing to oversaturation. To mitigate this, they introduce Low-Frequency Improved Classifier-Free Guidance (LF-CFG). LF-CFG uses an adaptive threshold to pinpoint locations with redundant low-frequency information by analyzing the change rate of these signals between diffusion steps. A down-weighting strategy is then applied to reduce the impact of this redundant information.  The paper demonstrates through experiments with several popular diffusion models (Stable Diffusion variants, SiT-XL) that LF-CFG effectively reduces oversaturation and improves the quality of generated images compared to standard CFG and other improvement techniques like APG.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper introduces a novel perspective on the oversaturation problem by focusing on the role of low-frequency signals. While previous works focused on adjusting guidance scales or decoupling gradient directions, this paper delves into the frequency domain. The idea of identifying redundant information accumulating in stable low-frequency regions is potentially valuable. The proposed LF-CFG method with its adaptive thresholding and down-weighting strategy also represents a novel technical contribution, though the mechanisms themselves are relatively simple. Prior works like PAG might have addressed frequency, but this work frames oversaturation around *redundancy* of low-frequency information which is a slight difference.

* **Significance:** The oversaturation problem in CFG is a well-known issue that limits the use of high guidance scales, which are desirable for better conditional alignment. A method that effectively reduces oversaturation allows for higher quality generated images. The experimental results demonstrate a consistent improvement in image quality (lower FID/KID, lower saturation) across multiple diffusion models. The fact that LF-CFG shows competitive or improved performance compared to APG, a recent and effective oversaturation mitigation technique, highlights the significance. The analysis of how low frequency signals interact with oversaturation can inform further research into improving diffusion-based generation.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear Problem Statement and Motivation:** The paper clearly identifies the oversaturation problem and provides a convincing argument for why low-frequency signals are a key factor.
    * **Novel Insight:** The focus on redundant information accumulation in stable low-frequency regions offers a new way to understand and address the problem.
    * **Effective Method:** The LF-CFG method is relatively simple to implement and demonstrates consistent improvements in image quality.
    * **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper evaluates LF-CFG on multiple diffusion models and compares it to relevant baselines.
    * **Ablation Studies:**  The ablation studies provide insights into the different components of LF-CFG and their impact on performance.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Simplicity of Method:** While the insight is novel, the technical implementation (adaptive thresholding and down-weighting) is relatively straightforward. It's not a particularly sophisticated or complex technique, potentially limiting its long-term impact.
    * **Limited Theoretical Justification:** While the paper provides an intuitive explanation, it lacks a deeper theoretical analysis of why redundant low-frequency information leads to oversaturation. A more rigorous mathematical framework would strengthen the argument.
    * **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** While they claim LF-CFG is not sensitive to hyperparameters, they do have a threshold which is statistically derived. It's unclear how well this would generalize to other datasets and prompts.
    * **Limited Comparison:** While comparisons to CFG and APG are included, comparisons to other methods that touch upon frequency aspects of diffusion models could enhance the evaluation.

* **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research by encouraging others to explore the frequency domain for improving diffusion-based generation. The finding that redundant information in stable low-frequency regions contributes to oversaturation could inspire new methods for mitigating this problem.  The relative simplicity of LF-CFG might make it attractive for adoption and further development.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7**.

Rationale: The paper offers a novel and valuable perspective on the oversaturation problem in CFG. The proposed LF-CFG method is effective and relatively simple, and the comprehensive experimental evaluation demonstrates its potential. However, the relatively low complexity of the implementation, the limited theoretical justification, and the possible sensitivity to hyperparameters reduce the overall impact. The contribution is significant enough to warrant interest in the diffusion model generation community, however it would need more theoretical contributions to earn a higher score.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### SmoothSinger: A Conditional Diffusion Model for Singing Voice Synthesis with Multi-Resolution Architecture
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21478v1)
- **Authors**: Kehan Sui, Jinxu Xiang, Fang Jin
- **Abstract**: Singing voice synthesis (SVS) aims to generate expressive and high-quality vocals from musical scores, requiring precise modeling of pitch, duration, and articulation. While diffusion-based models have achieved remarkable success in image and video generation, their application to SVS remains challenging due to the complex acoustic and musical characteristics of singing, often resulting in artifacts that degrade naturalness. In this work, we propose SmoothSinger, a conditional diffusion model designed to synthesize high quality and natural singing voices. Unlike prior methods that depend on vocoders as a final stage and often introduce distortion, SmoothSinger refines low-quality synthesized audio directly in a unified framework, mitigating the degradation associated with two-stage pipelines. The model adopts a reference-guided dual-branch architecture, using low-quality audio from any baseline system as a reference to guide the denoising process, enabling more expressive and context-aware synthesis. Furthermore, it enhances the conventional U-Net with a parallel low-frequency upsampling path, allowing the model to better capture pitch contours and long term spectral dependencies. To improve alignment during training, we replace reference audio with degraded ground truth audio, addressing temporal mismatch between reference and target signals. Experiments on the Opencpop dataset, a large-scale Chinese singing corpus, demonstrate that SmoothSinger achieves state-of-the-art results in both objective and subjective evaluations. Extensive ablation studies confirm its effectiveness in reducing artifacts and improving the naturalness of synthesized voices.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper, "SmoothSinger: A Conditional Diffusion Model for Singing Voice Synthesis with Multi-Resolution Architecture."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SmoothSinger, a conditional diffusion model for high-fidelity singing voice synthesis (SVS). It aims to overcome the limitations of traditional two-stage acoustic model-vocoder pipelines, which often introduce artifacts and reduce naturalness.  SmoothSinger uses a reference-guided dual-branch architecture, conditioning the diffusion process on low-quality audio from existing systems (e.g., FastSpeech2). It enhances the U-Net architecture with a parallel low-frequency upsampling path (Multi-Resolution module) for better pitch contour capture and spectral modeling. To improve training stability, the paper proposes replacing reference audio with degraded ground-truth audio during training to address temporal mismatches. Experimental results on the Opencpop dataset demonstrate state-of-the-art performance in both objective and subjective evaluations, with ablation studies confirming the effectiveness of each component. The model is also adapted and evaluated on the LJSpeech dataset for text-to-speech synthesis.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**
    *   **Reference-Guided Diffusion:** The use of a reference-based diffusion model is not entirely new, as prior work like RDSinger has explored this avenue. However, SmoothSinger's specific implementation, particularly how the reference audio is integrated through a dual-branch architecture *and* affects both the downsampling and upsampling paths, constitutes a novel architectural contribution. The integration is more comprehensive than simply injecting features at the bottleneck.

    *   **Multi-Resolution Module:** The parallel low-frequency upsampling path (MR module) is a key contribution. While multi-resolution approaches are common in generative modeling (especially for images), its application and specific design within the U-Net architecture for SVS seems original. The non-sequential connection directly influencing the final output is a distinct characteristic. It addresses a specific challenge in SVS, enhancing the capturing of pitch contours and spectral details.

    *   **Degraded Audio for Training:** The strategy of replacing reference audio with degraded ground truth audio during training to mitigate temporal misalignment is a clever and practical solution. This is a significant contribution that addresses a specific issue in reference-based SVS.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Addressing Artifacts:** The paper's primary goal is to reduce artifacts common in SVS, and the results demonstrate success in this area, as evidenced by improved subjective scores. Removing the need for a separate vocoder, which is typically a major artifact source, is a valuable contribution.

    *   **State-of-the-Art Results:** The paper reports state-of-the-art results on Opencpop, a widely used SVS dataset, which is significant. This confirms the practical effectiveness of the proposed techniques.

    *   **TTS Adaptation:**  The successful adaptation to the TTS task (LJSpeech) broadens the impact of the work.  It suggests that the core ideas of SmoothSinger may be valuable beyond just singing voice synthesis and applicable to other audio generation tasks with appropriate adjustments.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear and well-structured paper.
    *   Thorough experimental validation on a standard dataset.
    *   Comprehensive ablation studies demonstrating the importance of each component.
    *   Addresses a crucial problem (artifact reduction) in SVS.
    *   Provides code or makes models publicly available (this can be a major boon).

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Dependence on FastSpeech2: While using FastSpeech2 as a baseline model is practical, it also makes SmoothSinger dependent on the performance of FastSpeech2. It would be beneficial to examine how SmoothSinger behaves when guided by references from other SVS systems which could offer a spectrum of quality. While this is tested to some degree by including different reference audio signals in the ablation study, the choice of a reference signal needs to be a part of the discussion surrounding the choice of FastSpeech2.

    *   Limited Singer Variety: Experiments are conducted with single singer data. This limits the generalizability of the system. Experiments with multiple singer dataset, even with less control data, would improve the value of the contribution.

**Justification for Score:**

SmoothSinger makes a valuable contribution to the field of singing voice synthesis by addressing the problem of artifacts in a novel way, through a well-designed diffusion model architecture that leverages reference audio and multi-resolution processing. The temporal alignment with degraded audio training technique makes the approach distinct from existing methods. The solid experimental results and thorough ablation studies support the claims made in the paper. While the dependency on FastSpeech2 (or similar front-end models) and limited singer diversity are limitations, the technical contributions and demonstrated performance justify a high score.

Score: 8

- **Classification**: cs.SD
- **Score**: 8/10

### Bridging Offline and Online Reinforcement Learning for LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21495v1)
- **Authors**: Jack Lanchantin, Angelica Chen, Janice Lan, Xian Li, Swarnadeep Saha, Tianlu Wang, Jing Xu, Ping Yu, Weizhe Yuan, Jason E Weston, Sainbayar Sukhbaatar, Ilia Kulikov
- **Abstract**: We investigate the effectiveness of reinforcement learning methods for finetuning large language models when transitioning from offline to semi-online to fully online regimes for both verifiable and non-verifiable tasks. Our experiments cover training on verifiable math as well as non-verifiable instruction following with a set of benchmark evaluations for both. Across these settings, we extensively compare online and semi-online Direct Preference Optimization and Group Reward Policy Optimization objectives, and surprisingly find similar performance and convergence between these variants, which all strongly outperform offline methods. We provide a detailed analysis of the training dynamics and hyperparameter selection strategies to achieve optimal results. Finally, we show that multi-tasking with verifiable and non-verifiable rewards jointly yields improved performance across both task types.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the effectiveness of different reinforcement learning methods for fine-tuning large language models (LLMs) across various training regimes: offline, semi-online, and online.  The study focuses on both verifiable (math problems) and non-verifiable (instruction following) tasks.  The core findings reveal that semi-online and online training methods, particularly Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), outperform offline methods significantly.  Surprisingly, semi-online DPO achieves comparable performance to fully online DPO and Group Reward Policy Optimization (GRPO), suggesting that full online training may not always be necessary. The paper also explores joint optimization of verifiable and non-verifiable rewards, demonstrating improved performance across both task types. The authors provide detailed analysis of training dynamics, hyperparameter sensitivity, and mitigation strategies for instability in DPO.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's strength lies in its systematic comparison of different training regimes (offline, semi-online, online) for LLM fine-tuning across both verifiable and non-verifiable tasks. While individual components like DPO and GRPO are not novel, the *comparative* study and the introduction of the "semi-online" paradigm with varying synchronization speeds are valuable.  Exploring the transition between offline and online RL is a practical contribution. The observation that semi-online training performs comparably to fully online is a key finding.  The experiments combining verifiable and non-verifiable reward signals are a worthwhile exploration.

*   **Significance:** The finding that semi-online DPO can match the performance of fully online DPO/GRPO has significant practical implications. It allows for a trade-off between computational cost (less frequent model synchronization) and performance, making LLM fine-tuning more accessible and efficient.  The analysis of DPO's instability and hyperparameter sensitivity is useful for practitioners. The results relating to joint reward optimization is valuable to the community as well. The paper builds upon existing work in RLHF, DPO, and GRPO, but it advances the field by providing a clearer understanding of the trade-offs involved in different training paradigms and revealing opportunities for efficiency gains.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Systematic and well-controlled experiments.
    *   Comprehensive comparison of different training regimes and algorithms.
    *   Practical insights into hyperparameter tuning and training stability.
    *   Demonstration of joint optimization of verifiable and non-verifiable tasks.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experiments primarily rely on a single base LLM model (Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct). While understandable given computational constraints, it limits the generalizability of the findings to other model architectures.
    *   While some hyperparameter exploration is done, a more extensive hyperparameter search could potentially improve the results further.
    *   The analysis of "reward hacking" is limited. A deeper dive into the specific types of reward hacking observed and more robust mitigation strategies would strengthen the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence the field by shifting the focus from purely online RL for LLM fine-tuning towards more efficient semi-online approaches.  The insights into DPO instability will be valuable to practitioners. The work on joint reward optimization will spur further research into multi-task learning for LLMs.

*   **Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**  While the individual algorithms aren't new, the comparative analysis and introduction of the semi-online paradigm significantly advance the field. The paper offers valuable practical insights and challenges the assumption that fully online RL is always necessary. It opens avenues for more efficient and scalable LLM fine-tuning. The limitations related to model generalizability and deeper reward hacking analysis hold it back from scoring higher.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Mind2Web 2: Evaluating Agentic Search with Agent-as-a-Judge
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21506v1)
- **Authors**: Boyu Gou, Zanming Huang, Yuting Ning, Yu Gu, Michael Lin, Weijian Qi, Andrei Kopanev, Botao Yu, Bernal Jiménez Gutiérrez, Yiheng Shu, Chan Hee Song, Jiaman Wu, Shijie Chen, Hanane Nour Moussa, Tianshu Zhang, Jian Xie, Yifei Li, Tianci Xue, Zeyi Liao, Kai Zhang, Boyuan Zheng, Zhaowei Cai, Viktor Rozgic, Morteza Ziyadi, Huan Sun, Yu Su
- **Abstract**: Agentic search such as Deep Research systems, where large language models autonomously browse the web, synthesize information, and return comprehensive citation-backed answers, represents a major shift in how users interact with web-scale information. While promising greater efficiency and cognitive offloading, the growing complexity and open-endedness of agentic search have outpaced existing evaluation benchmarks and methodologies, which largely assume short search horizons and static answers. In this paper, we introduce Mind2Web 2, a benchmark of 130 realistic, high-quality, and long-horizon tasks that require real-time web browsing and extensive information synthesis, constructed with over 1,000 hours of human labor. To address the challenge of evaluating time-varying and complex answers, we propose a novel Agent-as-a-Judge framework. Our method constructs task-specific judge agents based on a tree-structured rubric design to automatically assess both answer correctness and source attribution. We conduct a comprehensive evaluation of nine frontier agentic search systems and human performance, along with a detailed error analysis to draw insights for future development. The best-performing system, OpenAI Deep Research, can already achieve 50-70% of human performance while spending half the time, showing a great potential. Altogether, Mind2Web 2 provides a rigorous foundation for developing and benchmarking the next generation of agentic search systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Mind2Web 2: Evaluating Agentic Search with Agent-as-a-Judge," along with a justified novelty/significance score.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Mind2Web 2, a new benchmark designed to evaluate agentic search systems. It addresses the limitations of existing benchmarks, which often focus on short search horizons and static answers, by presenting 130 realistic, high-quality, and long-horizon tasks. These tasks require real-time web browsing and extensive information synthesis.  A key contribution is the "Agent-as-a-Judge" framework, which uses task-specific judge agents based on tree-structured rubrics to automatically assess answer correctness and source attribution. The paper presents a comprehensive evaluation of nine agentic search systems and compares their performance to human performance, including a detailed error analysis to guide future development. Results indicate that while current systems lag behind humans, the best system (OpenAI Deep Research) can achieve 50-70% of human performance with less time, highlighting the potential of agentic search.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*Strengths:*

*   **Addresses a Critical Gap:** The paper directly tackles a pressing need in the field of agentic search: the lack of suitable benchmarks for evaluating increasingly complex and open-ended tasks. It recognizes that the shift towards autonomous web agents necessitates new evaluation methodologies.
*   **Realistic and Complex Tasks:** Mind2Web 2 moves beyond simplistic, predefined tasks, offering scenarios closer to real-world user needs. This is a significant improvement over benchmarks with static answers, better reflecting the capabilities and challenges faced by modern agentic systems.
*   **Novel Evaluation Framework:** The Agent-as-a-Judge approach is innovative and attempts to address the complexities of time-varying and nuanced answers.  The automated assessment of correctness and source attribution is crucial for promoting trustworthy and reliable agentic search.
*   **Rigorous Evaluation:** The paper provides a comprehensive evaluation of several prominent agentic search systems, including both commercial and research-based approaches.  The comparative analysis with human performance is valuable for understanding the current state-of-the-art.
*   **Detailed Error Analysis:** The in-depth error analysis offers valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of current systems. It identifies key areas for improvement, such as the ability to handle time-varying information and integrate web browsing effectively.
*   **Well-Documented and Replicable:**  The paper provides a clear description of the benchmark construction process, the Agent-as-a-Judge framework, and the experimental setup, making it more likely to be adopted and replicated by other researchers.

*Weaknesses:*

*   **Scalability and Maintenance:** The Agent-as-a-Judge framework, while innovative, may be complex to scale to a much larger task set. The creation and maintenance of detailed rubrics and judge agents can be a time-consuming process.
*   **Dependence on LLMs:** The Agent-as-a-Judge approach relies heavily on LLMs for information extraction and verification. While the paper acknowledges this, the potential biases and limitations of LLMs could impact the evaluation's objectivity.
*   **Focus on Factual Accuracy:**  The benchmark primarily assesses factual accuracy and source attribution.  It may not fully capture other important aspects of agentic search, such as creativity, common-sense reasoning, or the ability to handle subjective or nuanced information needs.
*   **Limited Generalizability:** The tasks, while realistic, are still confined to specific domains and types of information needs. It's not entirely clear how well the findings generalize to other types of agentic search tasks or user scenarios.

*Potential Influence on the Field:*

Mind2Web 2 has the potential to significantly influence the field of agentic search by:

*   **Driving Innovation:** Providing a more challenging and realistic benchmark will encourage researchers to develop more sophisticated and capable agentic systems.
*   **Promoting Trustworthiness:** The focus on source attribution will incentivize the development of more reliable and transparent agentic search.
*   **Guiding Future Research:** The detailed error analysis will help focus research efforts on the most pressing challenges in agentic search.
*   **Standardizing Evaluation:** If widely adopted, Mind2Web 2 could become a standard benchmark for evaluating agentic search systems, enabling better comparisons and progress tracking.

**Score and Justification:**

I assign the paper a score of **8** out of 10.

Justification:

The paper makes a significant and timely contribution to the field of agentic search by introducing a more realistic and comprehensive benchmark. The Agent-as-a-Judge framework is innovative and addresses key challenges in evaluating these complex systems. The rigorous evaluation and detailed error analysis provide valuable insights for future research. However, the potential scalability and dependence on LLMs, and limited generalizability, justify not giving a higher score. While the contributions are significant, further work on making the evaluation more scalable and more objective will be critical.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 0/10

### Potemkin Understanding in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21521v1)
- **Authors**: Marina Mancoridis, Bec Weeks, Keyon Vafa, Sendhil Mullainathan
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are regularly evaluated using benchmark datasets. But what justifies making inferences about an LLM's capabilities based on its answers to a curated set of questions? This paper first introduces a formal framework to address this question. The key is to note that the benchmarks used to test LLMs -- such as AP exams -- are also those used to test people. However, this raises an implication: these benchmarks are only valid tests if LLMs misunderstand concepts in ways that mirror human misunderstandings. Otherwise, success on benchmarks only demonstrates potemkin understanding: the illusion of understanding driven by answers irreconcilable with how any human would interpret a concept. We present two procedures for quantifying the existence of potemkins: one using a specially designed benchmark in three domains, the other using a general procedure that provides a lower-bound on their prevalence. We find that potemkins are ubiquitous across models, tasks, and domains. We also find that these failures reflect not just incorrect understanding, but deeper internal incoherence in concept representations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Potemkin Understanding in Large Language Models":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces the concept of "potemkin understanding" in large language models (LLMs), drawing an analogy to Potemkin villages where facades create an illusion of substance. The authors argue that LLMs can perform well on benchmarks (keystone questions) without genuine conceptual understanding, because their misunderstandings can deviate significantly from human misunderstandings. This invalidates the assumption that benchmark performance equates to true understanding if LLMs' reasoning processes are fundamentally different from humans'.

To quantify the prevalence of potemkin understanding, the authors develop two procedures:
1.  A benchmark dataset with concepts from literary techniques, game theory, and psychological biases, assessing the gap between the ability to define a concept and apply it in classification, generation, and editing tasks.
2.  An automated evaluation procedure based on LLM self-grading, where inconsistencies in an LLM's judgment of its own generated content reveal a lower bound on the rate of potemkin reasoning.

The results show that potemkin understanding is ubiquitous across models, tasks, and domains, often arising from incoherence in concept representation. This casts doubt on the validity of using human-designed benchmarks to assess LLM understanding.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's concept of "potemkin understanding" is novel and insightful. While previous work has questioned the relationship between benchmark performance and true understanding in LLMs, this paper provides a concrete framework for understanding *how* this disconnect manifests. The analogy to Potemkin villages is apt and helps to frame the problem in an accessible way. The two procedures developed to quantify the phenomenon are a significant contribution.
*   **Significance:** The paper has significant implications for how LLMs are evaluated and interpreted. It challenges the widespread practice of equating benchmark success with human-like comprehension. By showing that LLMs can succeed for reasons unrelated to genuine understanding, the paper underscores the need for more sophisticated and nuanced evaluation metrics. It highlights the need to delve deeper into the inner workings of LLMs to understand *how* they arrive at their answers, rather than just assessing *whether* their answers are correct. The paper encourages a more critical and cautious approach to interpreting LLM performance on tasks that require conceptual understanding.  The paper suggests that benchmarks will need to be revised to account for non-human-like failings in LLM comprehension.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The conceptual framework is well-defined and provides a clear basis for analysis.
    *   The two quantitative procedures are complementary and provide converging evidence for the prevalence of potemkin understanding.
    *   The benchmark dataset spans a diverse range of concepts, increasing the generalizability of the findings.
    *   The automated evaluation procedure is scalable and provides a lower bound on the rate of potemkin understanding, even without human annotation.
    *   The analysis is thorough, examining a variety of LLMs.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the benchmark dataset is diverse, it may not cover all possible types of conceptual understanding.
    *   The automated evaluation procedure only provides a lower bound on the rate of potemkin understanding. Further work could improve this measure.
    *   The paper focuses primarily on identifying and quantifying potemkin understanding, rather than exploring its causes or potential mitigation strategies.
    *   The reliance on self-consistency as a measure is potentially limited, especially if LLMs are systematically biased.
    *   The paper claims that "humans, by construction, cannot exhibit" Potemkin failures, which is not completely accurate. There are circumstances where humans can provide seemingly correct responses without true comprehension, exhibiting a form of "rote learning".

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to significantly influence the field of LLM research by:
    *   Shifting the focus from simply achieving high benchmark scores to understanding *how* LLMs achieve those scores.
    *   Motivating the development of new evaluation metrics that are more sensitive to the nuances of conceptual understanding.
    *   Encouraging researchers to explore the internal representations of LLMs in order to identify and mitigate potemkin understanding.
    *   Promoting the use of adversarial techniques to probe the limits of LLM comprehension.

*   **Rigorous Rationale:** The identification of Potemkin understanding is useful, and the framework itself provides a way of better interpreting LLM behavior and pointing out flaws in LLM benchmarks designed for humans. While the limitations are noteworthy (e.g., not being a definitive measure of potemkin rates, but instead focusing on providing a lower bound), the identification is impactful and has the potential to shift the paradigm of how we assess LLMs to begin to take these issues of non-human like reasoning errors into account.

### "What's Up, Doc?": Analyzing How Users Seek Health Information in Large-Scale Conversational AI Datasets
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21532v1)
- **Authors**: Akshay Paruchuri, Maryam Aziz, Rohit Vartak, Ayman Ali, Best Uchehara, Xin Liu, Ishan Chatterjee, Monica Agrawal
- **Abstract**: People are increasingly seeking healthcare information from large language models (LLMs) via interactive chatbots, yet the nature and inherent risks of these conversations remain largely unexplored. In this paper, we filter large-scale conversational AI datasets to achieve HealthChat-11K, a curated dataset of 11K real-world conversations composed of 25K user messages. We use HealthChat-11K and a clinician-driven taxonomy for how users interact with LLMs when seeking healthcare information in order to systematically study user interactions across 21 distinct health specialties. Our analysis reveals insights into the nature of how and why users seek health information, such as common interactions, instances of incomplete context, affective behaviors, and interactions (e.g., leading questions) that can induce sycophancy, underscoring the need for improvements in the healthcare support capabilities of LLMs deployed as conversational AI. Code and artifacts to retrieve our analyses and combine them into a curated dataset can be found here: https://github.com/yahskapar/HealthChat
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HealthChat-11K, a novel dataset of 11,000 real-world user conversations (25K user messages) with LLM-based chatbots seeking healthcare information. The authors filter and curate this dataset from larger conversational AI datasets. They then develop a clinician-driven taxonomy with 21 distinct health specialties and four message-level categories (Providing Context, Requesting Information, Interaction Management, Out-of-Scope) to systematically analyze user interactions. The analysis reveals insights into user behavior when seeking health information, identifying common interaction patterns, instances of incomplete context, affective behaviors (positive/negative emotions), and interactions that can induce sycophancy in LLMs. The authors release the dataset and code to facilitate further research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the creation of the HealthChat-11K dataset. While individual components (e.g., taxonomies for analyzing online health interactions, identifying biases in LLMs) exist, the combination of a large, real-world conversational dataset with a clinician-driven taxonomy for healthcare-specific user behavior analysis is a significant contribution. Prior datasets are often single-turn, synthetic, or focused on clinician-oriented tasks, lacking the multi-turn, patient-centered, and realistic context provided by HealthChat-11K.  The focus on identifying user behaviors that can negatively impact LLM performance is also a valuable contribution.

**Significance:** The paper highlights the growing trend of users turning to LLMs for healthcare information and underscores the need to understand the risks associated with these interactions. By analyzing real-world conversations, the authors reveal potential failure modes of LLMs in healthcare contexts that are not captured by existing benchmarks. The analysis of incomplete context, affective behaviors, and leading questions sheds light on how user behavior can influence LLM responses and potentially compromise the quality and reliability of healthcare information. This has important implications for the design and evaluation of healthcare chatbots, emphasizing the need for improvements in their ability to handle ambiguous queries, solicit complete information, manage emotional responses, and avoid sycophancy. The released dataset will enable further research into these issues and the development of more robust and reliable healthcare chatbots.

**Strengths:**

*   **Valuable Dataset:** The HealthChat-11K dataset is a valuable resource for researchers in the field of healthcare AI and conversational AI.
*   **Clinician-Driven Taxonomy:** The development of a clinician-driven taxonomy ensures that the analysis is grounded in clinical expertise and addresses relevant healthcare concerns.
*   **Systematic Analysis:** The systematic analysis of user interactions using the taxonomy provides valuable insights into user behavior and potential failure modes of LLMs.
*   **Practical Implications:** The paper highlights practical implications for the design and evaluation of healthcare chatbots.
*   **Reproducibility:** The release of the dataset and code promotes reproducibility and encourages further research.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Potential Biases:** The dataset is curated from existing large-scale datasets, which may be subject to inherent biases. The authors acknowledge that the data is restricted to English-language, non-toxic conversations, which may limit generalizability. The study also focuses solely on user messages which can limit the dyadic study of human chatbot dialogue.
*   **Taxonomy Limitations:** Although clinician-driven, the taxonomy might not capture every nuance of healthcare interactions and may require further refinement.
*   **Limited Scope of Case Studies:** While the case studies provide illustrative examples, they are limited in scope and may not fully capture the range of user behaviors and LLM responses.
*   **Dependency on LLM for annotation:** LLM driven annotation, while scalable, may inherit biases and inaccuracies. While the human verification is useful, it is only performed on a subset of the data.
*   **The human-LLM interaction loop is not explored:** The manuscript could be strengthened by looking into the chatbot responses and how it would change user behaviour or the topic of the conversation.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of **7.5** to this paper. The creation of the HealthChat-11K dataset and the systematic analysis of user interactions using a clinician-driven taxonomy represent a significant contribution to the field. The paper identifies important challenges in the use of LLMs for healthcare information seeking and highlights practical implications for the design and evaluation of healthcare chatbots. While the paper has some limitations, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses. The released dataset has the potential to stimulate further research and contribute to the development of more robust and reliable healthcare chatbots. The paper successfully addresses the urgent need to characterize real-world communication patterns in the field.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Exploring the Design Space of 3D MLLMs for CT Report Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2506.21535v1)
- **Authors**: Mohammed Baharoon, Jun Ma, Congyu Fang, Augustin Toma, Bo Wang
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have emerged as a promising way to automate Radiology Report Generation (RRG). In this work, we systematically investigate the design space of 3D MLLMs, including visual input representation, projectors, Large Language Models (LLMs), and fine-tuning techniques for 3D CT report generation. We also introduce two knowledge-based report augmentation methods that improve performance on the GREEN score by up to 10\%, achieving the 2nd place on the MICCAI 2024 AMOS-MM challenge. Our results on the 1,687 cases from the AMOS-MM dataset show that RRG is largely independent of the size of LLM under the same training protocol. We also show that larger volume size does not always improve performance if the original ViT was pre-trained on a smaller volume size. Lastly, we show that using a segmentation mask along with the CT volume improves performance. The code is publicly available at https://github.com/bowang-lab/AMOS-MM-Solution
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper, "Exploring the Design Space of 3D MLLMs for CT Report Generation."

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the design space of 3D Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) for automated radiology report generation (RRG) from CT scans. The authors systematically explore different architectural choices, including visual input representation (using AnyResolution for 3D volumes), projectors (MLP, Spatial Pooling Perceiver, TokenPacker), Large Language Models (LLMs, including Llama, Phi, Qwen, Gemma, Mistral), and fine-tuning techniques (freezing, LoRA, DoRA, full fine-tuning). They also introduce two knowledge-based report augmentation methods: Binary-based Questioning (BQ) and Naive Normality (NN), to improve the completeness of generated reports. The experiments are conducted on the AMOS-MM dataset, and the results indicate that RRG performance is largely independent of the LLM size under the same training protocol. The authors found that the M3D projector performs best and freezing LLMs during fine-tuning works best. In addition to improved performance from segmentation masks as input, the knowledge-based report augmentation strategies significantly boost GREEN score, achieving second place in the MICCAI 2024 AMOS-MM challenge.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Systematic Exploration:** The paper's primary strength lies in its systematic exploration of the design space. By varying different components of the MLLM architecture (visual representation, projector, LLM, fine-tuning), the authors provide valuable insights into their impact on RRG performance. This is a well-defined ablation study providing much needed evidence toward efficient architecture choices.

    *   **Knowledge-Based Augmentation:** The introduction of BQ and NN report augmentation techniques is a significant contribution. This improves the clinical usefulness of the generated reports by capturing potentially missed findings and enforcing completeness by explicitly stating normality. The observed increase in the GREEN score is promising. The use of GPT-3 mini to create the triplets is likely a strong source of hallucinations, however. This limits the clinical utility of the report augmentation method.

    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper employs a comprehensive evaluation methodology, using various metrics (GREEN, RaTEScore, BLEU, ROUGE, METEOR) to assess the quality of the generated reports.

    *   **Clear and Modular Implementation:** The authors provide a modular and open-source implementation, facilitating reproducibility and future research.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty in Base Architecture:** The core architecture relies heavily on existing frameworks like LLaVA and uses readily available LLMs. The novelty is primarily in the exploration of the design space within this existing framework and the report augmentation.

    *   **Metric limitations:** There are issues with relying only on the GREEN score and text-similarity metrics. Although text-similarity metrics such as bleu are useful, they are not the gold standard for radiology report generation. Radiology reports must be both accurate and comprehensive in their description of the scan.

    *   **Evaluation Dataset:** While using the AMOS-MM dataset is practical for comparison, it is still a limited dataset and is heavily weighted toward the abdomen and pelvis regions. Results may vary on other datasets.

    *   **Justification for Prompting LLMs for Knowledge Creation:** There is no discussion of the potential for hallucinations and inaccuracies from LLM knowledge creation. This is a crucial oversight.

    *   **Report Augmentation Details:** Details on the heuristics used for keyword search during augmentation are not given. The generalizability and utility of the keywords selected is unknown.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Practical Guidance:** The paper offers practical guidance for researchers and practitioners working on RRG. The systematic exploration of the design space provides valuable insights into the trade-offs involved in choosing different architectural components.

    *   **Improved Report Quality:** The proposed report augmentation techniques demonstrate a potential to improve the quality and completeness of automatically generated radiology reports.

    *   **Benchmarking:** The work serves as a benchmark for future research in the field of 3D MLLMs for RRG.

*   **Novelty:** The primary novelty lies in the systematic study, the report augmentation techniques, and the combination of existing tools and frameworks for 3D CT report generation. This novelty has limited clinical value, as there is a crucial step in knowledge creation using LLMs that has not been addressed properly.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field by providing valuable insights into the design and optimization of 3D MLLMs for RRG. The open-source implementation and report augmentation techniques can serve as a basis for future research and development. The results of the ablation study highlight efficient architecture choices for others to use in the field.

**Overall Score and Justification:**

Given the strengths and weaknesses, and a realistic assessment of the potential impact, I assign a score of **6.5**.

**Rationale:**

The paper provides a valuable systematic exploration of the design space for 3D MLLMs in RRG. The report augmentation techniques are a promising contribution that addresses a critical need for completeness in radiology reports. However, the limited novelty in the base architecture, the reliance on existing frameworks, reliance on hallucination prone LLM knowledge generation, and limited dataset size constrain the overall impact and score. The paper makes a solid contribution, but does not represent a groundbreaking advancement in the field.

- **Classification**: eess.IV
- **Score**: 0/10
